# Luyện Thi Quốc Tịch Úc — full question bank (v1.1.0)

1200 practice questions written from the testable content of the official booklet *Australian Citizenship: Our Common Bond* — 300 per topic. The correct answer is marked with ✓. This app is a study aid only and is not affiliated with the Australian Government.

## Topic 1: Australia and its people

**1. Who are the first inhabitants of Australia?**
- A. Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples ✓
- B. British settlers
- C. Dutch explorers
- D. Gold miners from China
- *Explanation:* Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples are the first inhabitants of Australia. Their cultures are among the oldest living cultures in the world.

**2. For about how long have Aboriginal peoples lived in Australia?**
- A. About 200 years
- B. More than 60,000 years ✓
- C. About 2,000 years
- D. Since 1788
- *Explanation:* Aboriginal peoples have lived in Australia for more than 60,000 years, long before European settlement.

**3. What name is given to the traditional Aboriginal stories about how the land and people were created?**
- A. The Federation stories
- B. The bush legends
- C. The Dreaming ✓
- D. The Anzac legend
- *Explanation:* The Dreaming is the name for the traditional stories that explain how the land, animals and people were created.

**4. Where are the Torres Strait Islands located?**
- A. Between Tasmania and Victoria
- B. Off the coast of Western Australia
- C. Near New Zealand
- D. Between the tip of Queensland and Papua New Guinea ✓
- *Explanation:* The Torres Strait Islands lie in the Torres Strait, between Cape York in Queensland and Papua New Guinea.

**5. In the Australian Aboriginal Flag, what does the yellow circle represent?**
- A. The sun ✓
- B. Gold
- C. The desert sand
- D. The golden wattle
- *Explanation:* In the Aboriginal Flag, black represents the people, red represents the earth and the yellow circle represents the sun.

**6. What symbol is shown in the centre of the Torres Strait Islander Flag?**
- A. A boomerang
- B. A traditional headdress and a white star ✓
- C. A kangaroo
- D. A map of the islands
- *Explanation:* The Torres Strait Islander Flag shows a traditional headdress, called a dhari, with a white five-pointed star beneath it.

**7. On which date is National Sorry Day held each year?**
- A. 25 April
- B. 26 January
- C. 26 May ✓
- D. 11 November
- *Explanation:* National Sorry Day is held on 26 May each year. It remembers the mistreatment of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples, especially the Stolen Generations.

**8. What did the successful 1967 referendum allow?**
- A. Aboriginal people to own land anywhere
- B. A separate Aboriginal parliament
- C. Voting in state elections for the first time
- D. The Commonwealth to make laws for Aboriginal people and to count them in the census ✓
- *Explanation:* After the 1967 referendum, the Commonwealth could make laws for Aboriginal people and they were counted in the national census.

**9. Which Aboriginal man led a famous legal fight for land rights on Murray Island?**
- A. Eddie Mabo ✓
- B. Charles Perkins
- C. David Unaipon
- D. Albert Namatjira
- *Explanation:* Eddie Mabo's campaign led to the High Court's 1992 Mabo decision, which recognised native title in Australia.

**10. What does NAIDOC Week celebrate?**
- A. The start of the football season
- B. The history, culture and achievements of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples ✓
- C. The wheat harvest
- D. The anniversary of Federation
- *Explanation:* NAIDOC Week celebrates the history, culture and achievements of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples.

**11. Before European settlement, about how many different Indigenous languages were spoken in Australia?**
- A. About 20
- B. About five
- C. More than 250 ✓
- D. Just one
- *Explanation:* More than 250 Indigenous languages were spoken in Australia before European settlement. Many are still spoken today.

**12. What historic statement did Prime Minister Kevin Rudd make in Parliament in 2008?**
- A. He declared Australia a republic
- B. He changed the national anthem
- C. He introduced a new Australian flag
- D. He made a formal apology to the Stolen Generations ✓
- *Explanation:* In 2008, Prime Minister Kevin Rudd formally apologised to the Stolen Generations — Aboriginal children who were removed from their families.

**13. What is a 'Welcome to Country'?**
- A. A ceremony by Traditional Owners welcoming visitors to their land ✓
- B. A ceremony at airports for new migrants
- C. The opening ceremony of Parliament
- D. A New Year celebration in Sydney
- *Explanation:* A Welcome to Country is performed by Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander Traditional Owners to welcome visitors to their Country.

**14. What does an 'Acknowledgement of Country' show?**
- A. That a person owns the land
- B. Respect for the Traditional Owners of the land ✓
- C. A claim for native title
- D. The exact borders of a state
- *Explanation:* An Acknowledgement of Country shows respect for the Traditional Owners of the land on which a meeting or event is held.

**15. Who commanded the First Fleet that arrived in Australia in 1788?**
- A. Captain James Cook
- B. Governor Lachlan Macquarie
- C. Captain Arthur Phillip ✓
- D. Matthew Flinders
- *Explanation:* Captain Arthur Phillip commanded the First Fleet and became the first Governor of the new colony of New South Wales.

**16. The First Fleet arrived at Sydney Cove on which date?**
- A. 1 January 1901
- B. 25 April 1915
- C. 4 July 1776
- D. 26 January 1788 ✓
- *Explanation:* The First Fleet arrived at Sydney Cove on 26 January 1788. This date is now marked as Australia Day.

**17. How many ships made up the First Fleet?**
- A. 11 ships ✓
- B. One ship
- C. Five ships
- D. 22 ships
- *Explanation:* The First Fleet was made up of 11 ships that sailed from England to Australia in 1787-88.

**18. Who were most of the people transported on the First Fleet to start the new colony?**
- A. Soldiers returning from war
- B. Convicts ✓
- C. Gold seekers
- D. Missionaries
- *Explanation:* Most of the passengers on the First Fleet were convicts sent from Britain to start a penal colony in Australia.

**19. In which year did Captain James Cook chart the east coast of Australia?**
- A. 1788
- B. 1606
- C. 1770 ✓
- D. 1801
- *Explanation:* James Cook charted the east coast of Australia in 1770 and claimed it for Britain, naming it New South Wales.

**20. What was the first British colony established in Australia called?**
- A. The Commonwealth of Australia
- B. Van Diemen's Land
- C. Australasia
- D. New South Wales ✓
- *Explanation:* New South Wales, founded in 1788, was the first British colony in Australia. Other colonies were later created from it.

**21. What event in the 1850s brought many new settlers to Australia, including many people from China?**
- A. The gold rushes ✓
- B. The First World War
- C. Federation
- D. The building of the Sydney Harbour Bridge
- *Explanation:* Gold was discovered in the 1850s. The gold rushes brought people from all over the world, including many Chinese settlers.

**22. Which of these people was a famous Australian bushranger?**
- A. Don Bradman
- B. Ned Kelly ✓
- C. Banjo Paterson
- D. Henry Lawson
- *Explanation:* Ned Kelly was a famous bushranger of the 1870s. He was captured at Glenrowan and hanged in Melbourne in 1880.

**23. Who wrote 'Waltzing Matilda', the song often called Australia's unofficial national anthem?**
- A. Henry Lawson
- B. Dorothea Mackellar
- C. Banjo Paterson ✓
- D. Ned Kelly
- *Explanation:* A.B. 'Banjo' Paterson wrote 'Waltzing Matilda' in 1895. It is one of Australia's best-loved songs.

**24. Which Australian is widely regarded as the greatest cricket batsman of all time?**
- A. Shane Warne
- B. Steve Waugh
- C. Ricky Ponting
- D. Sir Donald Bradman ✓
- *Explanation:* Sir Donald Bradman, who played for Australia in the 1930s and 1940s, is widely regarded as the greatest batsman in cricket history.

**25. What happened at Ballarat, Victoria, in 1854, when gold miners protested against unfair licence fees?**
- A. The Eureka Stockade ✓
- B. The Rum Rebellion
- C. The Gallipoli landing
- D. The Cowra breakout
- *Explanation:* At the Eureka Stockade in 1854, gold miners at Ballarat protested against expensive mining licences. It is remembered as a stand for fairness.

**26. On 1 January 1901, the separate British colonies in Australia joined together to form what?**
- A. The Republic of Australia
- B. The Commonwealth of Australia ✓
- C. The United States of Australia
- D. The Dominion of Great Britain
- *Explanation:* On 1 January 1901 the colonies united to form the Commonwealth of Australia. This event is known as Federation.

**27. What is the Australian Constitution?**
- A. A famous speech by the first Prime Minister
- B. A trade treaty with Britain
- C. The set of rules for the government of the Commonwealth of Australia ✓
- D. A list of Australia's national symbols
- *Explanation:* The Australian Constitution is the legal framework — the set of rules — for the government of the Commonwealth of Australia.

**28. Who was the first Prime Minister of Australia?**
- A. Alfred Deakin
- B. Andrew Fisher
- C. Joseph Cook
- D. Edmund Barton ✓
- *Explanation:* Edmund Barton was the first Prime Minister of the Commonwealth of Australia, from 1901 to 1903.

**29. In which city did the first Commonwealth Parliament meet in 1901?**
- A. Melbourne ✓
- B. Sydney
- C. Canberra
- D. Brisbane
- *Explanation:* The first Commonwealth Parliament met in Melbourne in 1901, and sat there until Parliament House in Canberra opened in 1927.

**30. The Australian and New Zealand soldiers of the First World War became famous by what name?**
- A. The Light Horsemen
- B. The ANZACs ✓
- C. The Rats of Tobruk
- D. The First Fleeters
- *Explanation:* ANZAC stands for Australian and New Zealand Army Corps. The soldiers who served in it became known as ANZACs.

**31. What do Australians commemorate on Anzac Day?**
- A. The arrival of the First Fleet in 1788
- B. The federation of the colonies in 1901
- C. The landing of the ANZACs at Gallipoli in 1915 and all who have served and died in wars ✓
- D. The end of the Second World War in 1945
- *Explanation:* On Anzac Day, 25 April, Australians remember the landing at Gallipoli in 1915 and honour all Australians who have served and died in wars.

**32. On which date is Anzac Day observed?**
- A. 26 January
- B. 1 January
- C. 11 November
- D. 25 April ✓
- *Explanation:* Anzac Day is observed on 25 April, the anniversary of the ANZAC landing at Gallipoli in 1915.

**33. Which flower is worn as a symbol of remembrance for Australians who died in wars?**
- A. The red poppy ✓
- B. The golden wattle
- C. The waratah
- D. The gum blossom
- *Explanation:* The red poppy is a symbol of remembrance. Poppies are worn especially around Remembrance Day, 11 November.

**34. Remembrance Day is observed on which date?**
- A. 25 April
- B. 11 November ✓
- C. 26 May
- D. 30 June
- *Explanation:* Remembrance Day is 11 November. At 11 am Australians observe one minute's silence, marking the time the First World War ended in 1918.

**35. What do Australians do at 11 am on Remembrance Day?**
- A. Sing the national anthem twice
- B. Fly flags at half mast all day
- C. Observe one minute's silence ✓
- D. Watch a march in every capital city
- *Explanation:* At 11 am on Remembrance Day, Australians pause for one minute's silence to remember those who died or suffered in wars.

**36. The phrase 'Lest we forget' is connected with what?**
- A. Australia Day celebrations
- B. The national anthem
- C. The Eureka Stockade
- D. Remembering Australians who served and died in wars ✓
- *Explanation:* 'Lest we forget' is said at remembrance services to promise that the sacrifice of those who served and died will always be remembered.

**37. Where did Australian and New Zealand troops land on 25 April 1915?**
- A. Gallipoli, Turkey ✓
- B. Kokoda, Papua
- C. Tobruk, Libya
- D. Normandy, France
- *Explanation:* On 25 April 1915, ANZAC troops landed at Gallipoli in Turkey. Thousands died during the eight-month campaign.

**38. During the Second World War, Australian soldiers fought a famous campaign along which jungle track?**
- A. The Birdsville Track
- B. The Kokoda Track ✓
- C. The Nullarbor Trail
- D. The Great Alpine Road
- *Explanation:* In 1942 Australian soldiers fought along the Kokoda Track in Papua, helping to stop the advance towards Australia.

**39. After the Second World War, Australia encouraged migration with an idea often summed up as what?**
- A. 'White Australia forever'
- B. 'No more migrants'
- C. 'Populate or perish' ✓
- D. 'Close the gate'
- *Explanation:* After the Second World War, Australia wanted a larger population for defence and development — an idea summed up as 'populate or perish'.

**40. Since 1945, migrants have come to Australia from how many countries?**
- A. Only Britain
- B. Only European countries
- C. Fewer than 20 countries
- D. More than 200 countries ✓
- *Explanation:* Since 1945, more than seven million people have migrated to Australia from more than 200 countries around the world.

**41. Where do new Australian citizens publicly make their commitment to Australia?**
- A. At a citizenship ceremony ✓
- B. In a court of law
- C. At a meeting of Parliament
- D. At an army barracks
- *Explanation:* At a citizenship ceremony, new citizens make the Australian Citizenship Pledge in front of family, friends and community members.

**42. Australia Day, Australia's national day, is celebrated on which date?**
- A. 1 January
- B. 26 January ✓
- C. 25 April
- D. The first Monday in June
- *Explanation:* Australia Day is celebrated on 26 January each year, the anniversary of the arrival of the First Fleet in 1788.

**43. Australia Day marks the anniversary of what event?**
- A. Federation, in 1901
- B. The end of the First World War
- C. The arrival of the First Fleet at Sydney Cove in 1788 ✓
- D. The first Olympic Games held in Melbourne
- *Explanation:* Australia Day, 26 January, marks the arrival of the First Fleet at Sydney Cove in 1788.

**44. Which city is the capital of Australia?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Melbourne
- C. Perth
- D. Canberra ✓
- *Explanation:* Canberra is the capital city of Australia and the home of the Australian Parliament.

**45. Why was Canberra chosen as the site of the national capital?**
- A. As a compromise between Sydney and Melbourne, which both wanted to be capital ✓
- B. It was Australia's largest city
- C. Gold was discovered there
- D. Captain Cook landed there
- *Explanation:* Sydney and Melbourne both wanted to be the capital. A new site, Canberra, was chosen as a compromise between the two rival cities.

**46. Which is the largest Australian city by population?**
- A. Melbourne
- B. Sydney ✓
- C. Brisbane
- D. Canberra
- *Explanation:* Sydney, in New South Wales, is Australia's largest city. It is famous for its harbour, Opera House and Harbour Bridge.

**47. About how many people live in Australia today?**
- A. About 6 million
- B. About 16 million
- C. About 26 million ✓
- D. About 46 million
- *Explanation:* Australia's population is approximately 26 million people, living in one of the least densely populated countries in the world.

**48. What is the national language of Australia?**
- A. There is no language commonly spoken in Australia
- B. Only Aboriginal languages
- C. French
- D. English ✓
- *Explanation:* English is the national language of Australia. It is the language of the citizenship test and of everyday public life.

**49. Which two animals appear on the Australian coat of arms?**
- A. The kangaroo and the emu ✓
- B. The koala and the wombat
- C. The platypus and the echidna
- D. The dingo and the emu
- *Explanation:* The Australian coat of arms shows a kangaroo and an emu supporting a shield, surrounded by golden wattle.

**50. Why were the kangaroo and the emu chosen to appear on the coat of arms?**
- A. They are Australia's biggest animals
- B. They can only move forwards, so they symbolise a nation moving forwards ✓
- C. They are found only in zoos
- D. They were the first animals seen by Captain Cook
- *Explanation:* The kangaroo and emu are said to move only forwards. They symbolise a nation always moving forwards.

**51. What are the national colours of Australia?**
- A. Red, white and blue
- B. Black and gold
- C. Green and gold ✓
- D. Blue and silver
- *Explanation:* Australia's national colours are green and gold. They come from the golden wattle, Australia's national flower.

**52. Where do Australia's national colours, green and gold, come from?**
- A. The Australian flag
- B. The opal
- C. The sea and the sand
- D. The golden wattle ✓
- *Explanation:* Green and gold are the colours of the golden wattle, which grows across Australia and flowers in spring.

**53. What is the national flower of Australia?**
- A. The golden wattle ✓
- B. The waratah
- C. The kangaroo paw
- D. The Sturt's desert pea
- *Explanation:* The golden wattle is Australia's national flower. The waratah, kangaroo paw and Sturt's desert pea are state floral emblems.

**54. In which month does the golden wattle usually start to bloom, signalling the start of spring?**
- A. January
- B. September ✓
- C. April
- D. December
- *Explanation:* The golden wattle blooms in spring, from around September, and is celebrated on Wattle Day, 1 September.

**55. What is Australia's national gemstone?**
- A. The diamond
- B. The ruby
- C. The opal ✓
- D. The sapphire
- *Explanation:* The opal is Australia's national gemstone. Australia produces most of the world's precious opals.

**56. What is the name of Australia's national anthem?**
- A. Waltzing Matilda
- B. God Save the King
- C. I Still Call Australia Home
- D. Advance Australia Fair ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia's national anthem is 'Advance Australia Fair'. It was officially adopted in 1984.

**57. What is the large seven-pointed star below the Union Jack on the Australian flag called?**
- A. The Commonwealth Star ✓
- B. The Tasman Star
- C. The Southern Cross
- D. The Emu Star
- *Explanation:* The large star under the Union Jack is the Commonwealth Star, also called the Federation Star.

**58. What do the points of the Commonwealth Star on the flag represent?**
- A. The seven founding fathers
- B. The six states and one point for the territories ✓
- C. The seven continents
- D. The seven seas
- *Explanation:* Six points of the Commonwealth Star are for the six states. The seventh point is for the territories and any future states.

**59. What group of stars appears on the right-hand side of the Australian flag?**
- A. The Milky Way
- B. The Seven Sisters
- C. The Southern Cross ✓
- D. Orion's Belt
- *Explanation:* The Southern Cross, a constellation of stars visible in the southern hemisphere, appears on the right side of the Australian flag.

**60. The flag in the top left corner of the Australian flag is the flag of which country?**
- A. France
- B. New Zealand
- C. Canada
- D. The United Kingdom ✓
- *Explanation:* The flag in the top left corner is the Union Jack of the United Kingdom, showing Australia's early British heritage.

**61. Where in the sky can the Southern Cross be seen?**
- A. In the southern hemisphere sky ✓
- B. Only at midday
- C. Only from Tasmania
- D. Only during an eclipse
- *Explanation:* The Southern Cross is a group of stars that can be seen in the night sky of the southern hemisphere, including from Australia.

**62. On the Australian coat of arms, what does the shield show?**
- A. A map of Australia
- B. The emblems of the six states ✓
- C. Portraits of six Prime Ministers
- D. Only the Southern Cross
- *Explanation:* The shield at the centre of the coat of arms carries the badges of the six Australian states.

**63. What sits above the shield on the Australian coat of arms?**
- A. The British Crown
- B. A boomerang
- C. The Commonwealth Star ✓
- D. A lone emu
- *Explanation:* The Commonwealth Star, in gold, sits above the shield on the coat of arms, over a wreath of golden wattle.

**64. What is the world's largest coral reef system, found off the coast of Queensland?**
- A. Ningaloo Reef
- B. The Great Southern Reef
- C. The Coral Sea Reef
- D. The Great Barrier Reef ✓
- *Explanation:* The Great Barrier Reef, off the Queensland coast, is the largest coral reef system in the world.

**65. Uluru, the great rock sacred to Aboriginal people, is in which state or territory?**
- A. The Northern Territory ✓
- B. South Australia
- C. Western Australia
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* Uluru is in the Northern Territory, in the Uluru-Kata Tjuta National Park. It is sacred to the Anangu people.

**66. The Great Dividing Range runs mainly along which part of Australia?**
- A. The west coast
- B. The east coast ✓
- C. The centre
- D. Only Tasmania
- *Explanation:* The Great Dividing Range runs down the east coast of Australia, roughly parallel to the coast, from Queensland to Victoria.

**67. What is the longest river in Australia?**
- A. The Darling River
- B. The Lachlan River
- C. The Murray River ✓
- D. The Swan River
- *Explanation:* The Murray River, about 2,500 kilometres long, is Australia's longest river. It forms much of the border between New South Wales and Victoria.

**68. What is the highest mountain on the Australian mainland?**
- A. Mount Uluru
- B. Mount Warning
- C. Mount Buffalo
- D. Mount Kosciuszko ✓
- *Explanation:* Mount Kosciuszko, in the Snowy Mountains of New South Wales, is the highest mountain on the Australian mainland.

**69. Which of these statements about Australia is correct?**
- A. It is the world's largest island and smallest continent ✓
- B. It is the world's largest continent
- C. It is the world's smallest island
- D. It is the world's second largest island
- *Explanation:* Australia is the world's smallest continent and its largest island. It is surrounded by the Indian and Pacific Oceans.

**70. Which ocean is to the west of Australia?**
- A. The Pacific Ocean
- B. The Indian Ocean ✓
- C. The Atlantic Ocean
- D. The Arctic Ocean
- *Explanation:* The Indian Ocean lies to the west and south-west of Australia; the Pacific Ocean lies to the east.

**71. Which ocean is to the east of Australia?**
- A. The Indian Ocean
- B. The Atlantic Ocean
- C. The Pacific Ocean ✓
- D. The Arctic Ocean
- *Explanation:* The Pacific Ocean lies to the east of Australia, between Australia and the Americas.

**72. Tasmania is separated from the Australian mainland by which body of water?**
- A. The Torres Strait
- B. The Timor Sea
- C. Cook Strait
- D. Bass Strait ✓
- *Explanation:* Bass Strait separates the island state of Tasmania from Victoria on the mainland.

**73. Which statement best describes the centre of Australia?**
- A. It is mostly dry desert and semi-arid land ✓
- B. It has snow all year
- C. It rains there every day
- D. It is covered in rainforest
- *Explanation:* Much of inland Australia is desert or semi-arid land. The coastal areas, where most people live, receive more rain.

**74. What is the dry, remote inland of Australia commonly called?**
- A. The Paddock
- B. The Outback ✓
- C. The Never-land
- D. The Scrub
- *Explanation:* The vast, dry inland areas of Australia are commonly called 'the Outback'.

**75. Canberra is located in which territory?**
- A. The Northern Territory
- B. The Jervis Bay Territory
- C. The Australian Capital Territory ✓
- D. Tasmania
- *Explanation:* Canberra is in the Australian Capital Territory (ACT), which was created so the capital would not be in any one state.

**76. How many states are there in the Commonwealth of Australia?**
- A. Five
- B. Seven
- C. Eight
- D. Six ✓
- *Explanation:* There are six states: New South Wales, Victoria, Queensland, South Australia, Western Australia and Tasmania.

**77. Which of these is one of Australia's two mainland territories?**
- A. The Northern Territory ✓
- B. Tasmania
- C. Kangaroo Island
- D. Norfolk Island
- *Explanation:* Australia's two mainland territories are the Australian Capital Territory and the Northern Territory.

**78. Sydney is the capital city of which state?**
- A. Victoria
- B. New South Wales ✓
- C. Queensland
- D. The Australian Capital Territory
- *Explanation:* Sydney is the capital of New South Wales, the oldest and most populous state.

**79. Melbourne is the capital city of which state?**
- A. Tasmania
- B. South Australia
- C. Victoria ✓
- D. Western Australia
- *Explanation:* Melbourne is the capital of Victoria, the smallest mainland state but the second most populous.

**80. Brisbane is the capital city of which state?**
- A. New South Wales
- B. The Northern Territory
- C. Western Australia
- D. Queensland ✓
- *Explanation:* Brisbane is the capital of Queensland, in the north-east of Australia.

**81. Perth is the capital city of which state?**
- A. Western Australia ✓
- B. South Australia
- C. Tasmania
- D. Victoria
- *Explanation:* Perth is the capital of Western Australia, the largest state by area.

**82. Adelaide is the capital city of which state?**
- A. Western Australia
- B. South Australia ✓
- C. The Northern Territory
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* Adelaide is the capital of South Australia. It was founded in 1836 as a planned, free colony.

**83. Hobart is the capital city of which state?**
- A. Victoria
- B. New South Wales
- C. Tasmania ✓
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* Hobart is the capital of Tasmania, the island state south of the mainland.

**84. Darwin is the capital city of which territory?**
- A. Queensland
- B. Western Australia
- C. South Australia
- D. The Northern Territory ✓
- *Explanation:* Darwin is the capital of the Northern Territory, in Australia's tropical north.

**85. New South Wales is historically important because it was what?**
- A. The first British colony in Australia ✓
- B. The last state to join the Federation
- C. A colony founded by gold miners in 1851
- D. Never a British colony
- *Explanation:* New South Wales was the first British colony in Australia, founded at Sydney Cove in 1788.

**86. Which Australian state is an island?**
- A. Victoria
- B. Tasmania ✓
- C. Queensland
- D. Western Australia
- *Explanation:* Tasmania is an island state, separated from the mainland by Bass Strait.

**87. Which is the largest Australian state by area?**
- A. Queensland
- B. New South Wales
- C. Western Australia ✓
- D. South Australia
- *Explanation:* Western Australia is the largest state by area, covering about a third of the continent.

**88. Which is the smallest state on the Australian mainland?**
- A. New South Wales
- B. South Australia
- C. Queensland
- D. Victoria ✓
- *Explanation:* Victoria is the smallest mainland state. Tasmania, an island, is smaller again.

**89. When James Cook claimed the east coast of Australia for Britain in 1770, what did he name it?**
- A. New South Wales ✓
- B. Terra Australis
- C. Australia
- D. Botany
- *Explanation:* Cook claimed the east coast for Britain and named it New South Wales.

**90. Botany Bay is remembered in Australian history as what?**
- A. The first capital of the colony
- B. The place where James Cook first landed in 1770 ✓
- C. A famous goldfield
- D. Australia's first prison island
- *Explanation:* Cook's ship Endeavour landed at Botany Bay in April 1770. The First Fleet later moved north to Sydney Cove, which had a better harbour.

**91. Which colony was planned as a free settlement, without convict transportation?**
- A. New South Wales
- B. Van Diemen's Land
- C. South Australia ✓
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* South Australia was planned as a free province. Unlike most other colonies, it never received transported convicts.

**92. In which year did women first win the right to vote in Commonwealth (federal) elections?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1918
- C. 1945
- D. 1902 ✓
- *Explanation:* From 1902, women could vote in federal elections, making Australia one of the first countries in the world to give women this right.

**93. Sir Douglas Mawson is famous in Australian history as what?**
- A. An Antarctic explorer and scientist ✓
- B. A champion cricketer
- C. A bushranger
- D. The first Governor-General
- *Explanation:* Douglas Mawson led early Australian expeditions to Antarctica in the years before the First World War.

**94. In which year did Melbourne host the Olympic Games?**
- A. 2000
- B. 1956 ✓
- C. 1932
- D. 1988
- *Explanation:* Melbourne hosted the Olympic Games in 1956, the first Olympics held in Australia. Sydney hosted them in 2000.

**95. In which year was the Sydney Opera House officially opened?**
- A. 1932
- B. 1956
- C. 1973 ✓
- D. 2001
- *Explanation:* The Sydney Opera House, one of the world's most famous buildings, was opened by Queen Elizabeth II in 1973.

**96. In which year was the Sydney Harbour Bridge opened?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1915
- C. 1973
- D. 1932 ✓
- *Explanation:* The Sydney Harbour Bridge — nicknamed 'the Coathanger' — was opened in 1932, during the Great Depression.

**97. What is the currency of Australia?**
- A. The Australian dollar ✓
- B. The Australian pound
- C. The Pacific dollar
- D. The Commonwealth crown
- *Explanation:* Australia's currency is the Australian dollar, divided into 100 cents.

**98. Decimal currency was introduced in Australia in which year?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1966 ✓
- C. 1945
- D. 1988
- *Explanation:* Australia changed from pounds, shillings and pence to decimal dollars and cents in February 1966.

**99. The Boxing Day Test at the Melbourne Cricket Ground is a famous match in which sport?**
- A. Rugby league
- B. Australian rules football
- C. Cricket ✓
- D. Soccer
- *Explanation:* The Boxing Day Test is a cricket match that begins on 26 December each year at the Melbourne Cricket Ground.

**100. The Melbourne Cup, called 'the race that stops a nation', is a famous event in which sport?**
- A. Yacht racing
- B. Motor racing
- C. Surfing
- D. Horse racing ✓
- *Explanation:* The Melbourne Cup is Australia's most famous horse race. It is held on the first Tuesday in November.

**101. The first recorded European landing on the Australian coast was made in 1606 by whom?**
- A. The Dutch captain Willem Janszoon ✓
- B. Captain James Cook
- C. Abel Tasman
- D. Dirk Hartog
- *Explanation:* Willem Janszoon sailed the Duyfken into the Gulf of Carpentaria and landed on Cape York in 1606. It was the first recorded European landing in Australia.

**102. What was the name of Willem Janszoon's ship, which reached Cape York in 1606?**
- A. Endeavour
- B. Duyfken ✓
- C. Investigator
- D. Beagle
- *Explanation:* Janszoon's ship was the Duyfken, a small Dutch vessel. He charted part of the Gulf of Carpentaria and Cape York, thinking it joined New Guinea.

**103. In 1616, Dutch explorer Dirk Hartog landed on the west coast and left behind what famous object?**
- A. A church bell
- B. A wooden cross
- C. An inscribed pewter plate ✓
- D. A stone fort
- *Explanation:* Hartog left an inscribed pewter plate nailed to a post on the island that now bears his name, near Shark Bay. The plate is one of the oldest European objects linked to Australia.

**104. The Dutch explorers gave the western part of the Australian continent which name?**
- A. Terra Australis
- B. New South Wales
- C. Van Diemen's Land
- D. New Holland ✓
- *Explanation:* Dutch sailors charted much of the west and north coasts in the 1600s and called the land New Holland. The name was used on maps for about 200 years.

**105. In which year did Abel Tasman sight Tasmania and New Zealand?**
- A. 1642 ✓
- B. 1606
- C. 1770
- D. 1788
- *Explanation:* On his 1642 voyage, Abel Tasman sighted the island he named Van Diemen's Land, now Tasmania. He also reached New Zealand on the same voyage.

**106. Abel Tasman named Van Diemen's Land after whom?**
- A. The King of the Netherlands
- B. Anthony van Diemen, the Dutch governor-general in Batavia ✓
- C. His own father
- D. A town in Holland
- *Explanation:* Tasman named the island after Anthony van Diemen, the governor-general of the Dutch East Indies who had sent him on the voyage. It was renamed Tasmania in 1856.

**107. William Dampier is remembered in Australian history as what?**
- A. The first Governor of New South Wales
- B. The captain of the First Fleet
- C. An English explorer and writer who described the west coast in the late 1600s ✓
- D. The discoverer of gold
- *Explanation:* William Dampier visited the west coast in 1688 and again in 1699, and published an account of the land and its people. His reports influenced later British interest in Australia.

**108. Matthew Flinders became famous for which voyage in 1802-03?**
- A. Founding the city of Melbourne
- B. Crossing the Nullarbor Plain
- C. Finding the first gold
- D. Sailing all the way around Australia and proving it was one continent ✓
- *Explanation:* In the sloop Investigator, Matthew Flinders circumnavigated Australia in 1802-03. His charts proved the east and west were parts of a single continent.

**109. Matthew Flinders popularised which name for the continent in his famous book of 1814?**
- A. Australia ✓
- B. Australasia
- C. Oceania
- D. New Holland
- *Explanation:* Flinders used the name 'Australia' in his book A Voyage to Terra Australis, published in 1814. The name, meaning 'southern land', gradually replaced New Holland.

**110. What was the name of Matthew Flinders' ship on his voyage around Australia?**
- A. Endeavour
- B. Investigator ✓
- C. Duyfken
- D. Sirius
- *Explanation:* Flinders commanded HMS Investigator on the first complete circumnavigation of Australia. He was accompanied by the Aboriginal man Bungaree.

**111. The English word 'kangaroo' comes from which source?**
- A. A Dutch word for a jumping animal
- B. The name of Captain Cook's ship
- C. The Guugu Yimithirr Aboriginal word 'gangurru' ✓
- D. A Latin scientific name
- *Explanation:* Cook's party recorded the word 'gangurru' from Guugu Yimithirr people near the Endeavour River in 1770. Over time it became the English word 'kangaroo'.

**112. George Bass and Matthew Flinders proved that Tasmania was an island by doing what?**
- A. Flying over the strait
- B. Asking Dutch sailors
- C. Reading Tasman's old journal
- D. Sailing right around it in the small sloop Norfolk in 1798-99 ✓
- *Explanation:* Bass and Flinders circumnavigated Van Diemen's Land in the Norfolk in 1798-99. This proved it was separated from the mainland by the strait now called Bass Strait.

**113. Port Arthur in Tasmania is famous today as what?**
- A. A major convict penal settlement ✓
- B. Australia's first goldfield
- C. The first meeting place of Parliament
- D. A famous whaling factory
- *Explanation:* Port Arthur, established in 1830, became one of the harshest convict stations in the colonies. Its ruins are now a World Heritage listed historic site.

**114. The Rum Rebellion of 1808 is significant in Australian history because it was what?**
- A. The first gold rush
- B. The only time a colonial government in Australia was overthrown by the military ✓
- C. The first international cricket match
- D. The beginning of Federation
- *Explanation:* In 1808, officers of the New South Wales Corps deposed Governor William Bligh. It remains the only military takeover of government in Australian history.

**115. Governor William Bligh, overthrown in the Rum Rebellion, had earlier been captain of which famous ship?**
- A. Endeavour
- B. Sirius
- C. HMS Bounty ✓
- D. Investigator
- *Explanation:* Bligh had commanded HMS Bounty, famous for the mutiny of 1789 led by Fletcher Christian. He became Governor of New South Wales in 1806.

**116. Governor Lachlan Macquarie (1810-1821) is remembered mainly for what?**
- A. Discovering gold in New South Wales
- B. Commanding the First Fleet
- C. Founding the city of Adelaide
- D. Developing the young colony and giving many former convicts a fresh start ✓
- *Explanation:* Macquarie built roads, towns and public buildings, and supported 'emancipists' — former convicts — in rebuilding their lives. Sydney's Macquarie Street and Macquarie University carry his name.

**117. John Macarthur is linked with developing which early Australian industry?**
- A. Fine wool from merino sheep ✓
- B. Whaling
- C. Opal mining
- D. Sugar cane farming
- *Explanation:* John and Elizabeth Macarthur pioneered the Australian fine wool industry using merino sheep. Wool exports later became the young nation's great source of wealth.

**118. In which year did the transportation of convicts to New South Wales end?**
- A. 1803
- B. 1840 ✓
- C. 1901
- D. 1868
- *Explanation:* Transportation to New South Wales ended in 1840, as free migration grew and opposition to the system increased. It continued longer in Van Diemen's Land and Western Australia.

**119. The very last convict ship to reach Australia arrived in Western Australia in which year?**
- A. 1788
- B. 1850
- C. 1868 ✓
- D. 1901
- *Explanation:* The Hougoumont brought the last convicts to Western Australia in 1868, ending transportation after 80 years. In total, more than 160,000 convicts were transported to Australia.

**120. Most convicts transported to Australia had been convicted of what kind of offences?**
- A. Murder in most cases
- B. Only treason against the King
- C. Making political speeches
- D. Crimes such as stealing, many of which seem minor today ✓
- *Explanation:* Most convicts were transported for theft — sometimes of items worth very little. Transportation was seen as a way to remove offenders and to build the new colonies.

**121. The Swan River Colony, founded in 1829, grew into which modern capital city?**
- A. Perth ✓
- B. Adelaide
- C. Hobart
- D. Brisbane
- *Explanation:* The Swan River Colony was established on the Swan River in 1829. Its settlement grew into Perth, the capital of Western Australia.

**122. The penal settlement at Moreton Bay later grew into which modern city?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Brisbane ✓
- C. Newcastle
- D. Darwin
- *Explanation:* A penal settlement was founded at Moreton Bay in 1824. The town that grew there became Brisbane, named after Governor Thomas Brisbane.

**123. Hobart, Australia's second-oldest capital city, was founded in which year?**
- A. 1788
- B. 1829
- C. 1804 ✓
- D. 1836
- *Explanation:* Hobart was founded in 1804 on the Derwent River, a year after the first Tasmanian settlement at Risdon Cove. Only Sydney is older among the capital cities.

**124. In which year was the colony of Van Diemen's Land officially renamed Tasmania?**
- A. 1803
- B. 1851
- C. 1901
- D. 1856 ✓
- *Explanation:* The name was changed to Tasmania in 1856, in honour of Abel Tasman. The change also helped the colony move beyond its convict reputation.

**125. The first crossing of the Blue Mountains in 1813, which opened the inland plains to farming, was made by which party?**
- A. Blaxland, Lawson and Wentworth ✓
- B. Burke and Wills
- C. Hume and Hovell
- D. Bass and Flinders
- *Explanation:* Gregory Blaxland, William Lawson and William Charles Wentworth crossed the Blue Mountains in 1813. Their route allowed the colony to expand onto the western plains.

**126. Victoria became a separate colony from New South Wales in which year?**
- A. 1788
- B. 1851 ✓
- C. 1859
- D. 1901
- *Explanation:* Victoria was separated from New South Wales on 1 July 1851. Gold was discovered in the new colony only weeks later, transforming its future.

**127. Queensland became a separate colony in which year?**
- A. 1803
- B. 1851
- C. 1859 ✓
- D. 1888
- *Explanation:* Queensland was separated from New South Wales in 1859, with separation proclaimed on 6 June. That date is still celebrated as Queensland Day.

**128. Edward Hargraves triggered the Australian gold rushes by finding payable gold in 1851 at which place?**
- A. Ballarat
- B. Bendigo
- C. Kalgoorlie
- D. Ophir, near Bathurst ✓
- *Explanation:* Hargraves found gold at Ophir, near Bathurst in New South Wales, in 1851. Rushes to the rich Victorian fields of Ballarat and Bendigo soon followed.

**129. The wealth of the Victorian goldfields in the 1850s made which city boom into one of the richest in the British Empire?**
- A. Melbourne ✓
- B. Sydney
- C. Adelaide
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* Gold money flooded into Melbourne, paying for grand banks, theatres and public buildings. The city was nicknamed 'Marvellous Melbourne'.

**130. The flag raised by the miners at the Eureka Stockade in 1854 showed what design?**
- A. The Union Jack by itself
- B. The Southern Cross on a blue field ✓
- C. A kangaroo and an emu
- D. The Commonwealth Star
- *Explanation:* The Eureka flag shows the white stars of the Southern Cross joined by a cross on a deep blue field. The original flag is kept in Ballarat.

**131. In 1893, Paddy Hannan's discovery started a famous gold rush at which Western Australian town?**
- A. Bathurst
- B. Bendigo
- C. Kalgoorlie ✓
- D. Broken Hill
- *Explanation:* Hannan found gold at Kalgoorlie in 1893. Together with nearby Coolgardie, it launched Western Australia's great gold boom.

**132. Broken Hill in far western New South Wales became famous for which mining wealth?**
- A. Gold mining only
- B. Coal mining
- C. Pearl diving
- D. Silver, lead and zinc mining ✓
- *Explanation:* Broken Hill sits on one of the world's richest silver, lead and zinc deposits. The mining company BHP was founded there in 1885.

**133. Sir Henry Parkes, the long-serving premier of New South Wales, is honoured with which title?**
- A. The Father of Federation ✓
- B. The first Prime Minister
- C. The founder of Melbourne
- D. The discoverer of gold
- *Explanation:* Parkes campaigned for the colonies to unite for many years and is called the Father of Federation. He died in 1896, five years before the Commonwealth began.

**134. Henry Parkes' famous 1889 speech calling on the colonies to unite is known as what?**
- A. The Gettysburg Address
- B. The Tenterfield Oration ✓
- C. The Barton Speech
- D. The Melbourne Declaration
- *Explanation:* Speaking at Tenterfield in October 1889, Parkes argued the colonies should form one federal government. It is seen as the speech that restarted the Federation movement.

**135. Before Federation, trade between the Australian colonies was held back because each colony did what?**
- A. Spoke a different language
- B. Refused to build any roads
- C. Charged its own customs duties and even used different railway track widths ✓
- D. Was fighting a war with the others
- *Explanation:* Travellers and goods faced customs posts at colony borders, and trains could not run through because each colony had chosen a different rail gauge. A single nation promised to end this muddle.

**136. How was the draft Australian Constitution approved before Federation?**
- A. By a decree of the Governor-General
- B. At one meeting of premiers only
- C. By the British army
- D. By voters at referendums held in the colonies in 1898-1900 ✓
- *Explanation:* People in each colony voted on the draft Constitution in a series of referendums. After changes were made, the voters approved it, making Federation a decision of the people.

**137. The Australian Constitution became law in 1900 after it was passed by the British Parliament and given royal assent by whom?**
- A. Queen Victoria ✓
- B. King Edward VII
- C. Queen Elizabeth II
- D. The Governor of New South Wales
- *Explanation:* Queen Victoria signed the Commonwealth of Australia Constitution Act on 9 July 1900. The new Commonwealth began on 1 January 1901.

**138. Who was Australia's first Governor-General?**
- A. Edmund Barton
- B. Lord Hopetoun ✓
- C. Sir Henry Parkes
- D. Lord Tennyson
- *Explanation:* Lord Hopetoun, a former Governor of Victoria, became the first Governor-General in 1901. Edmund Barton, as first Prime Minister, formed the first government.

**139. In which year were the first elections for the new Australian Parliament held?**
- A. 1900
- B. 1902
- C. 1901 ✓
- D. 1910
- *Explanation:* Australians voted in the first federal elections in March 1901. Edmund Barton's Protectionist Party won the largest share and Barton became Prime Minister.

**140. In 1894, South Australia gave women a world first. What was it?**
- A. The right to drive a car
- B. The right to attend university
- C. The right to open a bank account
- D. The right to stand for election to parliament, as well as to vote ✓
- *Explanation:* South Australia was the first place in the world where women could stand for parliament, and one of the first places where women could vote. Women first voted there in the 1895 elections.

**141. How was the design of the Australian national flag chosen in 1901?**
- A. Through a public design competition ✓
- B. By a vote in London
- C. By a decree of Captain Cook
- D. At the 1956 Olympic Games
- *Explanation:* A competition in 1901 drew more than 32,000 entries from the public. The winning design, shared by five entrants, became the Australian flag.

**142. Australian National Flag Day, on 3 September, marks what event?**
- A. The day of Federation
- B. The first flying of the Australian flag, in 1901 ✓
- C. The King's birthday
- D. The end of the Second World War
- *Explanation:* The new flag was first flown on 3 September 1901 above the Royal Exhibition Building in Melbourne. Flag Day is celebrated on that date each year.

**143. The present Australian coat of arms was granted in 1912 by which British monarch?**
- A. Queen Victoria
- B. King Edward VII
- C. King George V ✓
- D. Queen Elizabeth II
- *Explanation:* King George V granted the current coat of arms in 1912, replacing an earlier 1908 design. It shows the badges of the six states on the shield.

**144. Explorers Burke and Wills are remembered for which expedition of 1860-61?**
- A. Sailing around Tasmania
- B. Finding gold at Ballarat
- C. Crossing the Nullarbor Plain
- D. A tragic expedition that crossed the continent from Melbourne to the Gulf of Carpentaria ✓
- *Explanation:* Burke and Wills led the first expedition to cross Australia from south to north. Both men died on the return journey, near Cooper Creek.

**145. The crossings of the continent by explorer John McDouall Stuart helped to make which project possible?**
- A. The Overland Telegraph Line from Adelaide to Darwin ✓
- B. The Sydney Harbour Bridge
- C. The Snowy Mountains Scheme
- D. The Indian Pacific railway
- *Explanation:* Stuart finally crossed the continent in 1862 after several attempts. The Overland Telegraph Line was built along his route a decade later.

**146. When the Overland Telegraph Line was completed in 1872, it linked Australia to what?**
- A. Only Sydney and Melbourne
- B. The rest of the world, through an undersea cable from Darwin ✓
- C. New Zealand by a land bridge
- D. Antarctica by radio
- *Explanation:* A cable under the sea from Darwin to Java connected the telegraph line to Europe. News that once took months by ship now arrived in hours.

**147. Explorer Edward John Eyre, guided by the Aboriginal man Wylie, crossed which harsh region in 1840-41?**
- A. The Simpson Desert
- B. Cape York
- C. The Nullarbor Plain ✓
- D. The Kimberley
- *Explanation:* Eyre and Wylie walked some 1,600 kilometres along the Great Australian Bight, across the treeless Nullarbor. Wylie's loyalty helped Eyre survive the journey.

**148. Inland explorer Charles Sturt travelled down the Murray and Darling rivers hoping to find what?**
- A. A goldfield
- B. The Pacific Ocean
- C. A shortcut to Perth
- D. A great inland sea ✓
- *Explanation:* Many Europeans believed a sea or great lake lay in the centre of the continent. Sturt's journeys of 1828-30 mapped the Murray-Darling river system instead.

**149. The Afghan cameleers of the 1800s and early 1900s are remembered for what contribution?**
- A. Carrying supplies and opening up routes across the dry inland ✓
- B. Building Parliament House in Canberra
- C. Coaching the national cricket team
- D. Founding the city of Darwin
- *Explanation:* Cameleers from Afghanistan and British India carried food, mail and building materials to remote settlements, mines and telegraph stations. Their camels were perfectly suited to the desert.

**150. The famous train The Ghan takes its name from whom?**
- A. A river in the Northern Territory
- B. The Afghan cameleers who pioneered the desert route ✓
- C. An Aboriginal elder
- D. A gold-mining town
- *Explanation:* The Ghan, running between Adelaide and Darwin through Alice Springs, is named in honour of the Afghan cameleers. The line follows the path they once travelled.

**151. Australian colonial troops first served overseas in the Boer War, which was fought in which country?**
- A. North America
- B. France
- C. South Africa ✓
- D. Japan
- *Explanation:* Contingents from the Australian colonies, and then from the new Commonwealth, served in the Boer War in South Africa from 1899 to 1902.

**152. The famous charge of the Australian Light Horse in 1917 captured the town of Beersheba, in which region?**
- A. France
- B. Belgium
- C. Turkey
- D. Palestine ✓
- *Explanation:* On 31 October 1917, Australian Light Horsemen charged the wells at Beersheba, in what is now Israel. It is remembered as one of the last great mounted charges in history.

**153. John Simpson Kirkpatrick is remembered at Gallipoli for doing what?**
- A. Rescuing wounded soldiers with the help of a donkey ✓
- B. Flying the first aeroplane
- C. Sinking an enemy ship
- D. Composing the national anthem
- *Explanation:* Simpson carried wounded men down from the front line on a donkey, until he was killed in May 1915. 'Simpson and his donkey' became a beloved symbol of courage and mateship.

**154. Sir John Monash is remembered as what?**
- A. A wartime prime minister
- B. A great Australian general of the First World War, and a noted engineer ✓
- C. A champion cricketer
- D. Australia's first fighter pilot
- *Explanation:* Monash, an engineer in civilian life, commanded the Australian Corps in France in 1918. Monash University in Melbourne is named after him.

**155. During the First World War, Australian voters twice rejected government proposals to introduce what?**
- A. Voting rights for women
- B. Federation
- C. Conscription for overseas service ✓
- D. The secret ballot
- *Explanation:* In referendums in 1916 and 1917, Australians voted 'No' to compulsory military service overseas. Australia's First World War army remained an all-volunteer force.

**156. The Australian soldiers nicknamed 'the Rats of Tobruk' held out during a long siege in 1941 at Tobruk, in which country?**
- A. Singapore
- B. Greece
- C. Darwin
- D. Libya ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian troops held the port of Tobruk in Libya for months against German and Italian forces. Enemy radio called them 'rats', and the men proudly adopted the name.

**157. On 19 February 1942, Japanese aircraft carried out a major bombing raid on which Australian city?**
- A. Darwin ✓
- B. Sydney
- C. Perth
- D. Brisbane
- *Explanation:* The bombing of Darwin was the largest attack by a foreign power on the Australian mainland. More than 240 people were killed in the first raids.

**158. Who was Prime Minister of Australia for most of the Second World War?**
- A. Robert Menzies
- B. John Curtin ✓
- C. Edmund Barton
- D. Billy Hughes
- *Explanation:* John Curtin led Australia from October 1941 through the darkest years of the war, until his death in July 1945, only weeks before the war ended.

**159. The Papuan carriers who helped carry wounded Australian soldiers along the Kokoda Track were affectionately nicknamed what?**
- A. The Diggers
- B. The Rats
- C. The Fuzzy Wuzzy Angels ✓
- D. The Light Horse
- *Explanation:* Papuan carriers carried supplies and stretchered wounded Australians to safety over the mountains. Their kindness and care saved many lives.

**160. Victory in the Pacific Day, marking the end of the Second World War for Australia, fell on 15 August in which year?**
- A. 1918
- B. 1939
- C. 1950
- D. 1945 ✓
- *Explanation:* On 15 August 1945, Japan accepted the Allied terms and the war ended. Australians celebrated in the streets across the nation.

**161. The Australian War Memorial, which combines a shrine, a museum and an archive, stands in which city?**
- A. Canberra ✓
- B. Sydney
- C. Melbourne
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* The Australian War Memorial opened in Canberra in 1941. Its Roll of Honour records the names of more than 100,000 Australians who died in war.

**162. Melbourne's great memorial to those who served in the First World War is known as what?**
- A. The Opera House
- B. The Shrine of Remembrance ✓
- C. The Eureka Tower
- D. Federation Square
- *Explanation:* The Shrine of Remembrance was built in the years after the First World War. Its Stone of Remembrance is lit by a ray of sunlight at 11 am on 11 November.

**163. Australian forces served in the Korean War during which years?**
- A. 1914-1918
- B. 1939-1945
- C. 1950-1953 ✓
- D. 1975-1980
- *Explanation:* Australia was one of the first countries to support South Korea in 1950, sending ships, aircraft and soldiers. More than 17,000 Australians served in the Korean War.

**164. Australia's longest major war involvement of the twentieth century was in which country?**
- A. Korea
- B. South Africa
- C. Iraq
- D. Vietnam ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian advisers arrived in Vietnam in 1962 and combat troops served until 1972. More than 60,000 Australians served in Australia's longest war of the century.

**165. During the Vietnam War era, some young Australian men were chosen for national service by what method?**
- A. A ballot based on their birth dates ✓
- B. A test of their height
- C. A vote in parliament
- D. A measure of their family's wealth
- *Explanation:* From 1964, 20-year-old men were selected for national service by a lottery of birth dates, drawn from a barrel. The scheme was controversial and ended in 1972.

**166. The Ode of Remembrance, recited at Anzac services ('They shall grow not old...'), comes from a poem by whom?**
- A. Banjo Paterson
- B. Laurence Binyon ✓
- C. Henry Lawson
- D. C.J. Dennis
- *Explanation:* The words come from 'For the Fallen', written by English poet Laurence Binyon in 1914. They are followed by the response 'Lest we forget'.

**167. Dorothea Mackellar is best loved for which poem, beginning 'I love a sunburnt country'?**
- A. The Man from Snowy River
- B. Waltzing Matilda
- C. My Country ✓
- D. Clancy of the Overflow
- *Explanation:* Mackellar wrote 'My Country' as a young woman homesick in England. Its lines about a 'sunburnt country' are among the most quoted in Australian literature.

**168. The famous bush ballad 'The Man from Snowy River' was written by whom?**
- A. Henry Lawson
- B. Dorothea Mackellar
- C. Mary Gilmore
- D. Banjo Paterson ✓
- *Explanation:* Banjo Paterson published 'The Man from Snowy River' in 1895. The poem tells of a thrilling mountain ride to recapture a valuable colt.

**169. Writer Henry Lawson is remembered mainly for what?**
- A. Short stories and poems about everyday life in the bush ✓
- B. Commanding troops at Gallipoli
- C. Being a famous bushranger
- D. Discovering gold at Lambing Flat
- *Explanation:* Lawson's stories, such as 'The Drover's Wife', wrote honestly about hardship, humour and mateship in the Australian bush. He is regarded as one of Australia's finest short story writers.

**170. Dame Mary Gilmore is remembered as what?**
- A. An opera singer
- B. A poet and campaigner for workers and women ✓
- C. An Olympic swimmer
- D. The first woman prime minister
- *Explanation:* Mary Gilmore wrote poetry and campaigned for better conditions for workers, women and children. Her portrait appears on the Australian ten-dollar note.

**171. The Australian and New Zealand Army Corps was formed in 1914, before the Gallipoli landing, while troops were training in which country?**
- A. Australia
- B. New Zealand
- C. Egypt ✓
- D. England
- *Explanation:* The corps was formed in Egypt, where Australian and New Zealand troops were training. From the initials A.N.Z.A.C. came the word 'Anzac'.

**172. The Unknown Australian Soldier was laid to rest at the Australian War Memorial on 11 November in which year?**
- A. 1918
- B. 1945
- C. 2000
- D. 1993 ✓
- *Explanation:* The remains of an unknown soldier were brought from a war cemetery in France and entombed in 1993. Prime Minister Paul Keating delivered the famous eulogy at the ceremony.

**173. The well-known song 'I Was Only Nineteen' tells the story of an Australian soldier in which war?**
- A. The Vietnam War ✓
- B. The First World War
- C. The Second World War
- D. The Korean War
- *Explanation:* Redgum's 1983 song follows a young conscript's experience in Vietnam. It helped many Australians understand what returning soldiers had been through.

**174. 'Waltzing Matilda' was first performed in 1895 in which Queensland town?**
- A. Brisbane
- B. Winton ✓
- C. Cairns
- D. Toowoomba
- *Explanation:* Banjo Paterson wrote the song at Dagworth Station and it was first performed at Winton in April 1895. A museum in Winton, the Waltzing Matilda Centre, celebrates the song.

**175. The first Anzac Day commemorations were held on the first anniversary of the landing, on 25 April in which year?**
- A. 1915
- B. 1925
- C. 1916 ✓
- D. 1945
- *Explanation:* Services and marches were held across Australia on 25 April 1916, while the war was still being fought. Anzac Day has been commemorated ever since.

**176. Australian citizenship, as a legal status separate from being a British subject, began on 26 January in which year?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1910
- C. 1930
- D. 1949 ✓
- *Explanation:* The Nationality and Citizenship Act created Australian citizenship from 26 January 1949. Before then, Australians were British subjects.

**177. The very first Australian citizenship ceremony was held in 1949 in which city?**
- A. Canberra ✓
- B. Sydney
- C. Melbourne
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* The first ceremony was held at the Albert Hall in Canberra on 3 February 1949, when seven men from seven different countries became citizens. Citizenship ceremonies are now held in communities all over Australia.

**178. Australian Citizenship Day is celebrated each year on which date?**
- A. 26 January
- B. 17 September ✓
- C. 1 September
- D. 25 April
- *Explanation:* Citizenship Day, on 17 September, encourages all Australians to reflect on the meaning and value of citizenship. Many new citizens choose to receive their citizenship on this day.

**179. British migrants who came to Australia after the Second World War for a fare of only ten pounds were nicknamed what?**
- A. The First Fleeters
- B. The Pound Savers
- C. Ten Pound Poms ✓
- D. The New Chums
- *Explanation:* Under the assisted passage scheme, British migrants paid just ten pounds for the ship fare to Australia. The scheme brought more than a million British migrants.

**180. The Snowy Mountains Scheme, built between 1949 and 1974, is famous for what?**
- A. Building the Sydney Opera House
- B. Finding gold in the mountains
- C. Growing Australia's first wheat
- D. Building dams and power stations with a workforce drawn from more than 30 countries ✓
- *Explanation:* More than 100,000 people worked on the Snowy Scheme, and about two-thirds were born overseas. It became a proud symbol of a modern, multicultural Australia.

**181. Who became Australia's first Minister for Immigration in 1945?**
- A. Arthur Calwell ✓
- B. Edmund Barton
- C. John Curtin
- D. Robert Menzies
- *Explanation:* Arthur Calwell led the new Department of Immigration from 1945. He promoted the slogan 'populate or perish' and opened Australia to large-scale migration.

**182. The Bonegilla migrant reception centre, through which hundreds of thousands of new arrivals passed, was in which state?**
- A. New South Wales
- B. Victoria ✓
- C. Queensland
- D. South Australia
- *Explanation:* Bonegilla, near Wodonga in Victoria, operated from 1947 to 1971. More than 300,000 migrants from many countries began their Australian lives there.

**183. After the fall of Saigon in 1975, Australia welcomed many refugees from which country?**
- A. Korea
- B. Iraq
- C. Vietnam ✓
- D. Afghanistan
- *Explanation:* Following the end of the Vietnam War in 1975, Australia accepted tens of thousands of Vietnamese refugees. Vietnamese communities became an important part of Australian life.

**184. The old 'White Australia policy', which was dismantled between the 1960s and 1973, had mainly restricted what?**
- A. Trade with Asia
- B. Voting rights in cities
- C. Sporting contests
- D. Migration to Australia by people who were not European ✓
- *Explanation:* The policy, dating from 1901, favoured European migrants and excluded most others. It was gradually dismantled after the Second World War and ended in 1973.

**185. The Racial Discrimination Act, which made discrimination because of race unlawful, was passed in which year?**
- A. 1975 ✓
- B. 1901
- C. 1949
- D. 2000
- *Explanation:* The Racial Discrimination Act 1975 put into law Australia's commitment that all people are equal, whatever their race or background. It supports the multicultural society Australia has become.

**186. Harmony Day, held on 21 March, celebrates what in Australian society?**
- A. The anniversary of Federation
- B. Australia's cultural diversity, inclusiveness and respect for everyone who calls Australia home ✓
- C. The end of the Second World War
- D. The wheat harvest
- *Explanation:* Harmony Day coincides with the United Nations International Day for the Elimination of Racial Discrimination. Schools and communities mark it with shared food, music and orange ribbons.

**187. In the years after the Second World War, which two Mediterranean countries provided especially large numbers of migrants to Australia?**
- A. Spain and Portugal
- B. France and Croatia
- C. Greece and Italy ✓
- D. Turkey and Egypt
- *Explanation:* Greek and Italian migrants, along with people from Britain, the Netherlands, Germany and many other countries, helped rebuild Australia after the war. Their food, festivals and family life enriched Australian culture.

**188. After the Second World War, the term 'New Australians' was commonly used to describe whom?**
- A. Australian-born babies
- B. New army recruits
- C. Gold miners
- D. Newly arrived migrants ✓
- *Explanation:* The term 'New Australians' was meant as a welcome: migrants were not foreigners forever, but Australians in the making. Many older Australians still remember being called New Australians as children.

**189. Australia's Bicentenary — 200 years since the arrival of the First Fleet — was celebrated in which year?**
- A. 1988 ✓
- B. 1976
- C. 2001
- D. 1951
- *Explanation:* The Bicentenary in 1988 was marked by tall ships, festivals and the opening of the new Parliament House in Canberra. A re-enactment fleet sailed into Sydney Harbour on 26 January.

**190. The Centenary of Federation, marking 100 years of the Commonwealth of Australia, was celebrated in which year?**
- A. 1988
- B. 2001 ✓
- C. 1999
- D. 2011
- *Explanation:* Celebrations in 2001 included a great parade in Melbourne, where the first Parliament had met a century earlier. Many communities held their own Federation festivals.

**191. In the 1999 referendum, Australian voters were asked whether Australia should become what?**
- A. A member of the United Nations
- B. An Olympic host nation
- C. A republic ✓
- D. A state of the United States
- *Explanation:* The 1999 referendum proposed replacing the monarch with an Australian president chosen by Parliament. The proposal was not approved, and Australia remained a constitutional monarchy.

**192. At the Sydney 2000 Olympic Games, Cathy Freeman won the gold medal in which athletics event?**
- A. The 100 metres sprint
- B. The marathon
- C. The 200 metres sprint
- D. The 400 metres sprint ✓
- *Explanation:* Freeman's victory in the 400 metres, before a roaring home crowd, was one of the most memorable moments of the Sydney Games. She carried both the Australian and Aboriginal flags on her lap of honour.

**193. Cathy Freeman also performed which honour at the Sydney Olympic opening ceremony?**
- A. Lighting the Olympic cauldron ✓
- B. Carrying the team flag only
- C. Singing the national anthem
- D. Reading the athlete's oath for all nations
- *Explanation:* Freeman lit the cauldron at the opening ceremony, rising from a pool of water ringed by fire. Her gold medal win ten days later united the country in celebration.

**194. World Expo 88, a major international exhibition held during the Bicentenary, was hosted by which city?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Brisbane ✓
- C. Perth
- D. Adelaide
- *Explanation:* Expo 88 drew millions of visitors to Brisbane's South Bank. The riverside parklands enjoyed by Brisbane today were created on the Expo site.

**195. The yacht that won the America's Cup for Australia in 1983 was named what?**
- A. Kookaburra III
- B. Gretel II
- C. Australia II ✓
- D. Southern Cross
- *Explanation:* Australia II, with its secret winged keel, beat the American yacht Liberty in 1983. It was the first time in 132 years that the trophy left the United States.

**196. In 1928, Charles Kingsford Smith and his crew made the first aeroplane flight across which ocean?**
- A. The Atlantic Ocean
- B. The Indian Ocean
- C. The Arctic Ocean
- D. The Pacific Ocean ✓
- *Explanation:* Kingsford Smith flew the Southern Cross from the United States to Brisbane, via Hawaii and Fiji, in 1928. He became one of Australia's most celebrated aviators.

**197. Howard Florey shared a Nobel Prize for his work developing which life-saving medicine?**
- A. Penicillin ✓
- B. Insulin
- C. Aspirin
- D. The polio vaccine
- *Explanation:* Working at Oxford, Florey and his team turned penicillin into a usable drug during the Second World War. It has saved hundreds of millions of lives.

**198. The 'black box' flight recorder, carried on aircraft around the world, was invented by Australian scientist?**
- A. Graeme Clark
- B. David Warren ✓
- C. Howard Florey
- D. Victor Chang
- *Explanation:* David Warren, a Melbourne scientist, designed the first flight recorder in the 1950s after investigating a series of jet crashes. Modern recorders are actually painted bright orange.

**199. Professor Graeme Clark led the Australian team that developed which world-changing medical device?**
- A. The heart pacemaker
- B. The ultrasound scanner
- C. The cochlear implant, or bionic ear ✓
- D. The Hills Hoist
- *Explanation:* The bionic ear, first implanted in 1978, has given hearing to hundreds of thousands of people around the world. It is one of Australia's most successful inventions.

**200. Dr Victor Chang is remembered as one of Australia's pioneers in which field of medicine?**
- A. Eye surgery
- B. Cancer research
- C. Brain surgery
- D. Heart transplant surgery ✓
- *Explanation:* Victor Chang founded the heart transplant unit at St Vincent's Hospital in Sydney. He is honoured as one of Australia's greatest surgeons.

**201. What is the floral emblem of New South Wales?**
- A. The waratah ✓
- B. The kangaroo paw
- C. The common heath
- D. The Cooktown orchid
- *Explanation:* The waratah, a striking red flower of the Sydney bushland, became the floral emblem of New South Wales in 1962. Its name comes from an Aboriginal word.

**202. What is the floral emblem of Victoria?**
- A. The waratah
- B. The common heath (pink heath) ✓
- C. The Tasmanian blue gum flower
- D. Sturt's desert pea
- *Explanation:* The pink common heath was proclaimed Victoria's floral emblem in 1958. It was the first of the Australian state floral emblems to be chosen.

**203. What is the floral emblem of Queensland?**
- A. The royal bluebell
- B. The kangaroo paw
- C. The Cooktown orchid ✓
- D. Sturt's desert rose
- *Explanation:* The Cooktown orchid, a purple orchid from tropical north Queensland, was adopted as the state's floral emblem in 1959. It grows naturally in the Cape York region.

**204. What is the floral emblem of South Australia?**
- A. The common heath
- B. The waratah
- C. The blue gum flower
- D. Sturt's desert pea ✓
- *Explanation:* Sturt's desert pea, with its scarlet flowers and black centres, grows in the dry inland. It is named after explorer Charles Sturt.

**205. What is the floral emblem of Western Australia?**
- A. The red and green kangaroo paw ✓
- B. The Cooktown orchid
- C. Sturt's desert pea
- D. The royal bluebell
- *Explanation:* The kangaroo paw is named for its velvety, paw-shaped flowers. It grows only in the south-west of Western Australia, one of the world's great wildflower regions.

**206. What is the floral emblem of Tasmania?**
- A. The waratah
- B. The Tasmanian blue gum flower ✓
- C. The common heath
- D. The kangaroo paw
- *Explanation:* The flowering Tasmanian blue gum was proclaimed Tasmania's floral emblem in 1962. Its cream flowers appear on one of the world's tallest flowering trees.

**207. What is the floral emblem of the Northern Territory?**
- A. Sturt's desert pea
- B. The waratah
- C. Sturt's desert rose ✓
- D. The Cooktown orchid
- *Explanation:* Sturt's desert rose, with soft pink-purple flowers, grows in the Territory's dry ranges. It appears on the Northern Territory flag.

**208. What is the floral emblem of the Australian Capital Territory?**
- A. The kangaroo paw
- B. The common heath
- C. The blue gum flower
- D. The royal bluebell ✓
- *Explanation:* The royal bluebell, a small purple-blue wildflower, was adopted as the ACT's floral emblem in 1982. It grows in the high country around Canberra.

**209. What is the bird emblem of New South Wales?**
- A. The kookaburra ✓
- B. The black swan
- C. The brolga
- D. The wedge-tailed eagle
- *Explanation:* The laughing kookaburra, famous for its dawn and dusk chorus, is the bird emblem of New South Wales. It is the world's largest kingfisher.

**210. What is the bird emblem of Victoria?**
- A. The kookaburra
- B. The helmeted honeyeater ✓
- C. The yellow wattlebird
- D. The piping shrike
- *Explanation:* The helmeted honeyeater is found only in a small area east of Melbourne. It is one of Australia's most endangered birds and Victoria's bird emblem.

**211. What is the bird emblem of Queensland?**
- A. The black swan
- B. The gang-gang cockatoo
- C. The brolga ✓
- D. The emu
- *Explanation:* The brolga, a tall grey crane famous for its dancing displays, is Queensland's bird emblem. Brolgas live in wetlands across northern and eastern Australia.

**212. What is the bird emblem of South Australia?**
- A. The kookaburra
- B. The brolga
- C. The wedge-tailed eagle
- D. The piping shrike ✓
- *Explanation:* The piping shrike — the white-backed magpie — appears on the South Australian flag and badge. It has been part of the state's identity since the 1900s.

**213. What is the bird emblem of Western Australia?**
- A. The black swan ✓
- B. The helmeted honeyeater
- C. The yellow wattlebird
- D. The brolga
- *Explanation:* The black swan appears on Western Australia's flag and coat of arms. Early Dutch explorers were astonished to find black swans on the Swan River in 1697.

**214. What is the bird emblem of Tasmania?**
- A. The black swan
- B. The yellow wattlebird ✓
- C. The kookaburra
- D. The piping shrike
- *Explanation:* The yellow wattlebird, found only in Tasmania, is Australia's largest honeyeater. It feeds in flowering eucalypt forests.

**215. What is the bird emblem of the Northern Territory?**
- A. The brolga
- B. The gang-gang cockatoo
- C. The wedge-tailed eagle ✓
- D. The helmeted honeyeater
- *Explanation:* The wedge-tailed eagle, Australia's largest bird of prey, soars over the Territory's ranges. It is also the emblem of the NT Parks and Wildlife Service.

**216. What is the animal (mammal) emblem of New South Wales?**
- A. The koala
- B. The numbat
- C. The Tasmanian devil
- D. The platypus ✓
- *Explanation:* The platypus, which lives in the rivers of eastern Australia, became the animal emblem of New South Wales in 1971. It is one of the world's few egg-laying mammals, known as monotremes.

**217. What is the animal emblem of Victoria?**
- A. Leadbeater's possum ✓
- B. The platypus
- C. The numbat
- D. The red kangaroo
- *Explanation:* Leadbeater's possum, a small possum of Victoria's mountain ash forests, was thought extinct until rediscovered in 1961. It became the state's animal emblem in 1971.

**218. What is the animal emblem of Queensland?**
- A. The platypus
- B. The koala ✓
- C. The common wombat
- D. The bilby
- *Explanation:* The koala was proclaimed Queensland's animal emblem in 1971. Koalas live in eucalypt forests along the east coast and eat mostly gum leaves.

**219. What is the animal emblem of South Australia?**
- A. The numbat
- B. The Tasmanian devil
- C. The southern hairy-nosed wombat ✓
- D. The koala
- *Explanation:* The southern hairy-nosed wombat, a sturdy burrowing animal of the dry plains, is South Australia's animal emblem. Wombats dig extensive burrow systems with their strong claws.

**220. What is the animal emblem of Western Australia?**
- A. The platypus
- B. The koala
- C. Leadbeater's possum
- D. The numbat ✓
- *Explanation:* The numbat, a small striped termite-eating marsupial, survives only in the south-west of Western Australia. It is the state's animal emblem and is active by day, unlike most marsupials.

**221. What is the animal emblem of Tasmania?**
- A. The Tasmanian devil ✓
- B. The common wombat
- C. The bilby
- D. The quokka
- *Explanation:* The Tasmanian devil, the world's largest surviving carnivorous marsupial, is found in the wild only in Tasmania. Its powerful jaws and night-time screeches made it famous.

**222. What is the animal emblem of the Northern Territory?**
- A. The Tasmanian devil
- B. The red kangaroo ✓
- C. The numbat
- D. The platypus
- *Explanation:* The red kangaroo, the largest of all marsupials, appears on the Northern Territory coat of arms. Red kangaroos are superbly adapted to the arid centre.

**223. The winning design for Canberra, chosen in an international competition in 1912, was created by which architect?**
- A. Jorn Utzon
- B. Harry Seidler
- C. Walter Burley Griffin ✓
- D. Robin Boyd
- *Explanation:* American architect Walter Burley Griffin designed Canberra around the hills and a great lake. The lake, filled in 1964, is named Lake Burley Griffin in his honour.

**224. The Australian Capital Territory was created in 1911 when land was transferred from which state?**
- A. Victoria
- B. Queensland
- C. Tasmania
- D. New South Wales ✓
- *Explanation:* New South Wales gave the land for the capital territory, as the Constitution required the seat of government to be within NSW territory but not within 100 miles of Sydney.

**225. In which year did the Northern Territory gain self-government?**
- A. 1978 ✓
- B. 1901
- C. 1911
- D. 2001
- *Explanation:* The Northern Territory became self-governing on 1 July 1978, with its own Legislative Assembly in Darwin. Its residents had gained full voting rights in federal elections earlier, in 1968.

**226. K'gari, also known as Fraser Island, off the Queensland coast, is the world's largest island of what kind?**
- A. A coral island
- B. A sand island ✓
- C. A volcanic island
- D. An artificial island
- *Explanation:* K'gari is the largest sand island in the world, built entirely from sand carried by currents over hundreds of thousands of years. It is World Heritage listed and sacred to the Butchulla people.

**227. Kangaroo Island, off the coast of South Australia, ranks as Australia's what?**
- A. Largest island
- B. Smallest island
- C. Third-largest island ✓
- D. Northernmost island
- *Explanation:* After Tasmania and Melville Island, Kangaroo Island is Australia's third-largest island. It is famous for sea lions, koalas and dramatic coastal rock formations.

**228. Rottnest Island, just off Perth, is famous around the world for which small, smiling marsupial?**
- A. The bilby
- B. The wombat
- C. The numbat
- D. The quokka ✓
- *Explanation:* Dutch sailors mistook quokkas for giant rats and named the island 'Rottnest' — rats' nest. Quokkas are now loved worldwide for their friendly appearance.

**229. The Tiwi Islands, just north of Darwin, are made up of Bathurst Island and which other large island?**
- A. Melville Island ✓
- B. Kangaroo Island
- C. King Island
- D. Groote Eylandt
- *Explanation:* The Tiwi Islands — Bathurst and Melville — are home to the Tiwi people, known for distinctive art, ceremonies and Australian rules football. The islands are sometimes called the 'Islands of Smiles'.

**230. Australia's largest lake, in South Australia, is which lake?**
- A. Lake Hume
- B. Lake Eyre (Kati Thanda) ✓
- C. Lake Burley Griffin
- D. Lake Argyle
- *Explanation:* Lake Eyre, officially Kati Thanda–Lake Eyre, is usually a vast salt pan. On the rare occasions it floods, it becomes a wonderland of waterbirds in the desert.

**231. Steep Point, the westernmost point of the Australian mainland, is in which state?**
- A. South Australia
- B. The Northern Territory
- C. Western Australia ✓
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* Steep Point, near Shark Bay, is the most westerly point of the mainland. From there, the next land to the west is Africa, across the Indian Ocean.

**232. The Great Artesian Basin, underlying about one-fifth of the continent, is important because it holds what?**
- A. Australia's largest gold deposit
- B. A giant coral reef
- C. Most of Australia's coal
- D. Huge stores of underground water beneath the dry inland ✓
- *Explanation:* Bores tapping the Great Artesian Basin have watered sheep and cattle stations across the inland for more than a century. It is one of the largest underground water sources in the world.

**233. What is the largest desert in Australia?**
- A. The Great Victoria Desert ✓
- B. The Simpson Desert
- C. The Tanami Desert
- D. The Gibson Desert
- *Explanation:* The Great Victoria Desert, stretching across Western Australia and South Australia, is the country's largest desert. Its sand dunes and salt lakes cover an area bigger than many European countries.

**234. The name 'Nullarbor', for the great plain along the Great Australian Bight, comes from Latin words meaning what?**
- A. Big sky
- B. No trees ✓
- C. Red sand
- D. Far away
- *Explanation:* 'Nullarbor' combines the Latin nullus (none) and arbor (tree). Early travellers were struck by the plain's almost complete lack of trees.

**235. The Eyre Highway crossing the Nullarbor includes a famous feature: one of the world's longest what?**
- A. Road tunnels
- B. River bridges
- C. Straight stretches of road — about 146 kilometres without a bend ✓
- D. Ferry crossings
- *Explanation:* Between Balladonia and Caiguna in Western Australia, the highway runs dead straight for about 146 kilometres. It is often called the '90 Mile Straight'.

**236. The northernmost point of the Australian mainland is found at which place?**
- A. Darwin
- B. Cairns
- C. Broome
- D. The tip of Cape York in Queensland ✓
- *Explanation:* The rocky tip of Cape York Peninsula is the northernmost point of the mainland. From there, the islands of Torres Strait stretch towards Papua New Guinea.

**237. The easternmost point of the Australian mainland is which headland?**
- A. Cape Byron in New South Wales ✓
- B. Point Hicks in Victoria
- C. Cape York in Queensland
- D. Wilsons Promontory in Victoria
- *Explanation:* Cape Byron, near Byron Bay, is the mainland's most easterly point and the first place in Australia to see the sunrise each day. Its lighthouse began operating in 1901.

**238. The southernmost point of the Australian mainland is in which national park, in Victoria?**
- A. The Grampians
- B. Wilsons Promontory ✓
- C. Port Campbell
- D. Kakadu
- *Explanation:* South Point, in Wilsons Promontory National Park, is the southernmost point of the mainland. 'The Prom' is one of Victoria's most loved national parks.

**239. At about 15 metres below sea level, the shore of Lake Eyre is notable as what?**
