- A. Australia's hottest place
- B. Australia's wettest place
- C. The lowest point on the Australian continent ✓
- D. Australia's oldest town site
- *Explanation:* The Lake Eyre basin contains the continent's lowest point. In flood years, pelicans and other waterbirds somehow find their way to the filled lake from hundreds of kilometres away.

**240. Mount Augustus in Western Australia is often described as what?**
- A. Australia's highest mountain
- B. Australia's largest town
- C. An active volcano
- D. One of the largest single rocks in the world, even larger than Uluru ✓
- *Explanation:* Mount Augustus, called Burringurrah by the Wajarri people, rises from the Gascoyne region. It is about twice the size of Uluru and is often called the world's largest monocline.

**241. The Bungle Bungle Range, famous for its orange-and-black striped domes, is in which Western Australian national park?**
- A. Purnululu National Park ✓
- B. Kakadu National Park
- C. Uluru-Kata Tjuta National Park
- D. The Blue Mountains National Park
- *Explanation:* The beehive-shaped towers of Purnululu, in the Kimberley, were known to Aboriginal people for tens of thousands of years. The area became World Heritage listed in 2003.

**242. The Devils Marbles, a famous cluster of huge rounded granite boulders, are found in which state or territory?**
- A. Tasmania
- B. The Northern Territory ✓
- C. Victoria
- D. New South Wales
- *Explanation:* Karlu Karlu, the Devils Marbles, lie beside the Stuart Highway south of Tennant Creek. The site is sacred to the Warumungu, Kaytetye, Alyawarra and Warlpiri peoples.

**243. Wave Rock, a giant rock formation shaped like a breaking ocean wave, is in which state?**
- A. South Australia
- B. New South Wales
- C. Western Australia ✓
- D. Queensland
- *Explanation:* Wave Rock, near the town of Hyden, is 15 metres high and about 110 metres long. It was formed by weathering and water erosion over millions of years.

**244. Which is Australia's largest national park, covering nearly 20,000 square kilometres?**
- A. Uluru-Kata Tjuta National Park
- B. The Blue Mountains National Park
- C. Great Sandy National Park
- D. Kakadu National Park ✓
- *Explanation:* Kakadu, in the Northern Territory, protects wetlands, wildlife and Aboriginal rock art galleries thousands of years old. It is jointly managed with its Traditional Owners.

**245. The Daintree region of Queensland is world-famous as what?**
- A. One of the oldest tropical rainforests on Earth ✓
- B. Australia's first goldfield
- C. Australia's largest desert
- D. A famous ski field
- *Explanation:* The Daintree rainforest has survived, in changing forms, for well over 100 million years. It is home to plants and animals found nowhere else.

**246. Shark Bay in Western Australia is World Heritage listed partly for its stromatolites, which are what?**
- A. Giant natural pearls
- B. Rock-like structures built by tiny living organisms over thousands of years ✓
- C. Extinct volcanoes
- D. Shipwrecks from the 1600s
- *Explanation:* Stromatolites at Hamelin Pool are among the oldest forms of life on Earth, similar to organisms that lived billions of years ago. Shark Bay also shelters dugongs and dolphins.

**247. Ningaloo Reef, where visitors can swim with whale sharks, lies off the coast of which state?**
- A. Queensland
- B. Tasmania
- C. Western Australia ✓
- D. Victoria
- *Explanation:* Ningaloo is a fringing coral reef close to the shore near Exmouth. Whale sharks, the world's largest fish, visit each year after the coral spawning.

**248. The Kimberley region, known for deep gorges, boab trees and enormous tides, is in which part of Australia?**
- A. The Queensland coast
- B. The Northern Territory
- C. South Australia
- D. The far north of Western Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* The Kimberley is one of the world's last great wilderness areas. Its huge tides, among the biggest on Earth, create the famous Horizontal Falls.

**249. The Blue Mountains, west of Sydney, take their name from what?**
- A. A blue haze given off by eucalyptus trees ✓
- B. Blue wildflowers that cover the slopes
- C. Blue-coloured rock walls
- D. A famous painter
- *Explanation:* Oil from millions of eucalyptus leaves hangs in the air over the valleys, scattering blue light. The region was World Heritage listed in 2000.

**250. The Twelve Apostles, towering limestone stacks in the sea, stand along which famous Victorian coastal road?**
- A. The Pacific Highway
- B. The Great Ocean Road ✓
- C. The Hume Highway
- D. The Bruce Highway
- *Explanation:* The Twelve Apostles rise from the Southern Ocean near Port Campbell. Erosion continues to shape the coast, and some stacks have collapsed into the sea.

**251. The Ashes, the celebrated Test cricket series, is contested between Australia and which country?**
- A. India
- B. New Zealand
- C. England ✓
- D. South Africa
- *Explanation:* The Ashes began in 1882 and remains cricket's oldest rivalry. The tiny urn said to hold the ashes stays at Lord's Cricket Ground in London.

**252. The name 'the Ashes' began in 1882 after a mock newspaper obituary mourned the death of what?**
- A. The Melbourne Cup
- B. Cricket balls
- C. The Sheffield Shield
- D. English cricket ✓
- *Explanation:* After Australia's first Test win in England, a newspaper joked that English cricket had died and its ashes would be taken to Australia. The name has been used ever since.

**253. The Australian Football League Grand Final is traditionally played at which ground?**
- A. The Melbourne Cricket Ground ✓
- B. The Gabba
- C. Adelaide Oval
- D. Optus Stadium
- *Explanation:* The MCG, holding more than 100,000 people, hosts the AFL Grand Final each spring. The ground also hosted the 1956 Olympic Games and the famous Boxing Day Test.

**254. Rugby league's State of Origin series is fiercely contested between New South Wales and which state?**
- A. Victoria
- B. Queensland ✓
- C. Western Australia
- D. South Australia
- *Explanation:* State of Origin, begun in 1980, pits the NSW Blues against the Queensland Maroons. It is famous for the intense loyalty of fans to the state where players first played senior football.

**255. The Australian Open, one of tennis's four Grand Slam tournaments, is held each January in which city?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Brisbane
- C. Melbourne ✓
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* The Australian Open has been played at Melbourne Park since 1988. Its main stadium is named Rod Laver Arena, after Australia's great champion.

**256. Phar Lap, who died mysteriously in 1932 after a racing career that lifted spirits during the Depression, was one of Australia's most famous what?**
- A. Cricketers
- B. Swimmers
- C. Explorers
- D. Racehorses ✓
- *Explanation:* Phar Lap won the Melbourne Cup in 1930 and became a national hero during the hard years of the Depression. His mounted hide is displayed at the Melbourne Museum.

**257. Swimmer Dawn Fraser won the 100 metres freestyle gold medal at how many consecutive Olympic Games?**
- A. Three — in 1956, 1960 and 1964 ✓
- B. One
- C. Two
- D. Five
- *Explanation:* Fraser was the first swimmer to win the same Olympic event three times in a row. She remains one of Australia's most loved sporting champions.

**258. Rod Laver, the only player to win all four Grand Slam singles titles in a single year twice, is a legend of which sport?**
- A. Cricket
- B. Tennis ✓
- C. Golf
- D. Boxing
- *Explanation:* Laver achieved the calendar Grand Slam in 1962 and again in 1969. The centre court at Melbourne Park bears his name.

**259. Australia is one of a very small number of countries that has done what at the Olympic Games?**
- A. Hosted every Games
- B. Competed only at home Games
- C. Competed at every modern Summer Olympic Games ✓
- D. Never won a medal before 1956
- *Explanation:* An Australian, Edwin Flack, won two gold medals at the very first modern Olympics in Athens in 1896. Australian teams have attended every Summer Games since.

**260. The famous Sydney to Hobart yacht race starts each Boxing Day from which city?**
- A. Melbourne
- B. Adelaide
- C. Hobart
- D. Sydney ✓
- *Explanation:* The fleet races some 630 nautical miles down the coast and across Bass Strait to Hobart. The race, first held in 1945, is a Boxing Day tradition.

**261. Vegemite, the dark savoury spread loved by Australians, was developed in Melbourne in which year?**
- A. 1923 ✓
- B. 1788
- C. 1901
- D. 1966
- *Explanation:* Food scientist Cyril Callister created Vegemite in 1923 from leftover brewers' yeast extract. It is rich in vitamin B and famously spread thin on toast.

**262. The Hills Hoist, the rotary clothesline found in generations of Australian backyards, was invented in Adelaide by whom?**
- A. Graeme Clark
- B. Lance Hill ✓
- C. David Warren
- D. Cyril Callister
- *Explanation:* Lance Hill began making his rotating clothes hoist in 1945. The Hills Hoist became such an icon that one was carried in the closing ceremony of the Sydney Olympics.

**263. The Australian 'ute' was designed in Geelong in 1934 after a letter from whom, asking for a vehicle that could go to church on Sunday and carry pigs to market on Monday?**
- A. A soldier
- B. A gold miner
- C. A farmer's wife ✓
- D. The prime minister
- *Explanation:* Ford designer Lew Bandt answered the famous request by creating the coupe utility. The ute became a beloved symbol of Australian farm and working life.

**264. The Indian Pacific passenger train makes one of the world's great rail journeys between Sydney and which city?**
- A. Darwin
- B. Melbourne
- C. Brisbane
- D. Perth ✓
- *Explanation:* The Indian Pacific crosses the continent from ocean to ocean, including the longest straight stretch of railway track in the world across the Nullarbor. The journey takes about three days.

**265. Victoria's Great Ocean Road, one of the world's most scenic coastal drives, was built mainly by whom?**
- A. Returned soldiers of the First World War ✓
- B. Convicts
- C. Gold miners
- D. Prisoners of war
- *Explanation:* Work began in 1919, giving jobs to thousands of returned servicemen. The road is regarded as a memorial to those who died in the war.

**266. The 2018 Commonwealth Games were hosted by which Australian city?**
- A. Melbourne
- B. The Gold Coast ✓
- C. Sydney
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* The Gold Coast Games of 2018 were the fifth Commonwealth Games held in Australia. Australian athletes topped the medal tally on home soil.

**267. The Australian national team known as the Diamonds competes in which sport?**
- A. Hockey
- B. Basketball
- C. Netball ✓
- D. Rugby union
- *Explanation:* The Diamonds are among the most successful netball teams in the world. Netball is one of the most widely played sports by women and girls in Australia.

**268. Ian Thorpe, nicknamed 'the Thorpedo', won five Olympic gold medals in which sport?**
- A. Athletics
- B. Cycling
- C. Rowing
- D. Swimming ✓
- *Explanation:* Thorpe, famous for his size-17 feet and smooth freestyle, starred at the Sydney 2000 and Athens 2004 Olympics. He is one of Australia's greatest swimmers.

**269. Melbourne's Formula 1 Grand Prix is raced on a circuit around which lake?**
- A. Albert Park Lake ✓
- B. Lake Burley Griffin
- C. Lake Eyre
- D. Lake Macquarie
- *Explanation:* The Albert Park circuit, just south of central Melbourne, has hosted the Australian Grand Prix since 1996. The race usually opens the Formula 1 season.

**270. In 1968, Lionel Rose became the first Aboriginal Australian to achieve what in sport?**
- A. Win an Olympic gold medal
- B. Win a world boxing title ✓
- C. Play Test cricket
- D. Win the Melbourne Cup as a jockey
- *Explanation:* Rose beat Japan's Fighting Harada in Tokyo to win the world bantamweight title. He was named Australian of the Year in 1968.

**271. Evonne Goolagong Cawley, who won Wimbledon twice, is a champion of which sport?**
- A. Swimming
- B. Athletics
- C. Tennis ✓
- D. Netball
- *Explanation:* Goolagong Cawley won seven Grand Slam singles titles in the 1970s. A proud Wiradjuri woman, she later founded programs helping Aboriginal children through tennis and education.

**272. The Socceroos are Australia's national team in which sport?**
- A. Rugby league
- B. Cricket
- C. Netball
- D. Soccer (football) ✓
- *Explanation:* The Socceroos first reached the World Cup finals in 1974 and famously qualified again in 2005 by beating Uruguay in a penalty shootout in Sydney.

**273. The Matildas are Australia's national team in which sport?**
- A. Women's soccer ✓
- B. Women's cricket
- C. Women's hockey
- D. Women's netball
- *Explanation:* The Matildas captivated the nation at the 2023 Women's World Cup, co-hosted by Australia and New Zealand. Their semi-final was one of the most watched events in Australian television history.

**274. Shane Warne, who took 708 Test wickets, is regarded as one of the greatest masters of which bowling skill?**
- A. Fast bowling
- B. Leg-spin bowling ✓
- C. Wicketkeeping
- D. Swing bowling
- *Explanation:* Warne revived the art of leg-spin in the 1990s. His first ball in an Ashes Test in 1993, which bowled Mike Gatting, is called the 'Ball of the Century'.

**275. The much-loved song 'I Still Call Australia Home' was written and made famous by which entertainer?**
- A. John Farnham
- B. Jimmy Barnes
- C. Peter Allen ✓
- D. Keith Urban
- *Explanation:* Peter Allen wrote the song in 1980, expressing the feelings of Australians living far from home. It has been widely used to welcome Australians back from overseas.

**276. The Australian Red Ensign, a red version of the national flag, is traditionally flown by which vessels?**
- A. Navy warships
- B. Air Force aircraft
- C. Parliament House
- D. Australian merchant ships ✓
- *Explanation:* Merchant ships registered in Australia fly the Red Ensign. The navy flies the separate Australian White Ensign, adopted in 1967.

**277. In which year were green and gold officially proclaimed to be Australia's national colours?**
- A. 1984 ✓
- B. 1901
- C. 1956
- D. 2000
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General proclaimed green and gold as the national colours on 19 April 1984. Australian sporting teams had worn the colours for many decades before that.

**278. The golden wattle was officially proclaimed Australia's national floral emblem in which year?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1988 ✓
- C. 1966
- D. 2001
- *Explanation:* The golden wattle was proclaimed the national floral emblem on 1 September 1988, during Australia's Bicentennial year. The date is now marked as Wattle Day.

**279. Australia's national anthem, 'Advance Australia Fair', was composed by whom?**
- A. Banjo Paterson
- B. Henry Lawson
- C. Peter Dodds McCormick ✓
- D. Slim Dusty
- *Explanation:* Scottish-born Peter Dodds McCormick wrote the words and music, and the song was first performed in Sydney in 1878. It became the national anthem in 1984, following a national vote.

**280. In 2021, the words of the national anthem were changed so that the first line now describes Australians as what?**
- A. Young and free
- B. Bold and free
- C. Rich and free
- D. One and free ✓
- *Explanation:* The change from 'young and free' to 'one and free' recognised that Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander cultures are the world's oldest living cultures. The change took effect on 1 January 2021.

**281. Wattle Day, celebrating Australia's national flower in bloom, is held each year on which date?**
- A. 1 September ✓
- B. 26 January
- C. 3 June
- D. 17 September
- *Explanation:* Wattle Day falls on the first day of spring, when golden wattle blooms across much of Australia. Sprigs of wattle are worn as a symbol of national pride.

**282. National Reconciliation Week, beginning on 27 May each year, ends on 3 June — a date known as what?**
- A. Australia Day
- B. Mabo Day ✓
- C. Anzac Day
- D. Flag Day
- *Explanation:* Reconciliation Week links the anniversary of the 1967 referendum (27 May) with Mabo Day (3 June). It is a time for all Australians to learn about shared histories and cultures.

**283. Mabo Day, on 3 June, marks the anniversary of which landmark event?**
- A. Federation in 1901
- B. The 1967 referendum
- C. The High Court's Mabo decision in 1992 ✓
- D. The national Apology in 2008
- *Explanation:* On 3 June 1992, the High Court decided that native title existed in Australia, overturning the idea that the land had belonged to no one in 1788. The case carried Eddie Mabo's name.

**284. In most Australian states, the King's Birthday public holiday is held in which month?**
- A. January
- B. April
- C. September
- D. June ✓
- *Explanation:* Most states mark the holiday in June, although Western Australia and Queensland hold it later in the year. The holiday tradition goes back to 1788, when Governor Phillip marked King George III's birthday.

**285. Albert Namatjira (1902-1959) became famous across Australia for what?**
- A. His watercolour paintings of Central Australian landscapes ✓
- B. Commanding troops in New Guinea
- C. His cricket career
- D. His solo flights
- *Explanation:* Namatjira, an Arrernte man from Hermannsburg, painted the ranges and ghost gums of the Centre in glowing watercolour. In 1957 he became the first Aboriginal person granted Australian citizenship rights under the laws of the time.

**286. David Unaipon, whose portrait appears on the $50 note, is honoured as what?**
- A. A famous bushranger
- B. An Aboriginal inventor, writer and preacher known as 'Australia's Leonardo' ✓
- C. An Olympic swimmer
- D. Australia's first prime minister
- *Explanation:* Unaipon, a Ngarrindjeri man, patented a shearing handpiece design in 1909 and wrote down traditional stories. He spent his life working for Aboriginal people's rights and recognition.

**287. Edith Cowan, whose portrait also appears on the $50 note, made history in 1921 as the first woman in Australia to do what?**
- A. Win an Olympic gold medal
- B. Fly a plane solo
- C. Be elected to a parliament ✓
- D. Become a doctor
- *Explanation:* Cowan was elected to the Western Australian Legislative Assembly in 1921. She worked tirelessly for the welfare of women and children.

**288. Caroline Chisholm devoted her life in the 1800s to helping whom?**
- A. Returned soldiers
- B. Explorers of the inland
- C. Opera singers
- D. Newly arrived migrants, especially women and families ✓
- *Explanation:* Known as 'the emigrants' friend', Chisholm met migrant ships at Sydney docks and found work and homes for thousands of newcomers. Her portrait appeared on the old five-dollar note.

**289. Mary Reibey, whose portrait is on the $20 note, arrived in the colony as a convict and went on to become what?**
- A. A successful Sydney businesswoman ✓
- B. A famous nurse
- C. A goldfields storekeeper only
- D. An author of bush ballads
- *Explanation:* Transported at 15 for stealing a horse, Reibey became a wealthy shipping and property owner in early Sydney. Her story is celebrated as one of determination and second chances.

**290. In 1971, Neville Bonner became the first Aboriginal Australian to do what?**
- A. Win a world sporting title
- B. Sit in the federal Parliament ✓
- C. Graduate from a university
- D. Play for the Socceroos
- *Explanation:* Bonner, a Queensland senator, entered the Senate in 1971 and served for twelve years. Charles Perkins had earlier become the first Aboriginal graduate of an Australian university, in 1966.

**291. Charles Perkins led the famous Freedom Ride of 1965 to protest against what?**
- A. The Vietnam War
- B. High taxes
- C. Discrimination against Aboriginal people in country towns ✓
- D. The building of dams
- *Explanation:* Perkins and fellow students travelled by bus through New South Wales towns, exposing bans and segregation faced by Aboriginal people. The ride drew national attention to racism in Australia.

**292. Dame Nellie Melba was a world-famous Australian in which art?**
- A. Ballet dancing
- B. Landscape painting
- C. Swimming
- D. Opera singing ✓
- *Explanation:* Melba, from Melbourne — her stage name came from her home city — was one of the most celebrated sopranos in the world. The dishes Peach Melba and Melba toast were named in her honour.

**293. Slim Dusty, whose recording career lasted more than 50 years, was a beloved singer of which style of music?**
- A. Country music ✓
- B. Opera
- C. Jazz
- D. Heavy metal
- *Explanation:* Slim Dusty's songs, including 'A Pub with No Beer', told stories of the bush, the road and everyday Australians. He recorded more than 100 albums.

**294. Sir Edward 'Weary' Dunlop is remembered for his devoted care of fellow prisoners of war while serving as what?**
- A. A fighter pilot
- B. An army surgeon ✓
- C. A chaplain
- D. A cook
- *Explanation:* Dunlop cared for sick and starving prisoners on the Thai-Burma railway during the Second World War. After the war he worked for veterans' welfare and Australia-Asia friendship.

**295. Nancy Wake, nicknamed 'the White Mouse', became famous during the Second World War for what?**
- A. Swimming the English Channel
- B. Writing bush poetry
- C. Working with the French Resistance against the German occupation ✓
- D. Flying planes across the Pacific
- *Explanation:* Wake, born in Wellington and raised in Sydney, became one of the most decorated Allied servicewomen of the war. The Gestapo called her the White Mouse because she always escaped their traps.

**296. Eye surgeon Fred Hollows dedicated his life's work to what?**
- A. Saving the Murray River
- B. Coaching Olympic swimmers
- C. Exploring Antarctica
- D. Restoring eyesight to people in poor communities, in Australia and overseas ✓
- *Explanation:* Hollows worked to end avoidable blindness, especially trachoma among Aboriginal communities, and trained surgeons in Vietnam, Eritrea and Nepal. The Fred Hollows Foundation continues his work.

**297. Sir Donald Bradman's Test batting average, still a world record by a vast margin, was what?**
- A. 99.94 ✓
- B. 50.50
- C. 75.25
- D. 120.00
- *Explanation:* Bradman needed only four runs in his final innings in 1948 to average 100, but was bowled for a duck. His average of 99.94 remains unmatched in Test cricket.

**298. The famous Australian slouch hat, worn by generations of soldiers, is known for having one side of its brim what?**
- A. Painted gold
- B. Turned up and pinned against the crown ✓
- C. Covered in badges
- D. Made of steel
- *Explanation:* The turned-up side allowed a rifle to be carried on the shoulder without knocking the hat. The hat is often decorated with the Rising Sun badge and an emu plume in some units.

**299. Why must businesses seek official permission before using the word 'Anzac' in Australia?**
- A. It is a private company trademark
- B. It belongs to New Zealand
- C. The word is protected by law out of respect for those who served ✓
- D. The word is banned in all circumstances
- *Explanation:* Since 1920, Australian law has restricted the use of the word 'Anzac' so that it is treated with dignity. Permission is given for respectful uses, such as Anzac biscuits sold for veterans' charities.

**300. How many stars appear in the Southern Cross on the Australian national flag?**
- A. Four
- B. Six
- C. Seven
- D. Five ✓
- *Explanation:* The Southern Cross on the flag has five stars: four with seven points and one smaller star with five points. The constellation can be seen throughout Australia all year round.

## Topic 2: Australia's democratic beliefs, rights and liberties

**1. Australia's system of government is best described as what?**
- A. A parliamentary democracy ✓
- B. A dictatorship
- C. An absolute monarchy
- D. A military government
- *Explanation:* Australia is a parliamentary democracy. The people elect representatives to parliaments, and the government is accountable to the people.

**2. In a parliamentary democracy, who decides who forms the government?**
- A. The King
- B. The people, by voting in elections ✓
- C. The army
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* In a democracy, citizens choose their representatives in free elections, and this decides who forms the government.

**3. What is the key idea of parliamentary democracy?**
- A. One party rules forever without elections
- B. Judges make all the laws on their own
- C. Elected representatives form a government that is accountable to the people ✓
- D. Religious leaders approve every law
- *Explanation:* Parliamentary democracy means elected representatives make laws and govern on behalf of the people, who can vote them out at the next election.

**4. If a government loses an election in Australia, what must happen?**
- A. It can refuse to leave office
- B. The army takes control
- C. A civil war decides the result
- D. It peacefully hands over power to the winning party ✓
- *Explanation:* Peaceful change of government is a core democratic value in Australia. A government that loses an election hands over power peacefully.

**5. What does it mean that Australia is a democracy?**
- A. Citizens have a say in how the country is governed, through free elections ✓
- B. The King governs Australia personally
- C. Only landowners may take part in public life
- D. There are no laws
- *Explanation:* Democracy means government by the people. In Australia, citizens take part by voting in free and fair elections.

**6. Which of these is a core democratic belief in Australia?**
- A. One leader should rule for life
- B. All citizens are equal and have the same rights ✓
- C. Only wealthy people should vote
- D. Newspapers should print only good news about leaders
- *Explanation:* Australian democracy is built on the belief that all citizens are equal and share the same rights and freedoms.

**7. In Australia, who makes the laws?**
- A. The police
- B. Judges, acting alone
- C. Parliaments elected by the people ✓
- D. Religious leaders
- *Explanation:* Laws are made by parliaments whose members are elected by the people. Courts interpret the laws, but do not invent them alone.

**8. In practice, 'government by the people' in Australia means what?**
- A. Every citizen votes on every law personally
- B. The Governor-General governs alone
- C. There is no government at all
- D. Citizens choose representatives to govern on their behalf ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia is a representative democracy: citizens elect members of parliament to govern and make laws on their behalf.

**9. Why are regular free elections important in a democracy?**
- A. They let the people change the government peacefully ✓
- B. They are mainly a form of entertainment
- C. They decide the outcome of court cases
- D. They choose Australia's religious leaders
- *Explanation:* Regular elections allow people to keep or change their government peacefully, without violence.

**10. If you disagree with a decision of the Australian Government, what can you do?**
- A. Use violence against officials
- B. Campaign, protest peacefully, or vote for change at the next election ✓
- C. Stop obeying every law
- D. Bribe an official to change it
- *Explanation:* In a democracy you may argue against a decision, protest peacefully within the law, and vote for change at the next election.

**11. What is a political party?**
- A. A birthday celebration
- B. A sporting club
- C. A group of people who share similar ideas about how the country should be governed ✓
- D. A department of the police
- *Explanation:* A political party is a group of people with shared ideas about governing. Parties put forward candidates at elections.

**12. Who may start or join a political party in Australia?**
- A. Only members of parliament
- B. Only very rich people
- C. Only men
- D. Any citizen ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association means any citizen may start or join a political party, or choose not to join one.

**13. What does 'the rule of law' mean in Australia?**
- A. Everyone, including leaders and the police, must obey the law ✓
- B. The King personally makes every law
- C. Laws apply only to new migrants
- D. Police may ignore the law when they are busy
- *Explanation:* The rule of law means the law applies to everyone equally — including governments, leaders and the police. No one is above the law.

**14. Under the rule of law, who is above the law in Australia?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. No one ✓
- C. Police officers
- D. Judges
- *Explanation:* No one is above the law in Australia. Even the Prime Minister and the police must obey the law.

**15. If a person is charged with a crime in Australia, the law treats them as what?**
- A. Guilty until they prove their innocence
- B. Guilty if the media reports it
- C. Innocent until proven guilty ✓
- D. Guilty if they cannot afford a lawyer
- *Explanation:* A person accused of a crime in Australia is innocent until proven guilty in a court.

**16. What helps to make sure that trials in Australia are fair?**
- A. Trials are held in secret with no lawyers
- B. The accused is not allowed to speak
- C. Judges must take orders from the government
- D. The courts are independent of the government ✓
- *Explanation:* Courts in Australia are independent. Judges decide cases according to the law and the evidence, not on government instructions.

**17. Who enforces the law from day to day in Australia?**
- A. The police ✓
- B. Political parties
- C. Newspaper editors
- D. Religious leaders
- *Explanation:* Police forces enforce the law day to day. Australian courts then decide whether the law has been broken.

**18. What must police officers in Australia do?**
- A. Make up new laws
- B. Obey the law themselves while they enforce it ✓
- C. Favour people they know
- D. Ask political parties who to arrest
- *Explanation:* Police must obey the law themselves. They cannot favour friends or take political directions about arrests.

**19. If someone in Australia thinks a law is unfair, what is the right way to seek change?**
- A. Break the law until it is changed
- B. Bribe an official
- C. Argue for change peacefully, through Parliament and elections ✓
- D. Use violence against officials
- *Explanation:* Change in a democracy comes peacefully: discussion, campaigning, and voting, with Parliament changing the law.

**20. What can people who break the law in Australia expect?**
- A. Nothing, if they are famous
- B. Punishment without any court hearing
- C. Automatic deportation, even for citizens
- D. Legal consequences decided through the courts ✓
- *Explanation:* People who break the law face legal consequences through independent courts — the same process for everyone.

**21. Why is it important that Australian courts are independent?**
- A. So decisions are made according to law, free from government pressure ✓
- B. So judges can run the country
- C. So that laws never change
- D. So trials need no evidence
- *Explanation:* Independent courts protect everyone's rights, because cases are decided by law and evidence, not by government pressure.

**22. Can an Australian government minister order a court to free a friend?**
- A. Yes, ministers control the courts
- B. No, courts are independent and ministers must obey the law ✓
- C. Yes, if the friend is wealthy
- D. Yes, with the help of the police
- *Explanation:* No one can interfere with a court case — not even a government minister. This is part of the rule of law.

**23. Which of these does the rule of law protect?**
- A. The right of officials to accept bribes
- B. Special laws for famous people
- C. Equal treatment of everyone under the law ✓
- D. Secret trials
- *Explanation:* The rule of law protects equal treatment: the same law, applied in the same way, to every person.

**24. A person accused of a serious crime in Australia has the right to what?**
- A. Be punished before the trial
- B. No hearing at all
- C. Choose their own punishment
- D. A fair hearing and legal representation ✓
- *Explanation:* An accused person has the right to a fair hearing and to be represented by a lawyer, and to be helped by an interpreter if needed.

**25. Australians believe that conflicts and disagreements should be settled by what?**
- A. Discussion and the law, not violence ✓
- B. Fighting
- C. Threats
- D. Refusing to ever compromise
- *Explanation:* Australians value peaceful behaviour. Disagreements are settled by discussion and through the law, never by violence.

**26. How is violence viewed in Australian society?**
- A. It is acceptable in politics
- B. It is against the law and not an accepted way to settle differences ✓
- C. It is fine inside the home
- D. It is expected between rival sports fans
- *Explanation:* Violence is against the law in Australia and is never an accepted way to settle a disagreement.

**27. If someone disagrees with your religious or political views, what should you do?**
- A. Threaten them until they agree
- B. Damage their property
- C. Respect their right to hold their own opinion ✓
- D. Report them to the police for disagreeing
- *Explanation:* Australians respect the right of others to hold different opinions, even when they strongly disagree with them.

**28. Australians believe in living in peace and security. What does this mean in daily life?**
- A. That no laws will ever be enforced
- B. That the army settles neighbourhood arguments
- C. That bad news is never reported
- D. That people can live without violence and settle problems peacefully ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia is a peaceful country. People settle their problems through discussion and the law, not violence.

**29. What is the Australian way to try to change something in society that you think is wrong?**
- A. Peaceful action — discussion, protest within the law, and voting ✓
- B. Violence against property
- C. Bribing officials
- D. Keeping your children out of school
- *Explanation:* In Australia, change is pursued peacefully: discussion, lawful protest, campaigning and voting.

**30. Freedom of speech in Australia means that people can do what?**
- A. Make threats of violence online
- B. Express their ideas and discuss them peacefully ✓
- C. Lie under oath in court
- D. Demand that newspapers print their opinions
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech means people can express their ideas and opinions and discuss them peacefully with others.

**31. Are Australians allowed to criticise the government?**
- A. Never
- B. Only during election week
- C. Yes — peaceful criticism of the government is part of democracy ✓
- D. Only journalists may criticise it
- *Explanation:* Yes. In Australia, people may freely and peacefully criticise the government. This is a strength of democracy.

**32. What does freedom of the press mean in Australia?**
- A. Newspapers can be shut down for criticising leaders
- B. The government writes all the news
- C. Only government media may publish news
- D. The media can report and comment freely, including criticising the government ✓
- *Explanation:* A free press can report the news and question those in power without government control.

**33. Which of these is an example of freedom of speech in action?**
- A. Peacefully sharing your political opinion with others ✓
- B. Threatening a politician
- C. Stopping a public meeting by force
- D. Forging an official document
- *Explanation:* Sharing your opinion peacefully — in conversation, writing or a public meeting — is freedom of speech in action.

**34. Freedom of expression in Australia includes the right to do what?**
- A. Break copyright whenever you like
- B. Express ideas through art, writing, music and peaceful discussion ✓
- C. Ignore the road rules as a form of expression
- D. Refuse to pay any tax
- *Explanation:* Freedom of expression covers peaceful creative and public expression — art, writing, music and discussion.

**35. If a newspaper publishes a false story that harms a person's reputation, what protects that person?**
- A. Nothing at all
- B. The right to hire people to threaten the editor
- C. Defamation laws, decided by independent courts ✓
- D. A rule that the newspaper must close forever
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech has legal limits. Defamation law lets a person whose reputation is harmed by false claims seek justice in court.

**36. Why does freedom of speech have some legal limits in Australia?**
- A. To keep leaders comfortable
- B. To keep debates short
- C. To stop new ideas from spreading
- D. To protect public safety and the rights and reputations of others ✓
- *Explanation:* Speech is limited where it harms others — for example threats, inciting violence, or false claims that damage a reputation.

**37. Freedom of association in Australia means people can do what?**
- A. Join groups, clubs, unions and political parties of their own choice ✓
- B. Be forced to join a political party
- C. Form armed gangs
- D. Stop other people from meeting
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association means people may freely form and join groups — clubs, unions, community groups and political parties.

**38. Can a worker in Australia choose not to join a trade union?**
- A. No, every worker must join a union
- B. Yes, joining a union is a free choice ✓
- C. Only public servants can choose
- D. Only men can choose
- *Explanation:* Joining a union is voluntary in Australia. Workers are free to join or not join.

**39. Which of these is an example of freedom of association?**
- A. A person being forced to join a club
- B. A law banning all community groups
- C. Workers forming a union to discuss pay and conditions ✓
- D. A gang blocking a public street
- *Explanation:* Forming or joining a union, club or community group is freedom of association in action.

**40. A peaceful protest in Australia is what?**
- A. Illegal in all cases
- B. Allowed only for people born in Australia
- C. Allowed only inside private homes
- D. A lawful way for people to express a point of view in public ✓
- *Explanation:* Peaceful protest is a lawful part of Australian democracy, as long as protesters obey the law.

**41. What must people taking part in a peaceful protest in Australia do?**
- A. Obey the law, for example by not damaging property ✓
- B. Carry weapons for protection
- C. Block emergency vehicles
- D. Ignore every police direction in all situations
- *Explanation:* Protesters must stay within the law — no violence and no damage to property — while expressing their views.

**42. Freedom of movement means Australian citizens can do what?**
- A. Enter any private home they like
- B. Travel freely and live anywhere in Australia ✓
- C. Ignore the border laws of other countries
- D. Drive any car without a licence
- *Explanation:* Citizens are free to travel around Australia and to live and work in any state or territory.

**43. Which group of freedoms are core freedoms in Australia?**
- A. Speech, speeding and smuggling
- B. Speech, but only for celebrities
- C. Speech, association and religion ✓
- D. Australia does not recognise freedoms
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech, freedom of association and freedom of religion are core freedoms valued in Australia.

**44. If you strongly dislike the views of a public speaker, what is the democratic response?**
- A. Attack the speaker
- B. Burn the speaker's books
- C. Have the speaker arrested for their opinion
- D. Let them speak, and answer with your own peaceful arguments ✓
- *Explanation:* Democracy protects even unpopular opinions. The right response is peaceful argument, not force or censorship.

**45. Why does Australia have no official national religion?**
- A. Because the government is secular and treats all religions equally ✓
- B. Because religion is banned in Australia
- C. Because one religion is secretly official
- D. Because Australians follow no religions
- *Explanation:* Australia's government is secular. There is no national religion, and people of all religions are treated equally.

**46. In Australia, what is the position of religious rules and laws?**
- A. They override Australian law
- B. They are not imposed by the government; everyone must follow Australian law ✓
- C. They are used by courts instead of Australian law
- D. They replace the rules of schools
- *Explanation:* Governments in Australia do not impose religious law. Australian law applies equally to everyone.

**47. Can a person in Australia choose not to follow any religion?**
- A. No, everyone must choose a religion
- B. Only after the age of 60
- C. Yes — freedom of religion includes the freedom to follow no religion ✓
- D. Only people born overseas
- *Explanation:* Yes. Freedom of religion includes the right to follow a religion, to change religion, or to follow no religion at all.

**48. How does the Australian Government treat people of different religions?**
- A. It runs all places of worship
- B. It pays people to change religion
- C. It bans religious schools
- D. It treats all people equally, whatever their religion ✓
- *Explanation:* The government treats all people equally regardless of religion. No religion receives special power in government.

**49. A person in Australia is free to do what?**
- A. Practise their religion and customs, as long as they obey Australian law ✓
- B. Break Australian law for religious reasons
- C. Force neighbours to join their religion
- D. Stop other people from worshipping
- *Explanation:* People may practise their religion and cultural customs freely, provided they follow Australian law like everyone else.

**50. Which statement about religion in Australia is correct?**
- A. One religion is the official state religion
- B. People of many religions, and of no religion, live together and are equal under the law ✓
- C. Religious clothing is banned in public
- D. Only Christians may become citizens
- *Explanation:* Australians follow many different religions, and many follow none. All are equal under Australian law.

**51. If a religious custom or practice conflicts with Australian law, what must happen?**
- A. The religious practice always wins
- B. The person must leave Australia immediately
- C. Australian law must be obeyed ✓
- D. The police ignore the law in every such case
- *Explanation:* In Australia, everyone must obey Australian law, even if a custom or religious practice is different. The law comes first.

**52. Freedom of religion in Australia protects whom?**
- A. Only the largest religion
- B. Only religions founded in Australia
- C. Only people born in Australia
- D. Everyone — the right to worship, or not to worship ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion protects everyone in Australia, whatever they believe.

**53. Can religious groups in Australia run schools, hospitals and charities?**
- A. Yes, like other community organisations, within the law ✓
- B. No, never
- C. Only one religious group may do so
- D. Only with the personal permission of the Prime Minister
- *Explanation:* Yes. Religious and community organisations run many schools, hospitals and charities in Australia, under Australian law.

**54. What does it mean that Australia's government is 'secular'?**
- A. Religion is against the law
- B. Government and religion are separate; there is no official religion ✓
- C. Only religious people may vote
- D. Churches write the laws
- *Explanation:* A secular government keeps government and religion separate. Australia has no official religion.

**55. What does 'equality' mean in Australian democracy?**
- A. Everyone must earn exactly the same wage
- B. Everyone must think the same way
- C. Everyone is treated equally under the law and has the same basic rights ✓
- D. Everyone must do the same job
- *Explanation:* Equality means every person has the same basic rights and is treated equally under the law.

**56. In Australia, what is the position of men and women?**
- A. They have different voting rights
- B. They cannot do the same jobs
- C. They are educated separately by law
- D. They have equal rights and opportunities ✓
- *Explanation:* Men and women have equal rights in Australia — in voting, work, education and public life.

**57. Can a woman in Australia freely choose her own education and career?**
- A. Yes — women have the same rights as men ✓
- B. Only with her father's permission
- C. Only in some states
- D. No
- *Explanation:* Yes. Women in Australia can study, work and build careers on an equal basis with men.

**58. Who may stand for election to the Australian Parliament?**
- A. Only men
- B. Any eligible Australian citizen, woman or man ✓
- C. Only people born in Australia
- D. Only members of one party
- *Explanation:* Any eligible Australian citizen may nominate for election — women and men equally.

**59. Does Australia have a class system that fixes where a person belongs in society?**
- A. Yes, decided by birth
- B. Yes, decided by wealth
- C. No — people can succeed through talent and hard work, whatever their background ✓
- D. Only in small country towns
- *Explanation:* Australia has no class system. A person's background does not fix their future; success comes from talent and effort.

**60. What does 'equality of opportunity' mean?**
- A. Everyone is given the same result
- B. Jobs always go to the oldest applicant
- C. Only local people get opportunities
- D. Everyone has a fair chance to succeed, based on ability and effort ✓
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity means everyone gets a fair chance — outcomes depend on ability and effort, not background.

**61. When a woman and a man do the same work in Australia, what does the law expect?**
- A. Equal treatment, including fair pay for equal work ✓
- B. The woman must be paid less
- C. The man must be paid double
- D. Only one of them is paid
- *Explanation:* Australian law requires equal treatment at work. Paying a woman less than a man for the same work is against the law.

**62. Can girls in Australia attend school and university on the same basis as boys?**
- A. No
- B. Yes — education is open equally to girls and boys ✓
- C. Only primary school
- D. Only at private schools
- *Explanation:* Yes. Education in Australia is open to everyone, and girls and boys have the same right to learn.

**63. What does Australian society believe about a person's background?**
- A. It should decide their whole life
- B. Only certain races can succeed
- C. People should not be held back by their race, gender, religion or background ✓
- D. Migrants can never succeed
- *Explanation:* Australians believe no one should be held back by race, gender, religion or background. Everyone deserves a fair chance.

**64. A migrant who becomes an Australian citizen has what rights?**
- A. Fewer rights than other citizens
- B. Full rights only after 20 years
- C. No right to vote
- D. The same rights and responsibilities as every other citizen ✓
- *Explanation:* Citizenship is equal. A migrant who becomes a citizen has exactly the same rights and responsibilities as any other citizen.

**65. Two people apply for the same job. On what should the decision be based?**
- A. Their skills and merit ✓
- B. Who has the richer family
- C. A man should always be chosen first
- D. Whoever lives closest to the office
- *Explanation:* Jobs should be decided on merit — the skills and ability of each applicant — not on background, gender or connections.

**66. Treating a person unfairly because of their race or religion is what?**
- A. Encouraged in Australia
- B. Against Australian values and, in many cases, against the law ✓
- C. Acceptable in private business
- D. Acceptable at school
- *Explanation:* Discrimination based on race or religion is against Australian values and is unlawful in many situations.

**67. Which of these is an example of equality in practice?**
- A. Paying women less for the same work
- B. Refusing service to one ethnic group
- C. Giving every qualified applicant a fair chance at a job ✓
- D. Interviewing only people born locally
- *Explanation:* Giving every qualified person a fair chance is equality in practice.

**68. In Australia, the law applies equally to whom?**
- A. Only citizens
- B. Only permanent residents
- C. Only visitors
- D. Everyone in Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian law applies to everyone in Australia — citizens, residents and visitors alike.

**69. What does the Australian idea of 'a fair go' mean?**
- A. Everyone deserves a fair chance to succeed in life ✓
- B. Only sporting stars deserve a chance
- C. Luck decides everything
- D. Jobs should go to your mates first
- *Explanation:* 'A fair go' means everyone should have a fair opportunity to succeed, whatever their background.

**70. Under the idea of 'a fair go', what should mainly decide how far a person gets in life?**
- A. Their family name
- B. Their talents, work and effort ✓
- C. Their religion
- D. The country their grandparents came from
- *Explanation:* In Australia, what you achieve should depend on your talents, work and effort — not on family, wealth or background.

**71. Which of these is an example of 'a fair go'?**
- A. A job quietly given to the boss's relative without interviews
- B. A scholarship that only one family may receive
- C. A job advertised openly, so that anyone qualified can apply ✓
- D. A university place obtained with a bribe
- *Explanation:* Open competition on merit — like a publicly advertised job — is the 'fair go' in action.

**72. What does Australian society expect about success in life?**
- A. It is guaranteed to everyone
- B. It depends only on being born lucky
- C. It is shameful to succeed
- D. It should come from ability and hard work, not from gender or inherited wealth ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians respect success earned through ability and hard work. Gender, race or inherited position should not decide it.

**73. Respecting other people in Australia includes what?**
- A. Listening to others, even when you disagree with them ✓
- B. Agreeing with everyone about everything
- C. Ignoring anyone who is different
- D. Respecting only people with power
- *Explanation:* Respect means listening to others and treating them decently, even when you disagree with their views.

**74. What does 'mutual respect' mean as an Australian value?**
- A. Respecting only people from your own group
- B. Treating others as you would like to be treated, even when you disagree ✓
- C. Never discussing difficult topics
- D. Always letting others decide for you
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect means treating others the way you would like to be treated — with tolerance, even in disagreement.

**75. A new Australian has a custom that is different but does not break Australian law. How is this generally viewed?**
- A. It should be banned at once
- B. It should be mocked in public
- C. People's cultural differences are respected, within Australian law ✓
- D. The person should be avoided completely
- *Explanation:* Australia is a multicultural society. Different customs are respected as long as they follow Australian law.

**76. Australians value a society in which what happens?**
- A. One group rules over all the others
- B. Differences between people cause fear
- C. Migrants must hide their culture completely
- D. People of many backgrounds live together peacefully and respectfully ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia is a successful multicultural society where people of many backgrounds live together peacefully and respectfully.

**77. What does 'tolerance' mean in everyday Australian life?**
- A. Accepting that others may think and live differently, within the law ✓
- B. Agreeing with every idea you hear
- C. Never speaking to strangers
- D. Copying everyone else's lifestyle
- *Explanation:* Tolerance means accepting differences — other people's beliefs, customs and ways of life — within the law.

**78. In an Australian workplace, what is expected of you?**
- A. Only help people from your own background
- B. Treat workmates fairly and with respect ✓
- C. Refuse instructions from a woman manager
- D. Keep all your skills secret from others
- *Explanation:* Everyone at work deserves fair and respectful treatment, whatever their background, gender or position.

**79. Which of these is a way citizens support democracy in Australia?**
- A. Refusing to vote in every election
- B. Spreading false rumours about elections
- C. Voting in elections and taking an interest in public issues ✓
- D. Threatening election candidates
- *Explanation:* Democracy is stronger when citizens vote, stay informed and take part in community life.

**80. Why is it important that citizens can vote freely, without fear?**
- A. So that counting is quicker
- B. So parties can save money
- C. So elections finish earlier
- D. So the result truly reflects the people's free choice ✓
- *Explanation:* Free voting without pressure or fear means election results genuinely reflect the people's choice.

**81. What is special about the secret ballot used in Australian elections?**
- A. Your vote is private, so no one can pressure you about how you voted ✓
- B. All votes are read out in public
- C. Only officials ever see election results
- D. You must tell your employer how you voted
- *Explanation:* Voting in Australia is secret. Nobody — not family, employers or officials — can see how you voted.

**82. The secret voting system developed in Australia in the 1850s became known around the world as what?**
- A. The Melbourne method
- B. The Australian ballot ✓
- C. The Sydney system
- D. The colonial count
- *Explanation:* The secret ballot was pioneered in Victoria in 1856 and was copied around the world as 'the Australian ballot'.

**83. When you vote in an Australian election, who is allowed to watch how you mark your ballot paper?**
- A. Your employer
- B. Officials of a political party
- C. No one — you vote in private ✓
- D. Your whole family
- *Explanation:* You mark your ballot paper in a private booth. No one may watch or check your choices.

**84. How can an ordinary Australian citizen try to become a member of parliament?**
- A. Only by inheriting a seat
- B. Only if party leaders appoint them
- C. Only judges may nominate people
- D. Any eligible citizen may nominate as a candidate at an election ✓
- *Explanation:* Any eligible citizen can stand for election. Voters then decide who represents them.

**85. Independent candidates — people who are not members of a political party — in Australia may do what?**
- A. Stand for election, just like party candidates ✓
- B. Never stand
- C. Only watch elections
- D. Seek the King's personal permission first
- *Explanation:* Independent candidates can stand for election on the same basis as party candidates.

**86. How does a free press help protect democracy?**
- A. It must always praise the government
- B. It can question and criticise those in power, keeping them accountable ✓
- C. It exists only to sell advertising
- D. It reports only on celebrities
- *Explanation:* A free press questions governments and reports wrongdoing, which keeps leaders accountable to the people.

**87. If you believe a public official is corrupt, what can you do in Australia?**
- A. Attack the official in the street
- B. Stay silent forever
- C. Report it to the police or an integrity body, and speak about it peacefully ✓
- D. Offer the official a bribe to stop
- *Explanation:* Corruption can be reported to police and integrity agencies. Speaking up peacefully is protected in a democracy.

**88. What is the role of open debate in Australian democracy?**
- A. Debate is banned in Parliament
- B. Debate is a waste of time
- C. Only experts are allowed to debate
- D. Different ideas can be discussed and tested before decisions are made ✓
- *Explanation:* Open debate is how democracies test ideas. It happens in Parliament, the media and the community.

**89. Can Australians peacefully campaign to change the Constitution?**
- A. Yes — they can argue their case before the people vote in a referendum ✓
- B. No — discussing the Constitution is banned
- C. Only the government may discuss it
- D. Only in secret meetings
- *Explanation:* Yes. Before a referendum, everyone may campaign for or against the change, and the people decide.

**90. Which of these is a democratic right in Australia?**
- A. The right to stop other people from voting
- B. The right to vote in free and fair elections ✓
- C. The right to ignore court orders
- D. The right to be given a government job
- *Explanation:* Voting in free and fair elections is a fundamental democratic right — and also a responsibility — of citizens.

**91. Australians believe their government should be what?**
- A. In control of everyone's opinions
- B. Above the law
- C. Accountable to the people, through elections and the law ✓
- D. Never criticised by anyone
- *Explanation:* Governments in Australia are accountable: they answer to Parliament, to the courts, and to the people at elections.

**92. What does it mean that government power in Australia is limited?**
- A. Leaders can do anything once elected
- B. Courts can never review government actions
- C. The police answer to no one
- D. Governments may only use the powers given to them by the Constitution and the laws ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian governments have only the powers the Constitution and laws give them. Courts can stop governments acting beyond those powers.

**93. Why do democracies protect the rights of minority groups?**
- A. So majority rule cannot take away the basic rights of smaller groups ✓
- B. So minorities can rule instead
- C. So elections are unnecessary
- D. So laws apply to no one
- *Explanation:* Democracy is not just majority rule. The basic rights of minorities are protected by law and by the courts.

**94. If most people dislike a new law, how can it democratically be changed?**
- A. By rioting in the streets
- B. By peaceful campaigning and voting for change at the next election ✓
- C. By refusing to send children to school
- D. By refusing to pay tax forever
- *Explanation:* Unpopular laws are changed peacefully — through public debate, Parliament, and elections.

**95. Which statement best sums up Australia's democratic beliefs?**
- A. Might makes right
- B. Leaders are born, not chosen
- C. Government by the people, under the rule of law, with freedom and equality for all ✓
- D. Only the majority group has rights
- *Explanation:* Australia's beliefs combine democracy, the rule of law, freedom and equality for everyone.

**96. If an Australian citizen disagrees with a decision of a court, what can they do?**
- A. Attack the judge
- B. Nothing can ever be done
- C. Leave Australia at once
- D. Use the legal appeal process to have the decision reviewed ✓
- *Explanation:* Court decisions can be appealed to a higher court through the legal process. Disagreeing never justifies attacking anyone.

**97. Why should citizens accept the result of a fair election, even if their side lost?**
- A. Accepting peaceful change of government keeps democracy stable ✓
- B. Because protest is a crime
- C. Because the winners are always right
- D. Because voting is just a ceremony
- *Explanation:* Accepting election results peacefully is essential to democracy. There will always be another election.

**98. Freedom of speech in Australia does NOT give anyone the legal right to do what?**
- A. Criticise the government
- B. Incite violence against others ✓
- C. Draw a satirical cartoon of a politician
- D. Argue that a law should change
- *Explanation:* Speech that incites violence is not protected. Peaceful criticism, satire and argument all are.

**99. Which of these people is exercising a democratic freedom?**
- A. A person threatening voters at a polling place
- B. An official hiding public information for personal gain
- C. A resident writing to a newspaper criticising a council decision ✓
- D. A driver ignoring all road rules
- *Explanation:* Writing to criticise a government decision is freedom of speech and of the press in action.

**100. The Australian idea of freedom means people should be free to do what?**
- A. Harm others without consequences
- B. Break any law they dislike
- C. Stop other people from speaking
- D. Make their own choices about work, beliefs and how they live, within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom means making your own choices — about work, faith, family and life — while respecting the law and others' rights.

**101. A local council plans to close a public library. Residents who disagree with the plan can lawfully do what?**
- A. Speak at a council meeting, start a petition and campaign peacefully against the closure ✓
- B. Damage the library so it cannot be used
- C. Refuse to pay any council rates ever again
- D. Threaten the mayor's family until the plan stops
- *Explanation:* In a democracy, people may speak up, petition and campaign peacefully to influence decisions. Violence, threats and damaging property are against the law.

**102. A group of parents wants a new pedestrian crossing near their school, so they write to their local member of parliament. Why is this a democratic action?**
- A. Members of parliament personally build everything voters request
- B. Elected representatives are there to listen to the people they represent and raise their concerns ✓
- C. Writing to a politician is the only vote that counts
- D. The crossing will be built because the parents know a celebrity
- *Explanation:* Citizens can contact their elected representatives about local concerns. Representatives listen and raise issues, though one letter does not guarantee an outcome.

**103. At a public meeting about a new road, everyone is given a chance to speak, including people who support the plan and people who oppose it. What democratic idea does this show?**
- A. Only experts may discuss public plans
- B. Meetings must always end in an argument
- C. Open debate — different ideas can be heard before a decision is made ✓
- D. The loudest voice must always win
- *Explanation:* Open debate is central to democracy. Decisions are better when different views are heard and tested in public discussion.

**104. A candidate loses a local election by a small number of votes. What is the democratic thing for the candidate to do?**
- A. Refuse to accept that the election happened
- B. Tell supporters to block the council offices
- C. Leave Australia immediately
- D. Accept the result peacefully and, if they wish, stand again at the next election ✓
- *Explanation:* Accepting election results peacefully keeps democracy stable. There is always another election at which the people can decide again.

**105. Australians can attend meetings of their local council, and many council decisions are published for everyone to read. Why?**
- A. Government should be open and accountable to the people it serves ✓
- B. So residents can choose the mayor's holiday dates
- C. Because councils have nothing else to do
- D. So that no one ever disagrees again
- *Explanation:* Openness helps keep governments accountable. When people can see how decisions are made, they can question them and vote accordingly.

**106. A resident starts an online petition about saving a local park, and thousands of people sign it. What has the resident used?**
- A. The power to make a new law personally
- B. The democratic right to petition and to campaign peacefully for change ✓
- C. A rule that petitions always succeed
- D. The right to close the park to everyone else
- *Explanation:* Petitioning is a peaceful democratic right. A petition shows support for an idea, but elected bodies still make the final decision.

**107. Before a referendum, two neighbours campaign for opposite sides. One puts a 'Yes' sign in her window; the other hands out 'No' leaflets. What does this show?**
- A. Neighbours must always agree in public
- B. Leaflets are banned during referendums
- C. Everyone is free to campaign peacefully for the side they support ✓
- D. Only the government may discuss a referendum
- *Explanation:* Before a referendum, all Australians may argue for or against the change. The people then decide by voting.

**108. A community group is unhappy about bus services in their suburb. They invite the transport minister to a public meeting to hear their concerns. This is an example of what?**
- A. Breaking the law politely
- B. A group trying to run the buses themselves
- C. Forcing the minister to resign on the spot
- D. Citizens questioning their government peacefully and directly ✓
- *Explanation:* Ministers are accountable to the people. Meeting citizens and answering their questions is part of democratic government.

**109. A young citizen turns 18 and enrols to vote for the first time. Enrolling and voting in Australia is best described as what?**
- A. Both a right and a responsibility of citizens ✓
- B. A favour the government grants to some people
- C. Something only people with a university degree may do
- D. A private hobby with no effect on the country
- *Explanation:* Voting is a fundamental right of citizens and also a responsibility. Through elections, citizens choose who governs them.

**110. Election day is over and a friend says, 'My side lost, so democracy has failed.' What is the best answer?**
- A. Democracy only works when your side wins
- B. Democracy is working — the people have chosen, and there will be another election ✓
- C. The losing side should overturn the result by force
- D. Elections should be cancelled from now on
- *Explanation:* Democracy means accepting results you dislike. Parties that lose can campaign and try to win the people's support next time.

**111. A local newspaper reports that many residents disagree with a council plan. The mayor responds by explaining the plan at a public forum. What does this show?**
- A. Mayors must never explain themselves
- B. Newspapers are part of the council
- C. Leaders in a democracy answer questions and explain their decisions to the people ✓
- D. Public forums can change laws by shouting
- *Explanation:* Accountable leaders explain their decisions and face public questions. A free press and public discussion help make this happen.

**112. People in a country town want the speed limit on their main street lowered. Which path follows Australian democratic beliefs?**
- A. Block the street with cars until drivers slow down
- B. Paint their own speed signs and fine drivers
- C. Threaten the local police officer
- D. Raise it with the council and their elected representatives, and campaign peacefully for the change ✓
- *Explanation:* Change comes through peaceful action: raising concerns with elected representatives, discussion, and campaigning within the law.

**113. What is the point of community consultation, where governments ask residents for their views before making a big decision?**
- A. So the people affected can be heard before decisions are made ✓
- B. So the government can avoid deciding anything
- C. So only the richest residents are heard
- D. So decisions can be kept secret
- *Explanation:* Consultation lets citizens have their say on decisions that affect them. Final decisions rest with the elected government or council.

**114. A losing candidate at an election gives a speech congratulating the winner and thanking the voters. This tradition shows what?**
- A. Losing candidates must leave politics forever
- B. Peaceful acceptance of the people's decision ✓
- C. The winner was chosen by the loser
- D. Speeches change election results
- *Explanation:* Congratulating the winner is part of Australia's peaceful democratic culture. Power changes hands without violence.

**115. A student asks why Australia does not let one popular leader simply make all the decisions. What is the best answer?**
- A. Leaders enjoy sharing decisions as a hobby
- B. Parliament House is too big for one person
- C. Power is shared and limited so that no one person can rule without the people's consent ✓
- D. One person would find the work too tiring
- *Explanation:* In a parliamentary democracy, power is limited by the Constitution, parliaments and elections. No leader rules alone or forever.

**116. Residents are invited to make written submissions to a parliamentary inquiry into health services. Making a submission is an example of what?**
- A. Applying for a job in Parliament
- B. Voting early in an election
- C. Breaking parliamentary rules in writing
- D. Citizens having their say in how the country is governed ✓
- *Explanation:* Any citizen may contribute views to parliamentary inquiries. It is one of the ways ordinary people take part in democracy between elections.

**117. If a government in Australia becomes very unpopular, how will it most likely be removed?**
- A. The people vote it out at the next election ✓
- B. The army removes it
- C. Judges choose a new government
- D. It can never be removed once it is elected
- *Explanation:* Governments hold power only while they keep the support of the people's representatives — and ultimately of the voters at elections.

**118. A charity holds a peaceful street stall asking passers-by to sign a petition to Parliament. A passer-by does not agree with the petition and simply walks past. What does this scene show?**
- A. Petitions are illegal in public places
- B. People are free to campaign, and others are free not to join in ✓
- C. The passer-by must sign every petition offered
- D. The charity can force people to stop and listen
- *Explanation:* Freedom works both ways: people may campaign peacefully, and others may freely choose whether to take part.

**119. A local member of parliament holds regular meetings where anyone in the electorate can raise a problem. Why do representatives do this?**
- A. To choose which voters they like best
- B. To avoid ever going to Parliament
- C. Representatives serve the people who elected them and should hear their concerns ✓
- D. To tell voters how they must vote next time
- *Explanation:* Members of parliament represent everyone in their electorate. Listening to local concerns is part of their democratic role.

**120. A talkback radio host strongly criticises a new government policy, and callers ring in to argue both for and against it. This is an example of what?**
- A. A government controlling the media
- B. A crime called arguing in public
- C. Voters being told what to think
- D. Free public debate in a democracy ✓
- *Explanation:* Open debate in the media is a strength of democracy. People hear different views and make up their own minds.

**121. A community group believes a planning decision was unfair. They organise a lawful rally, speak to journalists and collect signatures. All of this is best described as what?**
- A. Peaceful democratic participation ✓
- B. Taking over the government
- C. Breaking the law in an organised way
- D. A court case
- *Explanation:* Rallies, media discussion and petitions are lawful ways to take part in democracy, as long as people obey the law.

**122. Two friends disagree about who should win the next election. They argue over coffee, then agree to disagree. What belief are they living by?**
- A. Friends must always vote the same way
- B. People can hold different political opinions and still respect each other ✓
- C. Politics must never be discussed between friends
- D. One of them must stop being a citizen
- *Explanation:* Democracy depends on people holding different views peacefully. Respect for other people's opinions is a core Australian belief.

**123. Why do Australian parliaments have an Opposition that questions the government on sitting days?**
- A. To make Parliament finish later
- B. To entertain television viewers
- C. Questioning the government keeps it accountable to the people ✓
- D. So the Opposition can make laws on its own
- *Explanation:* The Opposition tests the government's ideas and actions in public. This accountability is a key part of parliamentary democracy.

**124. A new migrant asks, 'In Australia, can ordinary people like me really influence decisions?' What is the correct answer?**
- A. No — only people born in Australia can
- B. No — only the very rich are heard
- C. Only if you personally know a minister
- D. Yes — citizens can vote, campaign, petition, join parties and speak up peacefully ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian democracy gives every citizen the same ways to be heard: voting, joining parties, petitioning, campaigning and peaceful protest.

**125. A council approves a controversial plan by one vote after a long public debate in which hundreds of residents spoke. Those who lost the argument accept the decision and plan to campaign at the next council election. This shows what?**
- A. Democratic decisions are accepted peacefully, and change is pursued through elections ✓
- B. Debate is pointless because someone always loses
- C. Councils must keep debating until everyone agrees
- D. The losing side should ignore the decision completely
- *Explanation:* In a democracy, decisions are made after debate and voting, and are accepted peacefully. Campaigning continues at the next election.

**126. A famous sports star is caught speeding on a public road. What should happen under the rule of law?**
- A. Nothing — fame protects her from fines
- B. She is treated the same as anyone else who speeds ✓
- C. Her club decides whether she is punished
- D. Fans vote on whether she pays the fine
- *Explanation:* The rule of law means the law applies equally to everyone — famous or unknown, rich or poor.

**127. A mayor is found to have broken a planning law when building an extension to his own house. What does the rule of law require?**
- A. The matter is forgotten because he is the mayor
- B. Council staff hide the files
- C. He faces the same legal consequences as any other resident ✓
- D. Residents forgive him at a community party
- *Explanation:* No one is above the law in Australia, including elected officials. Leaders who break the law face the same process as everyone else.

**128. A police officer drives through a red light while off duty and not responding to any emergency. What is the correct position?**
- A. Police officers are never fined
- B. She can fine another driver instead
- C. Her uniform makes running the light legal
- D. She must obey the road laws like everyone else and can be fined ✓
- *Explanation:* Police enforce the law, but they must also obey it. A uniform gives no exemption from ordinary laws.

**129. A wealthy businessman offers a council officer money to approve his building application ahead of everyone else's. What is this, and what must the officer do?**
- A. It is bribery, a serious crime — the officer must refuse it and report it ✓
- B. It is a normal tip — the officer may accept it
- C. It is legal if nobody sees it happen
- D. It is the officer's private bonus
- *Explanation:* Bribery is a serious crime in Australia. Public officials must act honestly and treat everyone equally.

**130. A recently arrived migrant breaks an Australian law that she honestly did not know about. What is the correct position?**
- A. She is excused from all Australian laws for her first year
- B. The law still applies to her, like everyone in Australia — people are expected to learn and obey Australian laws ✓
- C. Only citizens can break laws
- D. Her community leaders decide her punishment
- *Explanation:* Australian law applies to everyone in Australia. Not knowing a law does not remove it, which is why new arrivals are encouraged to learn Australian laws.

**131. A tourist visiting Australia is caught shoplifting from a shop. Which statement is correct?**
- A. Tourists cannot be charged with crimes
- B. The tourist's embassy decides the case
- C. The tourist can be charged and faces the same court process as a resident ✓
- D. The shop must simply let the tourist go
- *Explanation:* Everyone in Australia — citizens, residents and visitors — must obey Australian law and faces the same legal process.

**132. A large company is found to have polluted a river, breaking environmental laws. What should follow?**
- A. Nothing, because the company employs many people
- B. A warning only, because companies are special
- C. The company chooses its own punishment
- D. Legal consequences through the courts, just as for an individual who breaks the law ✓
- *Explanation:* The rule of law applies to organisations as well as to people. Courts decide consequences according to the law and the evidence.

**133. A driver receives a parking fine that she believes is wrong. What is the proper thing for her to do?**
- A. Use the official review process, or challenge the fine in court ✓
- B. Tear up the fine and ignore all letters about it
- C. Offer the parking officer money to cancel it
- D. Park illegally again in protest
- *Explanation:* People who believe a fine is wrong can challenge it through official review or the courts. Everyone has the right to a fair hearing.

**134. Police suspect a man of stealing from a warehouse, but they have very little evidence. Under Australian law, what can the police do?**
- A. Declare him guilty on television
- B. Investigate and gather evidence — only a court can decide whether he is guilty ✓
- C. Punish him themselves to save time
- D. Arrest his whole family instead
- *Explanation:* Police investigate and courts decide guilt. A person is innocent until proven guilty by a court, based on evidence.

**135. A politician is accused of misusing public money. Who decides whether she broke the law?**
- A. Her political party
- B. The newspapers
- C. The courts, after a fair process based on evidence ✓
- D. Her supporters, at a rally
- *Explanation:* Allegations against anyone — including politicians — are tested in independent courts, not decided by parties, the media or crowds.

**136. Why are Australian laws written down and published for everyone to read?**
- A. So lawyers can charge more to explain them
- B. So police can change them when they wish
- C. So that only judges know what they say
- D. So everyone can know what the law requires, and the law is applied openly and fairly ✓
- *Explanation:* Open, published laws are part of the rule of law. People can know their rights and duties, and officials must apply the law as written.

**137. A judge's own son is charged with an offence, and the case is listed in the judge's court. What does a fair legal system require?**
- A. The case is heard by a different judge, so the decision is fair and is seen to be fair ✓
- B. The judge quietly drops the charges
- C. The son is found guilty automatically, to prove fairness
- D. The judge asks the family what to decide
- *Explanation:* Courts must be independent and impartial. A judge cannot decide a case involving her own family; another judge hears it instead.

**138. A state premier is unhappy with a court decision against her government. What can she lawfully do?**
- A. Order the judge to change the decision
- B. Appeal to a higher court, or ask Parliament to change the law for the future ✓
- C. Refuse to let the court sit again
- D. Fine the judge personally
- *Explanation:* Governments cannot order courts around. They may appeal decisions, or seek to change laws through Parliament, within the Constitution.

**139. A man is arrested for a serious offence late on a Friday night. What protects him from simply being locked away without any process?**
- A. Nothing — police may hold people for as long as they like
- B. His personal wealth
- C. The law requires that he be charged and brought before a court, where the process is fair ✓
- D. His friends can demand his release
- *Explanation:* Australian law gives arrested people legal rights, including being charged and taken before a court. No one is punished without due process.

**140. Two neighbours receive exactly the same fine for the same offence. One is a doctor and the other is unemployed. What does this show?**
- A. A mistake — fines should match a person's job
- B. The doctor should have been excused
- C. The unemployed man should pay double
- D. The law treats people equally, whatever their position in society ✓
- *Explanation:* Equal treatment under the law is a core belief. The same law and the same process apply to everyone.
