**141. A council worker is told by her manager to approve a friend's application ahead of other people who applied earlier. What should she do?**
- A. Treat all applications equally and in order — favouring friends is against the rule of law ✓
- B. Approve the friend — managers must be obeyed in everything
- C. Approve everyone who asks, from now on
- D. Charge the friend a small fee for the favour
- *Explanation:* Officials must apply the rules equally and honestly. Giving friends special treatment undermines the rule of law.

**142. A new law is passed that many people strongly dislike. While it remains the law, what must people do?**
- A. Ignore it completely
- B. Obey it, while campaigning peacefully to have it changed ✓
- C. Leave the country at once
- D. Punish the politicians personally
- *Explanation:* Everyone must obey the law, even laws they dislike. In a democracy, they may campaign peacefully for change and vote accordingly.

**143. A famous actor is charged with assault. Television programs discuss the case for weeks before the trial. Who decides whether he is guilty?**
- A. Television hosts
- B. Viewers, by voting online
- C. A court, after hearing the evidence at a fair trial ✓
- D. His film studio
- *Explanation:* Media discussion is not a verdict. Only courts decide guilt, based on the evidence presented at a trial.

**144. A police officer asks a shopkeeper for free goods, hinting that the shop might then be inspected less often. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — it is a friendly custom
- B. Only the price of the goods is wrong
- C. Shopkeepers must refuse all customers
- D. It is an abuse of power — officials must act honestly and within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Officials who use their position for personal benefit break the law and the trust placed in them. No one is above the law.

**145. A young driver and an elderly driver are both caught using a mobile phone while driving. What does equality under the law mean here?**
- A. Both face the same legal consequences for the same offence ✓
- B. The young driver is excused because of inexperience
- C. The elderly driver is punished more heavily
- D. Age lets the police choose who is guilty
- *Explanation:* The law applies equally to people of all ages. The same offence carries the same consequences.

**146. A government department makes a decision that a citizen believes is unlawful. Where can the citizen challenge it?**
- A. Nowhere — departments can never be challenged
- B. In the courts, which can review whether officials acted within the law ✓
- C. Only by writing angry letters forever
- D. At the next football match
- *Explanation:* Independent courts can review government decisions. This is one way the rule of law limits government power.

**147. A man tells a magistrate, 'I should not be punished — I know the local police chief personally.' What is the correct response?**
- A. Friendships decide small cases
- B. The magistrate should phone the chief for advice
- C. Personal connections make no difference — the law applies equally to everyone ✓
- D. He can choose between a fine and a favour
- *Explanation:* Under the rule of law, who you know does not change how the law treats you. Cases are decided on facts and law.

**148. Why must governments in Australia obey court rulings that they disagree with?**
- A. Because judges are more popular than politicians
- B. Because courts control the army
- C. Because governments enjoy losing
- D. Because the rule of law binds everyone, including governments — no one is above the law ✓
- *Explanation:* When a court rules, governments must comply, like everyone else. This is what separates the rule of law from rule by power.

**149. Parliament passes a new law today. Police want to fine a man for doing the same thing last year, when it was legal. What does fairness under the rule of law say?**
- A. People are judged by the laws in force at the time — a new law does not punish actions that were lawful when they were done ✓
- B. New laws always apply backwards
- C. Police may backdate any fine they like
- D. Last year's actions are judged by next year's laws
- *Explanation:* A basic part of fairness is that people are judged by the law as it stood when they acted. Laws are not used to punish past behaviour that was legal at the time.

**150. A customs officer at the airport checks a returning politician's bags in exactly the same way as every other traveller's bags. This is an example of what?**
- A. Rudeness to an important person
- B. Equality under the law — official duties are carried out the same way for everyone ✓
- C. A waste of everyone's time
- D. The politician losing an election
- *Explanation:* Officials must do their duty without favour. Equal treatment, even for powerful people, is the rule of law in daily life.

**151. A woman who speaks very little English is charged with an offence. What help can the court provide so that her trial is fair?**
- A. None — she must manage alone
- B. A family member shouts translations from the public seats
- C. An interpreter, so she can understand the case and give her evidence ✓
- D. The trial is cancelled automatically
- *Explanation:* Courts can arrange interpreters so that everyone understands the proceedings. A fair hearing requires the accused to understand the case against them.

**152. A man faces a serious charge but cannot afford a lawyer. What does the Australian legal system offer?**
- A. He must sell his house before the trial can start
- B. Only rich people are allowed lawyers
- C. The judge acts as his lawyer as well
- D. Legal aid services can help people who cannot afford legal representation ✓
- *Explanation:* Legal aid helps people who cannot afford a lawyer, so that everyone can receive a fair hearing.

**153. A jury is chosen for a serious criminal trial. Who serves on a jury in Australia?**
- A. Ordinary citizens chosen from the community ✓
- B. Judges borrowed from other states
- C. Members of the accused's family
- D. Politicians from the local area
- *Explanation:* Juries are made up of ordinary citizens. Being called for jury service is a civic responsibility of citizenship.

**154. During a trial, the judge explains that the prosecution must prove its case. What does this protect?**
- A. The prosecution, from having to work hard
- B. The accused, who is presumed innocent until proven guilty ✓
- C. Witnesses, from attending court
- D. Newspapers, from reporting the case
- *Explanation:* The presumption of innocence means the prosecution must prove guilt. The accused does not have to prove their innocence.

**155. A witness in court promises to tell the truth, and is later found to have deliberately lied. Lying in court in this way is what?**
- A. A clever legal tactic
- B. Perfectly legal if it helps a friend
- C. A serious offence — courts depend on truthful evidence ✓
- D. The judge's problem, not the law's
- *Explanation:* Deliberately lying under oath is a crime. Fair trials depend on evidence that is truthful.

**156. Most court hearings in Australia are open, so members of the public and journalists may attend. Why?**
- A. So trials can become entertainment shows
- B. So the accused can sell tickets
- C. So judges can become famous
- D. Openness keeps the justice system honest — justice is seen to be done ✓
- *Explanation:* Open courts are part of a fair system. Public scrutiny helps ensure that trials are conducted properly.

**157. A young person is charged with a crime for the first time and is frightened and confused. Which right matters most here?**
- A. The right to a fair hearing and to legal advice ✓
- B. The right to be punished quickly without a hearing
- C. The right to choose any punishment
- D. The right to have friends decide the case
- *Explanation:* Everyone charged with an offence — young or old — has the right to a fair hearing and to be represented by a lawyer.

**158. Police are questioning a woman who has been arrested. She says she wants to stay silent and speak to a lawyer first. What is the correct position?**
- A. She must answer every question immediately
- B. She has the right to remain silent and to seek legal advice ✓
- C. Silence is treated as proof of guilt
- D. Only citizens born in Australia may stay silent
- *Explanation:* A person who is arrested has the right to remain silent and to contact a lawyer. These rights protect everyone in the legal process.

**159. A defendant believes the judge made a serious legal mistake in his case. What can he do?**
- A. Refuse to accept that courts exist
- B. Offer the judge a gift to reconsider
- C. Appeal to a higher court to have the decision reviewed ✓
- D. Punish the judge through social media posts
- *Explanation:* Court decisions can be appealed through the legal process. Appeals are how mistakes can be corrected lawfully.

**160. A court finds a woman not guilty after a trial. A radio host says she is 'really guilty' and listeners should shun her. What does the court's decision mean in law?**
- A. She is guilty because the host is popular
- B. She must stand trial again until the host is satisfied
- C. Her neighbours decide her legal status
- D. She is not guilty in law and goes free, whatever opinions others hold ✓
- *Explanation:* Courts decide guilt according to law and evidence. Media opinions do not change a legal verdict.

**161. Why are judges in Australia appointed rather than elected by popular vote?**
- A. So they can decide cases by law and evidence, without needing to please voters or governments ✓
- B. Because elections are too expensive for courts
- C. So judges never have to explain their decisions
- D. To keep courts secret from the public
- *Explanation:* Judicial independence protects fair trials. Judges answer to the law, not to election campaigns or political pressure.

**162. Two neighbours disagree about a fence, and a court hears both sides before deciding. What democratic protection did both neighbours receive?**
- A. The richer neighbour won automatically
- B. A fair hearing before an independent decision-maker ✓
- C. The right to build the fence across the road
- D. A promise that both of them would win
- *Explanation:* Fair hearings are not only for criminal cases. Independent courts settle civil disputes by hearing both sides and applying the law.

**163. An accused man wants his trial delayed forever, so that witnesses forget what happened. What does the legal system aim to provide instead?**
- A. Trials whenever the accused feels ready
- B. No trials for patient people
- C. A fair trial without unreasonable delay, while still allowing time to prepare a defence ✓
- D. Immediate punishment, to save time
- *Explanation:* Fair trials balance both sides: enough time to prepare a defence, but justice should not be delayed unreasonably.

**164. A court clerk is offered money by a defendant to 'lose' some evidence. What must the clerk do?**
- A. Accept, if the amount is large
- B. Ask the judge to share the money
- C. Lose only a little bit of the evidence
- D. Refuse — interfering with evidence is a serious crime, and court staff must act honestly ✓
- *Explanation:* Everyone in the justice system must act honestly. Interfering with evidence attacks the fairness of trials and is a serious offence.

**165. A person is found guilty and sentenced. Later, new evidence suggests the conviction may be wrong. What does the system provide?**
- A. Appeal processes, through which higher courts can review the case ✓
- B. Nothing — convictions can never be reviewed
- C. The police simply release whomever they like
- D. A public vote on the person's guilt
- *Explanation:* Appeals allow higher courts to review convictions. This safety valve is part of a fair legal system.

**166. What is the role of evidence in an Australian trial?**
- A. Evidence is optional decoration
- B. Decisions are based on the evidence presented and tested in court, not on rumours or status ✓
- C. The most famous person's story always wins
- D. Evidence matters only if the judge likes it
- *Explanation:* Courts decide cases on evidence that both sides can test. Rumour, wealth and social status are not evidence.

**167. A witness is frightened about giving evidence against a dangerous person. What does this situation highlight?**
- A. Witnesses have no importance
- B. Trials should rely on gossip instead
- C. Truthful evidence matters greatly to courts, and intimidating a witness is a serious offence ✓
- D. The witness should lie to stay safe
- *Explanation:* Intimidating a witness is a serious crime. The legal system protects the process by which the truth is brought before the court.

**168. A magistrate must decide a case involving a company that once donated money to a cause she supports. What must she do?**
- A. Rule in the company's favour, as thanks
- B. Ask the cause's organisers what to decide
- C. Refuse to hear any company cases ever again
- D. Decide only on the law and the evidence, free from any outside influence ✓
- *Explanation:* Judges and magistrates are independent. Past connections and outside pressure must not affect their decisions.

**169. A defendant chooses to represent himself in court instead of using a lawyer. What does the court still owe him?**
- A. A fair hearing — the same legal process and standards as everyone else ✓
- B. An automatic win, because he is brave
- C. A shorter law that applies only to him
- D. The right to ignore court procedures entirely
- *Explanation:* People may represent themselves, but the process stays the same. Fairness means the same law and procedure for all.

**170. Why can court decisions in Australia be published and reported in the media?**
- A. To embarrass the people involved
- B. So the public can see that justice is done openly, and courts can be scrutinised ✓
- C. Because judges need advertising
- D. So lawyers can become celebrities
- *Explanation:* Open justice lets the community see how courts work. It is a safeguard for fairness and for public confidence.

**171. A man is charged with theft. At his first court appearance, he says he does not understand the charge. What should happen?**
- A. The trial continues anyway, at double speed
- B. He is found guilty for not understanding
- C. The charge is explained to him, and he is given the chance to get legal advice before the case proceeds ✓
- D. His family is charged instead of him
- *Explanation:* A fair process requires the accused to understand the charge against them, and to have the opportunity to obtain legal advice.

**172. A government minister telephones a judge about a case involving the minister's friend, and asks for 'a good outcome'. What is this?**
- A. Normal cooperation between government and courts
- B. A favour that judges expect
- C. The minister's right as an elected official
- D. Improper interference — courts are independent, and ministers must not try to influence cases ✓
- *Explanation:* Ministers must never attempt to influence court cases. Judicial independence protects everyone's right to a fair trial.

**173. Two very similar cases are heard in different towns. What should the outcomes be based on?**
- A. The same law applied to similar facts, leading to consistent outcomes ✓
- B. Whichever town is richer
- C. The mood of the local judge that day
- D. How well known the accused is locally
- *Explanation:* Consistency is part of the rule of law. Similar cases should be treated in similar ways under the same law.

**174. A juror realises that she knows one of the witnesses in the trial personally. What should she do?**
- A. Say nothing, and vote for the witness's side
- B. Tell the court, so the trial stays fair and impartial ✓
- C. Tell the other jurors to trust the witness completely
- D. Discuss the case on social media
- *Explanation:* Jurors must be impartial. Declaring a personal connection helps the court keep the trial fair for both sides.

**175. A person is arrested and charged, and a court date is set some weeks away. In the eyes of the law during those weeks, the person is what?**
- A. Already guilty
- B. Half guilty
- C. Innocent, unless and until a court proves guilt ✓
- D. Guilty if the neighbours think so
- *Explanation:* Being charged is not the same as being convicted. The presumption of innocence applies until a court decides otherwise.

**176. A resident writes a blog post strongly criticising the local council's spending. A friend warns her, 'You can't say that about the council!' What is correct?**
- A. The friend is right — councils may never be criticised
- B. Blogs about councils are banned
- C. Only councillors may write blogs
- D. Peaceful criticism of government, including councils, is protected in a democracy ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians may freely criticise governments at every level. Peaceful public criticism is a strength of democracy, not a crime.

**177. A political cartoonist draws the Prime Minister as a puppet of big business. Is this allowed in Australia?**
- A. Yes — satire and cartoons are part of free expression in a democracy ✓
- B. No — leaders may never be drawn
- C. Only if the Prime Minister approves the drawing first
- D. Cartoons are allowed only about sport
- *Explanation:* Satire is a long tradition of free expression in Australia. Leaders are questioned, and even mocked peacefully, in a democracy.

**178. A journalist investigates claims that a government contract was given to a minister's friend, and publishes the story. This is an example of what?**
- A. A journalist breaking the law
- B. A free press holding power to account ✓
- C. The government writing the news
- D. A journalist standing for election
- *Explanation:* A free press investigates and reports on those in power. This scrutiny helps keep governments honest and accountable.

**179. A student writes an article for the school newsletter arguing that the school day should start later. Some teachers disagree with her view. What should happen, in line with Australian freedoms?**
- A. The article is banned and the student is punished
- B. Students may never express opinions
- C. Her peaceful opinion can be published and debated, like any other view ✓
- D. Only teachers' views may appear in newsletters
- *Explanation:* Freedom of expression includes young people. Peaceful opinions can be shared, and answered with debate.

**180. A man posts online that a local shopkeeper cheated him, knowing the claim is false. The shop loses many customers. What legal issue arises?**
- A. None — anything published online is allowed
- B. The shopkeeper must close the shop
- C. The man wins a free speech award
- D. False statements that harm a person's reputation or business can lead to a defamation action in court ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech has limits. Knowingly false claims that damage reputations can be challenged through defamation law.

**181. A talkback radio caller says the government is 'hopeless' and should be voted out. The host disagrees, but lets the caller finish. What does this show?**
- A. Strong peaceful criticism of government is accepted in Australian democracy ✓
- B. Radio hosts must agree with all callers
- C. Callers may be arrested for rudeness about leaders
- D. Only positive comments about government are allowed on radio
- *Explanation:* Australians may criticise governments strongly and publicly. Disagreement is answered with debate, not punishment.

**182. A political group produces leaflets for an election campaign and hands them out at a train station. This is an example of what?**
- A. Forcing people to vote a certain way
- B. Peaceful political communication — a normal part of free elections ✓
- C. Illegal advertising
- D. Working for the Electoral Commission
- *Explanation:* Campaign material and discussion are part of free elections. Voters remain free to accept or ignore the message.

**183. A newspaper discovers a serious mistake in one of its stories and publishes a correction. Why do responsible newspapers do this?**
- A. Because mistakes are impossible in newspapers
- B. So they can repeat the same story again
- C. A free press is also expected to be responsible — accuracy matters, because false claims can harm people ✓
- D. Corrections are written by the government
- *Explanation:* Press freedom comes with responsibility. Correcting errors protects reputations and keeps public debate honest.

**184. A comedian performs a routine making fun of politicians, judges and celebrities. An audience member says comedy should never mock leaders. What is the democratic response?**
- A. Comedians need government permission for jokes
- B. Mocking leaders is a criminal offence
- C. The show must be closed immediately
- D. Satire of the powerful is a normal, protected part of free expression ✓
- *Explanation:* Peaceful satire is protected expression in Australia. Nobody is above being questioned — or laughed at — in a democracy.

**185. A woman wants to speak at a public meeting against a proposed mine near her town. The chairperson personally supports the mine. What should happen?**
- A. She is given a fair chance to speak, like those who support the mine ✓
- B. Only supporters may speak
- C. She may speak only if she whispers
- D. Meetings hear one side only
- *Explanation:* Fair public debate hears different views. Everyone has the same right to express their opinion peacefully.

**186. An author's book argues for major changes to Australia's Constitution, and it is sold in bookshops everywhere. This is possible because of what?**
- A. Books are never read by governments
- B. Freedom of speech includes publishing peaceful arguments for political change ✓
- C. The Constitution cannot be mentioned in books
- D. Bookshops are outside Australian law
- *Explanation:* People may publish peaceful arguments about any public question, including changing the Constitution. Change itself only happens through a referendum.

**187. A teenager posts threats of violence against a classmate on social media, and claims it is 'free speech'. Is she right?**
- A. Yes — words online can never be punished
- B. Yes — threats are protected speech
- C. No — threats of violence are against the law and are not protected by freedom of speech ✓
- D. Only if the classmate reads the post
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech does not protect threats or incitement to violence. Such behaviour is against the law.

**188. A local paper reports accurately on a criminal trial, naming the accused and summarising the evidence given in open court. Is this allowed?**
- A. No — trials may never be reported
- B. Only guilty verdicts may be reported
- C. Papers must invent extra details to sell copies
- D. Yes — reporting what happens in open court is part of open justice and a free press ✓
- *Explanation:* A free press may report open court proceedings. This transparency helps the public trust the justice system.

**189. A business pays for an advertisement criticising a new tax. The government of the day dislikes the ad. What is the government's proper position?**
- A. The ad is lawful peaceful expression — the government may argue its own case, but cannot ban criticism ✓
- B. The government can ban all advertisements it dislikes
- C. Businesses may never comment on taxes
- D. The business must also advertise praise for the government
- *Explanation:* Governments must tolerate public criticism, including paid advertising. They answer with their own arguments in open debate.

**190. A poet writes verses supporting a cause that most Australians disagree with. Bookshops still sell the poems, and no one is punished. What does this show?**
- A. Unpopular ideas are secretly banned later
- B. Freedom of expression protects unpopular peaceful views, not just popular ones ✓
- C. Poetry is not covered by any freedom
- D. Majorities may censor art they dislike
- *Explanation:* Freedom means little if it protects only popular views. Peaceful expression is protected even when most people disagree with it.

**191. A protester holds a sign with a message that many people find offensive, but which threatens no one. Police at the scene should do what?**
- A. Arrest the protester for giving offence
- B. Confiscate all signs
- C. Protect the peaceful protest — offence alone does not make peaceful expression a crime ✓
- D. End every protest in the area
- *Explanation:* Peaceful protest is lawful even when its messages are unpopular or offensive. Police act against violence or law-breaking, not against opinions.

**192. A website publishes the home address of a police officer and urges readers to 'visit him tonight'. Why is this not protected speech?**
- A. Websites may never mention police officers
- B. Addresses are too boring to publish
- C. The officer works for the government
- D. It endangers a person's safety — speech that threatens or incites harm is against the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech ends where it endangers others. Publishing details to encourage harm is not protected and can be a crime.

**193. A documentary maker produces a film critical of Australia's migration policies, and it is shown on national television. This is an example of what?**
- A. Freedom of expression through film and media ✓
- B. A film maker breaking migration law
- C. Television stations deciding migration policy
- D. A subject that documentaries are banned from covering
- *Explanation:* Film and television are forms of expression. Peaceful criticism of government policy is lawful and common in Australia.

**194. Why does Australia protect journalists who report information that the government would prefer to keep quiet?**
- A. To annoy governments
- B. Because a free press keeps power accountable and informs the people's vote ✓
- C. Because journalists are above the law in all things
- D. Because governments enjoy being criticised
- *Explanation:* Reporting that embarrasses governments is exactly what a free press is for. Informed voters are essential to democracy.

**195. A school holds a student debate in which one team must argue a position its members personally disagree with. What democratic skill does this teach?**
- A. That disagreement is dangerous
- B. That only one side may ever be right
- C. Listening to and testing different arguments — the habit of open debate ✓
- D. That students must hide their real opinions forever
- *Explanation:* Open debate is how democracies test ideas. Understanding opposing arguments is a civic skill Australians value.

**196. A council tries to stop a community newspaper from publishing letters that criticise the council. What do democratic principles say?**
- A. Councils may silence their critics
- B. Letters pages are illegal
- C. The council owns all local news
- D. Peaceful criticism cannot be silenced by the body being criticised — a free press is protected ✓
- *Explanation:* Governments and councils cannot use their power to silence peaceful critics. A free press is a core democratic protection.

**197. A man shouts political slogans through a loudspeaker outside a hospital at two o'clock in the morning, waking patients. Authorities act against him. Why is this not an attack on free speech?**
- A. Freedom of speech has reasonable legal limits — the action is about the time, place and harm to others, not about his opinion ✓
- B. Political slogans are banned at night everywhere
- C. His opinion was unpopular with patients
- D. Hospitals control all speech in their suburb
- *Explanation:* Limits on speech target harm — such as disturbing the sick — not the viewpoint itself. He may share the same message lawfully at a proper time and place.

**198. A famous singer uses a concert to urge fans to vote for change at the coming election. Is this acceptable?**
- A. Celebrities must never mention elections
- B. Yes — anyone may peacefully express political opinions, famous or not ✓
- C. Only politicians may discuss elections
- D. Fans must vote exactly as the singer says
- *Explanation:* Political expression belongs to everyone. Audiences remain free to make up their own minds.

**199. A local historian uncovers unflattering facts about a celebrated national figure and publishes them in a book. What protects the historian?**
- A. Nothing — history must only ever praise
- B. A rule that famous people have no past
- C. Freedom of speech and of the press — peaceful publication of research and opinion ✓
- D. The fact that the figure's family cannot read books
- *Explanation:* Free expression protects inquiry and debate about public figures, past and present. Where published claims are false, defamation law protects reputations.

**200. What is the main reason freedom of speech is limited at all in Australia?**
- A. To protect leaders' feelings
- B. To make debates shorter
- C. To keep unpopular people quiet
- D. To protect people's safety, rights and reputations, so that freedom works for everyone ✓
- *Explanation:* Limits exist to protect others — from threats, violence and false claims that destroy reputations. Within those limits, expression is free.

**201. Five friends decide to start a monthly book club that meets in each other's homes. Starting the club is an example of what?**
- A. Freedom of association — people freely forming a group of their own choice ✓
- B. A business that needs a government licence to discuss books
- C. A political party
- D. An activity allowed only for authors
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association covers everyday groups, from book clubs to national unions. People may form groups without government permission.

**202. Members of a residents' group vote to expel a member who repeatedly broke the group's own rules, following the group's written constitution. What does this show?**
- A. Groups must keep every member forever
- B. Freedom of association includes groups running their own affairs and membership rules, within the law ✓
- C. Expelling a member is a criminal matter for police
- D. Group rules are written by the government
- *Explanation:* Associations are free to organise themselves — setting their own rules and choosing their own members — as long as they act within the law.

**203. Residents of a street form a neighbourhood association to organise clean-up days and a street party. This is possible because of what freedom?**
- A. Freedom to make local laws
- B. Freedom to ignore council rules at parties
- C. Freedom of association — neighbours freely joining together for community purposes ✓
- D. Freedom to close the street forever
- *Explanation:* Community and residents' groups are classic examples of freedom of association in Australian life.

**204. A peaceful march of several hundred people walks through a city centre supporting more funding for hospitals. Police walk alongside and manage the traffic. This shows what?**
- A. Marches are legal only with fewer than 100 people
- B. The police have joined the protest
- C. The marchers are breaking the law slowly
- D. Peaceful assembly is a lawful part of democracy, and police help it happen safely ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians may gather and march peacefully. Police manage safety and traffic so that lawful protest can take place.

**205. An employer tells workers they will lose their jobs if they join a union. Why is this wrong in Australia?**
- A. Workers are free to choose whether to join a union — threatening them attacks that freedom ✓
- B. Employers must join unions themselves
- C. Workers must obey employers about everything
- D. Unions choose workers' jobs for them
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association protects workers' free choice. Threatening to punish workers for joining a union is against Australian law and values.

**206. A surf lifesaving club, a chess club and a parents' group all use the local community hall on different nights of the week. What freedom are they all exercising?**
- A. Freedom to own the hall
- B. Freedom of association — freely forming and joining groups ✓
- C. Freedom to ban other groups from the hall
- D. Freedom from all hall rules
- *Explanation:* Clubs and community groups are the everyday face of freedom of association in Australia.

**207. A young nurse in Sydney is offered a better job in Perth and moves across the country to take it. What freedom has she used?**
- A. The freedom to take any job without applying
- B. A government relocation program
- C. Freedom of movement — citizens may live and work anywhere in Australia ✓
- D. The freedom to keep being paid by her Sydney hospital
- *Explanation:* Citizens are free to travel, live and work in any state or territory. No permission is needed to move within Australia.

**208. A retired couple decide to sell their Melbourne house and move to a small town in Tasmania for a quieter life. What is their position?**
- A. They need the Tasmanian government's permission to move
- B. Melbourne residents may not leave Victoria
- C. Only people born in Tasmania may live there
- D. They are free to move and live anywhere in Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of movement means Australians choose where to live. Australia's internal borders do not restrict citizens' lives.

**209. A community group books the local hall for a public meeting about a proposed development. The council, which supports the development, hires the hall to all groups on equal terms. Why does this matter?**
- A. People are free to meet and discuss public issues — peaceful assembly is part of democracy ✓
- B. Meetings about developments are always banned
- C. The council may choose which opinions are allowed to meet
- D. Hall meetings can overturn laws
- *Explanation:* The freedom to gather and discuss public questions is a democratic freedom. Groups meeting peacefully must be treated equally.

**210. A man joins three different clubs: a football club, a gardening club and a choir. A friend says he is only allowed to join one group. Who is right?**
- A. The friend — one group per person is the law
- B. The man — people may join as many lawful groups as they wish ✓
- C. The choir — singing groups have priority
- D. Nobody — clubs are illegal
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association has no 'one group only' rule. Australians join many clubs, teams and organisations.

**211. Workers at a warehouse meet after work to discuss forming a union. Their supervisor hears about the meeting and is annoyed. What is the legal position?**
- A. Workers may never discuss unions
- B. The supervisor may ban all worker conversations
- C. Workers are free to meet and discuss joining a union — association is their choice ✓
- D. Only supervisors may hold meetings
- *Explanation:* Discussing and forming a union is a protected exercise of freedom of association. Employers cannot lawfully prevent it.

**212. A religious congregation, a sporting club and a political party all rent rooms in the same civic centre. What does this show about freedom of association?**
- A. Only one type of group may use public facilities
- B. Political parties own civic centres
- C. Religious groups must always meet first
- D. Different kinds of groups are all free to organise and meet peacefully ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association protects all kinds of lawful groups equally — social, sporting, religious and political.

**213. A family drives from Adelaide to Brisbane for a holiday, crossing several state borders without asking anyone's permission. This is normal because of what?**
- A. Freedom of movement within Australia ✓
- B. A special holiday law for drivers
- C. State borders are not real borders
- D. Families never need licences for anything
- *Explanation:* Australians travel freely between states and territories. Crossing a state border requires no permit or permission.

**214. A student starts a debating society at her school and invites students of all political views to join. Which freedoms does the society rest on?**
- A. The freedom to skip all classes
- B. Freedom of association, together with freedom of speech ✓
- C. The freedom to mark her own exams
- D. A rule that schools must agree with the society
- *Explanation:* Forming a group and debating ideas combine two core freedoms: association and speech.

**215. A protest group holds a peaceful rally outside Parliament House. They stay on public land, follow police directions and clean up afterwards. Their rally is best described as what?**
- A. An attempt to replace Parliament
- B. Trespass, because Parliament is an important place
- C. Lawful peaceful assembly — a normal part of Australian democracy ✓
- D. Illegal, because politicians work nearby
- *Explanation:* Peaceful protest near the seats of power is a democratic tradition. Staying within the law keeps assembly protected.

**216. A woman resigns from a community association. The association tells her she must now also resign from her unrelated sports club. Is that right?**
- A. Yes — one resignation means resigning from everything
- B. Yes — associations control all of a person's memberships
- C. Yes — clubs share member lists by law
- D. No — membership of each group is a separate free choice ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association means each person chooses their own memberships, group by group.

**217. Migrants from many countries form a cultural association to teach their children traditional dances and language on weekends. This group is an example of what?**
- A. Freedom of association — and Australia's respect for different cultures within the law ✓
- B. A group that needs to hide its activities
- C. An attempt to avoid Australian law
- D. A school run by a foreign government
- *Explanation:* Cultural associations are a welcome part of Australian life. People may celebrate their heritage freely, within Australian law.

**218. A country town's football club cannot field a full team, so players from two towns combine into one club. What freedom makes this easy?**
- A. The freedom to change football rules
- B. Freedom of association — people may form and join groups as they choose ✓
- C. A government order merging all clubs
- D. Freedom of movement for footballs
- *Explanation:* Joining together into new groups is exactly what freedom of association protects — in sport as in everything else.

**219. An Australian citizen returns home from an overseas holiday and passes through passport control back into Australia. Underlying this everyday event is what freedom?**
- A. The freedom to travel overseas only once a year
- B. A favour granted by airport staff
- C. Citizens are free to leave Australia and to return home ✓
- D. A rule that only business travellers may return
- *Explanation:* Freedom of movement includes citizens travelling overseas and coming home. Australians are not locked in, or locked out of, their own country.

**220. A group of retirees meets every morning in a public park to practise tai chi together. What is their position in law and in Australian life?**
- A. Groups may never use parks
- B. Only young people may gather outdoors
- C. They must buy the park to continue
- D. People may gather peacefully in public places — peaceful assembly is lawful ✓
- *Explanation:* Gathering peacefully with others in public is a normal freedom in Australia, as long as people obey the law.

**221. A political party opens a branch in a small town and invites locals to join. A resident who prefers different ideas starts a branch of another party instead. What does this show?**
- A. Freedom of association lets citizens organise politically for competing ideas ✓
- B. Only one party branch is allowed per town
- C. Residents must join the first branch that opens
- D. Parties may not operate in small towns
- *Explanation:* Political association is free and competitive. Citizens choose — or found — the party that matches their ideas.

**222. After a flood, neighbours form a volunteer group to help clean up damaged homes. Nobody ordered them to do it. What does their group show?**
- A. Obedience to government orders
- B. Freedom of association, together with the Australian value of helping others ✓
- C. Freedom to ignore safety rules while helping
- D. A government work program
- *Explanation:* Australians freely form groups to help others, especially in hard times. Volunteering is freedom of association put to generous use.

**223. A man decides to leave a political party he joined years ago, because his views have changed. What is his position?**
- A. He must stay for life once he has joined
- B. He needs the party leader's written permission to leave
- C. He is free to leave — joining and leaving groups are both free choices ✓
- D. He loses his vote by leaving a party
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association includes leaving. No party or group owns its members.

**224. A choir wants to perform in a shopping centre, and asks the centre's management, who agree to a Saturday performance in the food court. Why did the choir need to ask?**
- A. Choirs may never perform in public
- B. Singing requires a police permit everywhere
- C. Food courts are part of Parliament
- D. The centre is private property — freedoms operate within the law, including other people's property rights ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedoms have lawful limits. Performing on private property needs the owner's agreement, just as public protest must stay within the law.

**225. A university student joins a peaceful protest on campus in the morning, goes to her part-time job in the afternoon, and attends her book club at night. Which statement best sums up her day?**
- A. She has exercised everyday Australian freedoms — assembly, work and association ✓
- B. She has broken three laws before dinner
- C. Students are not allowed political views
- D. Only one of these activities is legal for students
- *Explanation:* Freedoms are not abstract. Australians use them daily in ordinary life — meeting, working, joining and speaking.

**226. A Muslim employee asks her manager if she may pray during her lunch break in a quiet room. Allowing this, where work requirements permit, reflects what?**
- A. A rule that only one religion may pray at work
- B. Freedom of religion — people may practise their faith within the law and reasonable workplace arrangements ✓
- C. The manager joining her religion
- D. Religion replacing workplace safety rules
- *Explanation:* Australians may practise their religion in daily life, within the law and within reasonable arrangements at work.

**227. A Sikh man wears a turban as part of his faith while working in an office. What is the correct position in Australia?**
- A. Religious dress is banned in all workplaces
- B. He must choose between his faith and any job
- C. People may wear religious dress — freedom of religion is protected, subject to safety laws that apply to everyone ✓
- D. Only religious leaders may wear religious dress
- *Explanation:* Religious dress is part of practising a faith and is respected in Australia, within general laws such as safety requirements.

**228. A Hindu family celebrates a festival with food, music and decorations at their home, and invites their neighbours to join in. This is an example of what?**
- A. A festival that must be kept secret
- B. Neighbours being forced to convert
- C. A breach of Australian values
- D. Freedom of religion and cultural life, practised peacefully within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians of many faiths celebrate their festivals openly. Sharing celebrations with neighbours is part of multicultural life.

**229. A young woman raised in one faith decides as an adult to follow a different religion. Her family disagrees, but the choice is hers. Why?**
- A. Freedom of religion includes the freedom to change your religion ✓
- B. Adults must keep their childhood religion forever
- C. Changing religion requires a court order
- D. Only unmarried people may change religion
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion protects changing faiths as much as keeping them. The choice belongs to each person.

**230. An atheist attends a friend's church wedding as a guest. He joins the celebration but does not take part in the prayers. What does this show?**
- A. Guests must perform every ritual at weddings
- B. Freedom of religion includes following no religion — and respecting others' choices ✓
- C. Atheists may not enter places of worship
- D. Weddings are government ceremonies
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion protects belief and non-belief alike. Australians respect each other's choices in matters of faith.

**231. A congregation wants to build a new place of worship in a suburb. What must they do, like any other builder?**
- A. Nothing — religious buildings need no approvals
- B. Obtain the Prime Minister's personal signature
- C. Follow the same planning and building laws as everyone else ✓
- D. Build only in secret
- *Explanation:* Religious groups enjoy the same freedoms — and the same legal duties — as everyone else. Planning laws apply equally to all builders.

**232. A Jewish employee arranges leave in advance with his employer so that he does not work on his holy days. This arrangement rests on what?**
- A. A rule that holy days cancel all workplaces
- B. The employer converting to his religion
- C. A rule that workplaces must close on every holy day of every faith
- D. Freedom of religion — his observance is respected within normal workplace arrangements ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians' religious observances are respected in daily life, worked out within the same laws and agreements that cover all workers.

**233. A Buddhist group holds a peaceful meditation gathering in a public park, open to anyone who is interested. This is an example of what?**
- A. Freedom of religion — practising and sharing a faith peacefully in public ✓
- B. A gathering that must be hidden from other park users
- C. The park becoming a temple
- D. A rule that only one faith may use parks
- *Explanation:* Faiths may be practised and shared peacefully in public life, like any lawful activity in a public place.

**234. One family on a street sends their daughter to a Catholic school because they value its religious education. Another family chooses the local public school. What does this show?**
- A. One of the families must be breaking the law
- B. Families are free to choose education that matches their beliefs, within the law ✓
- C. Religious schools choose families by suburb
- D. Public schools are closed to religious families
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion and parental choice allow families to select schooling that fits their beliefs, from the options the law provides.

**235. A man leaves the religion he grew up in and joins no other faith. Years later he still describes himself as 'not religious'. What is his position in Australia?**
- A. He must register as an offender
- B. He has fewer rights than religious people
- C. He is fully equal — freedom from religion is protected just like freedom of religion ✓
- D. He must pick a religion before he can vote
- *Explanation:* Australians may follow any religion or none at all. Rights and treatment under the law do not depend on belief.

**236. A charity run by a religious group serves free meals to homeless people of all backgrounds, asking nothing about their beliefs. This reflects what?**
- A. A scheme to increase church membership by law
- B. Religious groups escaping the law
- C. Meals that may only go to believers
- D. Religious and community organisations contributing to Australian community life, within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Religious organisations run many charities in Australia. Helping people in need, whatever their beliefs, is part of community life.

**237. A prisoner asks to see a chaplain of his own faith for spiritual support. Providing this, where possible, reflects what?**
- A. Freedom of religion does not disappear because a person is in prison — it applies within prison rules ✓
- B. Prisons must convert all prisoners
- C. Only one faith is allowed visitors
- D. Chaplains decide prisoners' sentences
- *Explanation:* People in prison keep their basic rights, including practising their religion, within the rules needed for safety and order.

**238. Two friends of different faiths marry each other, and both families celebrate together. What do Australian law and belief say?**
- A. Marriages between faiths are banned
- B. Adults are free to choose their own partner — faith is no legal barrier ✓
- C. One partner must convert before the wedding, by law
- D. The couple must marry in secret
- *Explanation:* In Australia, whom you marry is your own free choice. A difference in religion does not remove that freedom.

**239. A door-to-door preacher knocks at a house. The householder politely says she is not interested, and the preacher leaves straight away. What does this small scene show?**
- A. Preaching door to door is always illegal
- B. Householders must listen for ten minutes by law
- C. People may share their faith peacefully, and others may freely decline to listen ✓
- D. The preacher may return every hour until she listens
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion lets people share their beliefs peacefully. It equally protects other people's freedom to say no.

**240. A hospital patient asks for food that meets her religious dietary rules, and the hospital provides it where it can. This reflects what?**
- A. Hospitals must serve only religious food
- B. The patient taking control of the whole menu
- C. Dietary rules overriding medical needs
- D. Everyday respect for religious practice within public services ✓
- *Explanation:* Public services in Australia commonly respect religious practices, such as dietary needs, as part of treating people with dignity.

**241. In one town, a church, a mosque, a synagogue and a temple all stand within a few streets of each other, and their members live side by side. What does this picture show?**
- A. Freedom of religion in practice — many faiths living together peacefully and equally ✓
- B. A failure of town planning
- C. That one of the buildings must be illegal
- D. Religions competing to own the town
- *Explanation:* Australia's streets reflect its freedoms: people of many faiths — and of none — live together as equals under the law.

**242. A new citizen wonders whether becoming a citizen means she must adopt a particular religion. What is the correct answer?**
- A. Yes — citizens must share one faith
- B. No — Australia has no official religion, and citizens keep their own beliefs ✓
- C. Yes — but only for the first year
- D. Only citizens born overseas must convert
- *Explanation:* Citizenship requires commitment to Australia's democratic beliefs and laws — not to any religion. Australia's government is secular.

**243. One student in a class wears a small cross on a necklace, another wears a hijab, and a third wears no religious symbol at all. What principle covers all three?**
- A. Only one symbol is permitted per classroom
- B. Students must hide all beliefs at school
- C. Freedom of religion — each person's practice, or non-practice, is respected equally ✓
- D. Schools decide students' religions
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion protects every student's choice equally — to practise a faith openly, or to follow none.

**244. A religious leader tells his followers that they must break Australian law in order to follow their faith properly. What is the correct Australian position?**
- A. Religious leaders may suspend Australian law
- B. Followers must obey the leader rather than the law
- C. The law stops at the place of worship's door
- D. Everyone must obey Australian law, whatever their role or their beliefs ✓
- *Explanation:* No religious teaching can override Australian law. Everyone — leaders and followers alike — must obey the law.

**245. A witness in court who does not follow any religion is asked how she will promise to tell the truth. What does the court allow?**
- A. She may make a non-religious affirmation instead of swearing a religious oath ✓
- B. She cannot give evidence at all
- C. She must pretend to follow a religion
- D. Her evidence counts for half as much
- *Explanation:* Courts allow witnesses to affirm rather than swear a religious oath. Belief or non-belief does not reduce a person's place in the legal system.

**246. A city council lights up its hall for several different religious festivals during the year, treating each community alike. What does this reflect?**
- A. The council choosing an official religion each month
- B. Equal treatment of all religions — no faith is favoured by government ✓
- C. Festivals becoming government property
- D. Religions paying for equal treatment
- *Explanation:* Governments in Australia treat religions equally. Recognising different communities' celebrations in an even-handed way reflects a secular, equal approach.

**247. A man prays quietly at his desk before eating his lunch at work. A colleague says religion should be 'kept at home'. What is the correct position?**
- A. The colleague is right — all prayer at work is banned
- B. Only silent religions are allowed anywhere
- C. Quiet personal practice that does not disturb others is part of freedom of religion ✓
- D. The man must invite everyone to join in
- *Explanation:* Personal religious practice is protected in daily life, provided it follows the law and respects others.

**248. Why do Australian governments not run or control places of worship?**
- A. Because governments dislike religion
- B. Because places of worship run themselves by magic
- C. Because only private companies may build them
- D. Because government and religion are separate in a secular system ✓
- *Explanation:* Separation of government and religion protects both: governments stay neutral between faiths, and faiths remain free.

**249. A Sikh schoolboy, a Christian schoolgirl and an atheist schoolboy all appear in the same class photo. What protects each of them equally?**
- A. Freedom of religion — the right to follow a religion, or to follow none ✓
- B. A rule that schools must mix religions in photos
- C. Only the photographer's personal kindness
- D. Nothing — only adults have this freedom
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion belongs to everyone in Australia, including children, whatever they believe.

**250. A woman says, 'My religion teaches me to help people in need, so I volunteer at a food bank every week.' Which statement is right?**
- A. Volunteering is allowed only for religious people
- B. Her faith motivates her, and volunteering is open to everyone — religious or not ✓
- C. Food banks are religious organisations by law
- D. She must recruit the people she helps
- *Explanation:* People volunteer from many motivations, faith among them. Community service in Australia is open to all.

**251. A café owner refuses to serve a customer because of the customer's race. What is the correct position?**
- A. Owners may refuse anyone for any reason, including race
- B. It is rude but fully legal everywhere
- C. It is discrimination — against Australian values and against the law ✓
- D. The customer should simply accept it in silence forever
- *Explanation:* Refusing service because of race is discrimination. It offends Australian values of equality and is unlawful.

**252. A young woman applies for an apprenticeship as an electrician, a trade with mostly male workers. She is the best-qualified applicant. What should happen?**
- A. The job must go to a man, to keep tradition
- B. She may apply only if no man wants the job
- C. She should be offered office work instead
- D. She is judged on her skills and merit — women may do any job in Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* Jobs are open to women and men equally. Decisions are made on merit, not on gender.

**253. Two workers doing the same job, with the same experience, discover that the woman is being paid much less than the man. What do Australian law and belief say?**
- A. Paying a woman less than a man for the same work is against the law — equal work deserves equal treatment ✓
- B. Pay differences by gender are a private choice for employers
- C. The woman should be grateful to have a job
- D. The man must personally give her half of his pay
- *Explanation:* Equal treatment at work is required by law. Gender is not a lawful reason to pay someone less for the same work.

**254. A boy wants to join his school's netball team and a girl wants to play in the football competition. What reflects Australian beliefs?**
- A. Both must stick to 'traditional' sports for their gender
- B. Opportunities should be open on merit and interest, not fixed by gender stereotypes ✓
- C. Only the girl may switch sports
- D. Sport is closed to both of them
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity means girls and boys are not held back by stereotypes about what they may do.

**255. A landlord refuses to rent a flat to a family because of their religion. What is this?**
- A. A landlord's absolute right in all cases
- B. Unfair, but required by law
- C. Discrimination — treating people unfairly because of religion is against Australian values and the law ✓
- D. A sign the family should change religion to get the flat
- *Explanation:* People must not be treated unfairly in housing because of their religion, race or background. Equality applies in everyday life.

**256. An elderly worker is passed over for training that is given to younger staff, although he does the same job and wants to learn. What principle is at stake?**
- A. Training is a prize reserved for the young
- B. Older workers have no right to learn
- C. He should retire to make room for others
- D. People should be treated fairly at work regardless of age — opportunity should not depend on stereotypes ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian values reject discrimination. Fair treatment at work applies to people of all ages.

**257. A woman with a disability applies for a job she is fully qualified to do, in a wheelchair-accessible office. The manager hires less-qualified people instead, to avoid 'complications'. What has gone wrong?**
- A. She has been discriminated against — decisions should be based on her ability to do the job ✓
- B. Nothing — managers may avoid disability freely
- C. She should apply only for special 'disability jobs'
- D. Qualifications matter less than convenience in all hiring
- *Explanation:* Judging a person by a disability rather than by their ability is discrimination. Hiring should be based on merit.

**258. At a citizenship ceremony, new citizens from twenty different countries receive exactly the same rights as citizens born in Australia. Why?**
- A. Ceremonies erase legal differences by accident
- B. Citizenship is equal — every citizen has the same rights and responsibilities ✓
- C. Only their children will be equal citizens
- D. They must wait ten years for voting rights
- *Explanation:* There are no classes of citizen in Australia. A citizen by migration has exactly the same rights as a citizen by birth.

**259. A bus driver orders passengers of one ethnic background to sit at the back of the bus. Passengers object to this. They are right, because of what?**
- A. Passengers may sit anywhere only on weekends
- B. Drivers own the seats personally
- C. Everyone in Australia is entitled to equal treatment — segregation by race has no place here ✓
- D. The back seats are reserved by law for new migrants
- *Explanation:* Equal treatment is a core Australian belief. Ordering people around by race is discrimination, and is not accepted in Australia.

**260. A girl from a migrant family tops her school's science competition and wins a scholarship that was open to all students. This is an example of what?**
- A. Luck that should not be repeated
- B. A scholarship that should be reserved for local-born students
- C. The school breaking equality rules
- D. Equality of opportunity — success based on talent and effort, whatever your background ✓
- *Explanation:* Open competition judged on merit is the 'fair go' in action. Background does not limit a person's chance to succeed.

**261. A man is mocked at work every day because of his accent, and his supervisor laughs along. What is the correct description of this?**
- A. Bullying and discrimination — everyone at work deserves fair and respectful treatment ✓
- B. Harmless fun that the man must simply accept
- C. The man's own fault for having an accent
- D. Something allowed because the supervisor enjoys it
- *Explanation:* Mocking a person for their background or accent is discrimination and bullying. Workplaces must treat everyone with respect.

**262. Two candidates are interviewed for a council job. One is the mayor's nephew; the other scores far better in the interviews. Who should get the job, by Australian beliefs?**
- A. The nephew — family connections come first
- B. The better-qualified candidate — public jobs are filled on merit ✓
- C. Whoever the mayor likes best at lunch
- D. The older candidate, automatically
- *Explanation:* Public positions are filled on merit. Using public office to benefit family members is against Australian values and rules.

**263. A female candidate and a male candidate stand in an election, and voters judge their policies at public debates. This normal scene rests on what belief?**
- A. Women may speak but not be voted for
- B. Debates are for male candidates only
- C. Men and women have equal political rights — to vote, to campaign and to be elected ✓
- D. Candidates are chosen by lot, not by voters
- *Explanation:* Women and men participate equally in Australian democracy — as voters, campaigners and members of parliament.

**264. A community hall can be hired by any local group. The manager refuses a booking from one group because most of its members follow a particular religion. What is this?**
- A. Good management
- B. The group's fault for having a religion
- C. Legal, because halls are busy places
- D. Discrimination — public facilities must treat groups fairly, whatever their religion ✓
- *Explanation:* Equal treatment applies to community facilities. Refusing a group because of its members' religion is discrimination.

**265. A baby girl and a baby boy are born in the same hospital. As they grow up in Australia, their legal position will be what?**
- A. Exactly the same basic rights — gender does not decide a person's rights ✓
- B. The boy will have greater voting rights
- C. The girl will need permission to work
- D. Their rights will depend on their parents' wealth
- *Explanation:* From birth, Australians hold equal basic rights. Neither gender, wealth nor background changes that equality.

**266. A talented young cricketer from a poor family is selected for a state team ahead of richer players who trained at expensive academies. This reflects what?**
- A. Poverty being rewarded automatically
- B. Selection on talent and effort — the 'fair go' in sport ✓
- C. Rich players being banned from cricket
- D. Teams being chosen by lottery
- *Explanation:* In Australian life, what counts is ability and effort. Background and wealth should not decide opportunity.

**267. A shop assistant serves customers in order, but keeps skipping an elderly woman and serving younger people first. What principle is she ignoring?**
- A. Young people must always be served first
- B. Queues are optional for staff
- C. Equal and fair treatment of every customer ✓
- D. Elderly people should shop online only
- *Explanation:* Fair treatment in everyday services is equality in practice. Nobody should be pushed aside because of age or background.

**268. An immigrant engineer whose qualifications are recognised applies for the same job as a local graduate. The employer should judge them how?**
- A. By birthplace — locals first, by law
- B. By accent
- C. By which school sounds more familiar
- D. On their skills, qualifications and merit ✓
- *Explanation:* Hiring decisions based on merit treat all applicants fairly. Birthplace and accent are not lawful measures of a person's ability.

**269. A council provides interpreters at its service centre so that residents with limited English can use council services. What does this support?**
- A. Equal access to services for everyone in the community ✓
- B. A rule that English speakers receive no service
- C. Residents being forced to bring family members as translators
- D. Services being available only to citizens
- *Explanation:* Equality includes fair access to public services. Helping people overcome language barriers lets everyone take part in community life.

**270. A woman is elected leader of her political party through a vote of its members. Some commentators say leadership is 'not women's work'. Australian beliefs say what?**
- A. The commentators decide who may lead
- B. Women have the same right as men to lead in public life ✓
- C. She may lead only if all the men retire
- D. Party leadership is decided by television audiences
- *Explanation:* Women and men hold equal rights in public life. A woman's right to lead is the same as a man's.

**271. A school enrols children from many faiths and backgrounds, and treats them all under the same rules and expectations. This is an example of what?**
- A. The school ignoring differences completely
- B. Children being forced to abandon their cultures
- C. Equality in practice — the same fair treatment for every child ✓
- D. A school breaking the law
- *Explanation:* Equality means the same fair rules and chances for every child, while families keep their own cultures and faiths.

**272. A rich man and a poor man are each given the same standard fine for the same littering offence. The rich man complains that the fine is 'nothing to him, so it is unfair'. The fine being the same shows what?**
- A. Fines should be kept secret
- B. Littering should be legal for the poor
- C. The poor man should pay extra
- D. The law treats people equally — the same offence carries the same consequence ✓
- *Explanation:* Standard penalties applied to everyone express equality under the law. Wealth does not buy different treatment.

**273. A company promotes a woman to manage a team of men. One worker refuses her instructions because she is a woman. What is the correct position?**
- A. Her authority comes from her role — refusing her because of her gender is discrimination ✓
- B. The worker may choose his manager's gender
- C. She must manage only women
- D. The company must replace her with a man
- *Explanation:* Women hold positions of authority on the same basis as men. Refusing to accept a manager because she is a woman is discriminatory.

**274. A deaf student is provided with support services so that she can take part fully in her university course. This is an example of what?**
- A. Special treatment that breaks equality
- B. Equality of opportunity — removing barriers so that everyone has a fair chance ✓
- C. The university lowering all of its standards
- D. Support available only to citizens by birth
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity sometimes means removing barriers. Fair chances — not identical treatment in every detail — are the goal.

**275. A local council names a new park after an Aboriginal elder, after consulting the community. What does this decision reflect?**
- A. Councils naming parks by accident
- B. Only elders mattering in history
- C. Respect for the contributions of people of all backgrounds in the community ✓
- D. A law requiring all parks to have personal names
- *Explanation:* Recognising people of different backgrounds reflects Australia's belief that everyone belongs, and that everyone's contribution counts.

**276. Most people in a town tell a council survey they want a small religious group's annual festival in the park stopped. What protects the group?**
- A. Nothing — a majority can cancel any minority activity
- B. The group must move to another town
- C. Festivals need majority approval in all cases
- D. Basic rights are not removed by majority opinion — the group may celebrate peacefully within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Democracy is not unlimited majority rule. The rights of minorities are protected by law, even when majorities dislike them.

**277. A driver caught speeding tells the police officer, 'I have freedom — I can drive how I like.' What is the correct response?**
- A. Freedom operates within the law — road laws protect everyone's safety ✓
- B. The officer must let him go
- C. Freedom includes breaking safety laws
- D. Only professional drivers have freedom
- *Explanation:* No freedom in Australia includes breaking laws that protect others. Freedom and responsibility go together.

**278. A factory owner says his 'freedom' allows him to dump waste in a creek. Why is he wrong?**
- A. Creeks belong to factory owners
- B. His actions harm others and the environment — freedoms are limited by law to protect the community ✓
- C. Only small factories have limits
- D. Freedom means never being told anything
- *Explanation:* Freedom ends where harm to others begins. Laws protecting the community bind everyone equally.

**279. A small language community wants its children to learn their heritage language at weekend classes. Some people object that 'everyone should just be the same'. What do Australian beliefs say?**
- A. The classes must be banned
- B. Heritage languages may be spoken only in secret
- C. Families may keep their languages and cultures, within Australian law, while sharing in Australian life ✓
- D. Children must choose between cultures by the age of ten
- *Explanation:* Australia respects the cultures people bring with them. Keeping a heritage language harms no one, and enriches the community.

**280. A candidate wins an election while promising to take the vote away from citizens who disagree with her. Why would this be against Australian beliefs, even if she is popular?**
- A. Popular leaders may do anything at all
- B. Voting is a gift handed out by leaders
- C. Disagreement becomes illegal once someone wins
- D. Voting rights belong to citizens equally — democracy protects everyone's right to take part ✓
- *Explanation:* Democratic rights are not gifts from leaders. Every citizen's right to vote is protected, whoever is in power.

**281. A party that loses a national election issues a statement accepting the result and promising to hold the new government accountable in Parliament. What does this show?**
- A. Democracy continuing peacefully — opposition is a respected part of the system ✓
- B. The party giving up politics forever
- C. A mistake, because losers should resist
- D. Parliament being cancelled
- *Explanation:* A loyal opposition is part of democracy. Accepting results, while continuing to question the government, keeps the system strong.

**282. A man starts an online group encouraging people to attack members of a particular religion. What is the legal position?**
- A. Encouraging attacks is protected speech
- B. Inciting violence against others is against the law — it is not protected expression ✓
- C. Online groups are outside the law
- D. Only the religion's members may object
- *Explanation:* Speech that incites violence against others crosses the legal limit of freedom. Everyone is entitled to live in peace and security.

**283. A majority of students at a school vote to stop one student with unpopular political views from speaking at assembly. The principal allows the speech. Why?**
- A. Principals must ignore all votes
- B. Unpopular views must always win
- C. Peaceful expression is protected even when a majority dislikes the view ✓
- D. Assemblies are outside all school rules
- *Explanation:* Protecting unpopular peaceful speech is the real test of freedom. Majority disapproval does not remove a person's rights.

**284. A newspaper columnist writes that a minority community should not be allowed to build a community centre. Residents campaign against the column, and the centre is built lawfully. Which freedoms were exercised in this story?**
- A. Only the columnist's
- B. Only the centre builders'
- C. Nobody's — councils build community centres
- D. Both sides' — the columnist's freedom of speech, and the residents' peaceful campaigning and association ✓
- *Explanation:* Democracy allows strong disagreement. The columnist may write, residents may campaign, and lawful decisions proceed — all peacefully.

**285. A new citizen asks why her rights matter, if she will often be in a minority on issues she cares about. What is the best answer?**
- A. Laws and rights protect everyone equally — majorities change, but rights remain ✓
- B. Minorities have no real rights
- C. Rights only matter for election winners
- D. She should join the majority on everything
- *Explanation:* Rights exist precisely so that nobody depends on being in the majority. Today's minority view may become tomorrow's majority.

**286. A shopkeeper displays a sign: 'No entry to people of one particular faith.' Regular customers boycott the shop, and the authorities act. Why?**
- A. Signs in shop windows are banned
- B. The sign is discrimination — equal treatment in public life is protected by law ✓
- C. Customers may not boycott shops
- D. The faith's members must pay for entry
- *Explanation:* Discrimination in public services is against Australian values and against the law. Everyone is entitled to equal treatment.

**287. A teenager argues that freedom of speech means she can say absolutely anything to anyone, at any time. What is the balanced truth?**
- A. She is completely right
- B. Speech is banned for teenagers
- C. Speech is free and wide in Australia, but limited by laws protecting safety and reputations ✓
- D. Adults decide all speech for teenagers
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech is broad, but it is not unlimited. Threats, incitement to violence and defamation are its main legal limits.

**288. An election candidate knocks on doors in her electorate on a Saturday morning, chatting with residents about her policies. A resident who supports another candidate listens politely, then closes the door. This ordinary scene shows what?**
- A. Candidates may visit only their own supporters
- B. Door-knocking is banned in Australia
- C. Residents must invite candidates inside
- D. Free political communication — candidates may campaign peacefully, and voters decide freely ✓
- *Explanation:* Campaigning door to door is a normal part of democratic life. Everyone may listen, argue, or politely decline.

**289. A woman is pressured by relatives not to vote in an election, because they say politics is 'not for women'. What do Australian beliefs say?**
- A. Women have exactly the same right to vote as men — no one may take that right away ✓
- B. Families decide how women vote
- C. Voting is optional for women only
- D. Her relatives may vote on her behalf by custom
- *Explanation:* Every citizen's vote is their own. Women and men hold identical political rights in Australia.

**290. A community radio station broadcasts programs in many different languages for the communities in its area. What does this reflect?**
- A. Radio breaking language laws
- B. A multicultural democracy, where diverse communities can speak and be heard ✓
- C. Each community living under its own separate laws
- D. English being banned on radio
- *Explanation:* Freedom of expression serves all communities. Many voices in public life strengthen democracy.

**291. A judge rules that a government program unlawfully discriminates against a small group, and orders it changed. Supporters of the program are angry. Why is the ruling democratic?**
- A. Judges should always please majorities
- B. The small group bought the judge
- C. Independent courts protect the rights of everyone, including small and unpopular groups ✓
- D. Government programs can never be questioned
- *Explanation:* Courts protect minorities against unlawful treatment — even by popular governments. That is the rule of law in action.

**292. A man says he will refuse to serve on a jury if he is called, because court decisions are 'not his business'. What is the correct position?**
- A. Jury service is optional entertainment
- B. Only lawyers serve on juries
- C. He may ignore a jury summons forever
- D. Jury service is a civic responsibility that helps keep trials fair ✓
- *Explanation:* Fair trials depend on citizens serving on juries. It is one of the responsibilities that come with citizenship.

**293. A political leader suggests that newspapers which criticise him should lose their licences. Why is this against Australian beliefs?**
- A. A free press must be able to criticise leaders without punishment — that is its democratic job ✓
- B. Licences are decided by leaders' feelings
- C. Newspapers should praise leaders every month
- D. Criticism is allowed only in election years
- *Explanation:* Punishing critics is what democracies exist to prevent. Leaders answer criticism with argument, not with power.

**294. A student from a minority background is chosen as school captain by a student vote, ahead of more 'popular' candidates. What does this show?**
- A. Votes only count for majorities
- B. Democratic opportunity is open to everyone — background does not limit what a person can achieve ✓
- C. The election must be run again
- D. Captains are chosen by birth
- *Explanation:* Equal opportunity in democratic life means anyone can earn the community's trust, whatever their background.

**295. A religious group and an atheist group run stalls side by side at a community fair, chatting happily with each other and the public. What Australian belief does the scene show?**
- A. One group must be pretending
- B. Fairs are religious events by law
- C. People of different beliefs living together peacefully and respectfully ✓
- D. Both groups have broken the law
- *Explanation:* Australians of all faiths, and of none, live side by side. Mutual respect in everyday life is a core belief.

**296. A person says, 'Democracy means the majority gets whatever it wants, always.' What is the correction?**
- A. The majority is always right
- B. Minorities should decide everything instead
- C. There are no limits on majorities in Australia
- D. Majorities govern, but the law and the rights of minorities set limits — everyone keeps their basic rights ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian democracy combines majority rule with the rule of law. Basic rights are not voted away.

**297. A council removes a resident's angry letter from its public website, but keeps letters praising the council. The resident complains. What principle supports her?**
- A. Governments must not silence peaceful criticism — public debate includes views the council dislikes ✓
- B. Websites may hold only praise
- C. Letters to councils are the private property of mayors
- D. Angry letters are always illegal
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech protects critics of government. Public forums in a democracy cannot keep only the praise.

**298. Why do Australians say that rights and responsibilities go together?**
- A. Rights are payment for good behaviour
- B. Your freedoms are protected by the same system of laws that asks you to respect the freedoms of others ✓
- C. Responsibilities cancel out rights
- D. Only responsible people may have rights
- *Explanation:* Rights survive because everyone respects them. Obeying the law and respecting others keeps freedom alive for all.

**299. A class of future citizens learns that Australia's freedoms did not appear by accident. What keeps them strong?**
- A. Nothing — freedoms cannot be lost or used
- B. Only ancient documents
- C. Citizens who use their rights peacefully, respect others' rights, and take part in democracy ✓
- D. Governments being left completely alone
- *Explanation:* Freedoms stay strong when citizens participate — voting, speaking, joining, and respecting the equal rights of others.
