**300. A new citizen sums up what she has learned: 'My rights are protected by law — and I must respect the same rights for everyone else.' What has she understood?**
- A. Only half of democracy — rights without duties
- B. That citizens need not respect others' rights
- C. That rights apply only to new citizens
- D. The heart of Australian democracy — equal rights and mutual respect for all ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian democracy joins rights with respect: everyone's freedoms are protected, and everyone is expected to honour the freedoms of others.

## Topic 3: Government and the law in Australia

**1. The Australian Constitution came into effect on which date?**
- A. 1 January 1901 ✓
- B. 26 January 1788
- C. 25 April 1915
- D. 1 January 2001
- *Explanation:* The Constitution came into effect on 1 January 1901, when the colonies formed the Commonwealth of Australia.

**2. How can the Australian Constitution be changed?**
- A. By an order of the Prime Minister
- B. Only by the people voting to approve the change at a referendum ✓
- C. By the King signing a new version
- D. By a vote of newspaper editors
- *Explanation:* The Constitution can only be changed by referendum — the Australian people must vote to approve the change.

**3. For a referendum to succeed in Australia, it needs what?**
- A. A majority of voters in one state
- B. Approval by the Senate alone
- C. A majority of voters overall, and a majority of voters in a majority of states ✓
- D. Every single voter to agree
- *Explanation:* A referendum needs a 'double majority': most voters across the nation, and most voters in most states (four of the six).

**4. Who is the head of state of Australia?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. The Governor-General
- C. The Chief Justice
- D. The King of Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia's head of state is the King of Australia. The Governor-General is the King's representative in Australia.

**5. Who represents the King in Australia?**
- A. The Governor-General ✓
- B. The Prime Minister
- C. The Speaker of the House
- D. The Chief Justice
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General represents the King in Australia and carries out the formal duties of the head of state.

**6. How does a person become Governor-General of Australia?**
- A. They are elected by the people
- B. They are appointed by the King on the advice of the Australian Prime Minister ✓
- C. They are chosen by the High Court
- D. The position is inherited
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General is appointed by the King, on the advice of the Australian Prime Minister of the day.

**7. Which of these is a duty of the Governor-General?**
- A. Deciding the verdict in court cases
- B. Running a state government
- C. Signing Bills passed by Parliament into law (royal assent) ✓
- D. Setting the rate of income tax
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General signs Bills into law on behalf of the King. This is called royal assent.

**8. How many levels of government are there in Australia?**
- A. One
- B. Two
- C. Four
- D. Three ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia has three levels of government: the Australian (federal) Government, state and territory governments, and local government.

**9. What are the three levels of government in Australia?**
- A. Australian (federal), state/territory, and local ✓
- B. Federal, royal, and local
- C. Military, civil, and local
- D. National, royal, and suburban
- *Explanation:* The three levels are the Australian Government, the state and territory governments, and local councils.

**10. Which level of government is responsible for defence, immigration and citizenship?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The Australian Government ✓
- C. State governments
- D. Neighbourhood committees
- *Explanation:* The Australian Government looks after defence, immigration, citizenship, trade and foreign affairs for the whole country.

**11. Which level of government is mainly responsible for schools and hospitals?**
- A. The Australian Government only
- B. Local councils
- C. State and territory governments ✓
- D. The Governor-General
- *Explanation:* State and territory governments run schools, hospitals, police and public transport.

**12. Local governments (councils) are mainly responsible for what?**
- A. Defence
- B. The national currency
- C. Immigration
- D. Local roads, parks, libraries and rubbish collection ✓
- *Explanation:* Councils look after local matters: roads, parks, libraries, building rules, pet control and rubbish collection.

**13. What is the title of the person who heads a local council?**
- A. A mayor ✓
- B. A premier
- C. A governor
- D. A senator
- *Explanation:* A mayor (or shire president) leads a local council, together with elected councillors.

**14. What are the local rules made by a council called?**
- A. Referendums
- B. By-laws ✓
- C. Treaties
- D. Constitutions
- *Explanation:* Councils make by-laws — local rules about things like parking, pets, signs and building in their area.

**15. The Commonwealth of Australia was formed in 1901 by joining together what?**
- A. Six capital cities
- B. Six political parties
- C. Six separate British colonies ✓
- D. Six army battalions
- *Explanation:* At Federation in 1901, the six separate British colonies became the six states of the new Commonwealth of Australia.

**16. What is the title of the leader of a state government?**
- A. The mayor
- B. The governor
- C. The chancellor
- D. The premier ✓
- *Explanation:* The leader of a state government is the Premier. The leader of the Australian Government is the Prime Minister.

**17. Who represents the King in each Australian state?**
- A. The Governor of the state ✓
- B. The Premier
- C. The Lord Mayor
- D. The Chief Justice
- *Explanation:* Each state has a Governor, who represents the King in that state, just as the Governor-General does for the whole country.

**18. What is the title of the leader of a territory government?**
- A. The premier
- B. The Chief Minister ✓
- C. The governor
- D. The president
- *Explanation:* Territory governments are led by a Chief Minister — for example in the ACT and the Northern Territory.

**19. Australia's two self-governing mainland territories are the Australian Capital Territory and which other?**
- A. Tasmania
- B. Norfolk Island
- C. The Northern Territory ✓
- D. Victoria
- *Explanation:* The two mainland territories are the Australian Capital Territory and the Northern Territory. Both have their own parliaments.

**20. Where does the Australian Parliament meet?**
- A. Old Parliament House, Melbourne
- B. Government House, Sydney
- C. The High Court building, Canberra
- D. Parliament House, Canberra ✓
- *Explanation:* The Australian Parliament meets at Parliament House in Canberra, which opened in 1988.

**21. The Australian Parliament consists of the King (represented by the Governor-General) and which two houses?**
- A. The House of Representatives and the Senate ✓
- B. The House of Commons and the House of Lords
- C. Congress and the Senate
- D. The Assembly and the Guildhall
- *Explanation:* The Australian Parliament has two houses: the House of Representatives and the Senate.

**22. Members of the House of Representatives are elected to represent what?**
- A. Whole states, equally
- B. Electorates — voting districts with roughly equal numbers of voters ✓
- C. Local councils
- D. Political parties only
- *Explanation:* Each member of the House of Representatives represents one electorate. Electorates have roughly equal numbers of voters.

**23. Why is the Senate sometimes called the 'States' House'?**
- A. Senators are the state premiers
- B. It meets in a different state each year
- C. Each state elects the same number of senators ✓
- D. Only states may watch its debates
- *Explanation:* In the Senate, every state — large or small — elects the same number of senators: twelve.

**24. How many senators does each Australian state elect?**
- A. Two
- B. Five
- C. Ten
- D. Twelve ✓
- *Explanation:* Each of the six states elects 12 senators, giving smaller states an equal voice in the Senate.

**25. How many senators does each mainland territory elect?**
- A. Two ✓
- B. Twelve
- C. None
- D. Twenty
- *Explanation:* The ACT and the Northern Territory each elect two senators, so there are 76 senators in total.

**26. How many senators are there in the Australian Parliament in total?**
- A. 50
- B. 76 ✓
- C. 100
- D. 151
- *Explanation:* There are 76 senators: 12 from each of the six states and two from each mainland territory.

**27. At a federal election, the party or coalition that wins a majority of seats in the House of Representatives does what?**
- A. Chooses the Governor-General
- B. Takes control of the courts
- C. Forms the government ✓
- D. Rewrites the Constitution
- *Explanation:* The party or coalition with a majority in the House of Representatives forms the government.

**28. The leader of the party that forms the Australian Government becomes what?**
- A. The Governor-General
- B. The Chief Justice
- C. The Speaker
- D. The Prime Minister ✓
- *Explanation:* The leader of the governing party becomes the Prime Minister, the head of the Australian Government.

**29. The Prime Minister is the head of what?**
- A. The Australian Government ✓
- B. The Australian court system
- C. Every state government
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* The Prime Minister is the head of the Australian Government. The head of state is the King, represented by the Governor-General.

**30. The largest party or coalition that is not in government is known as what?**
- A. The Reserve
- B. The Opposition ✓
- C. The Senate
- D. The Cabinet
- *Explanation:* The Opposition questions the government and offers voters an alternative at the next election.

**31. What is the Cabinet in the Australian Government?**
- A. A room where laws are stored
- B. The judges of the High Court
- C. The group of senior ministers who meet to make key government decisions ✓
- D. The staff of the Opposition
- *Explanation:* The Cabinet is the group of senior ministers, led by the Prime Minister, that makes the main decisions of government.

**32. What are government ministers responsible for?**
- A. Only their own electorates
- B. All state police forces
- C. The decisions of the courts
- D. Government departments and areas of work, such as health or defence ✓
- *Explanation:* Each minister is responsible for a department or area of government — for example health, education or defence.

**33. What is a proposed new law in Parliament called?**
- A. A Bill ✓
- B. A verdict
- C. A by-law
- D. A ballot
- *Explanation:* A proposed law is called a Bill. If it is passed by Parliament and signed by the Governor-General, it becomes a law (an Act).

**34. What must usually happen before a Bill becomes a law?**
- A. The King must write it personally
- B. It must be passed by both houses of Parliament and signed by the Governor-General ✓
- C. The High Court must vote for it
- D. Every local council must approve it
- *Explanation:* A Bill must be agreed to by both the House of Representatives and the Senate, then signed — given royal assent — by the Governor-General.

**35. When the Governor-General signs a Bill into law on behalf of the King, this is called what?**
- A. A pardon
- B. An election
- C. Royal assent ✓
- D. A referendum
- *Explanation:* Royal assent is the final step that turns a Bill passed by Parliament into a law.

**36. Which house of the Australian Parliament is often called the 'house of review'?**
- A. The House of Representatives
- B. A state Legislative Assembly
- C. A local council chamber
- D. The Senate ✓
- *Explanation:* The Senate reviews Bills passed by the House of Representatives, which is why it is called the house of review.

**37. Bills about raising or spending government money (Money Bills) must begin in which house?**
- A. The House of Representatives ✓
- B. The Senate
- C. A state parliament
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* Money Bills must start in the House of Representatives, the house where the government is formed.

**38. How often must elections for the House of Representatives be held?**
- A. Every year
- B. At least every three years ✓
- C. Every ten years
- D. Only when the Governor-General wishes
- *Explanation:* Members of the House of Representatives are elected for a maximum of three years, so elections are held at least every three years.

**39. For how long are senators normally elected?**
- A. One year
- B. Three years
- C. Six years ✓
- D. Life
- *Explanation:* Senators are elected for six years. Half of the state senators face election every three years.

**40. Which independent body runs federal elections in Australia?**
- A. The governing party
- B. The Australian Defence Force
- C. The state premiers
- D. The Australian Electoral Commission ✓
- *Explanation:* The Australian Electoral Commission (AEC) is the independent body that runs federal elections and referendums.

**41. What does the Australian Electoral Commission do?**
- A. Runs federal elections and maintains the electoral roll ✓
- B. Campaigns for the largest party
- C. Polices polling places as a party branch
- D. Broadcasts election advertising
- *Explanation:* The AEC keeps the electoral roll (the list of voters), runs elections and educates voters. It is independent of the government.

**42. Who must vote in Australian federal elections?**
- A. Only citizens born in Australia
- B. Enrolled Australian citizens aged 18 and over — voting is compulsory ✓
- C. Only people who own property
- D. Only men aged over 21
- *Explanation:* Voting is compulsory for Australian citizens aged 18 and over who are enrolled. It is a right and a responsibility.

**43. Before an Australian citizen can vote, their name must be on what?**
- A. A political party's membership list
- B. The telephone directory
- C. The electoral roll ✓
- D. A council rates notice
- *Explanation:* Citizens aged 18 and over must enrol — put their name on the electoral roll — so they can vote.

**44. Elections in Australia are decided by what method of voting?**
- A. Shouting out your choice
- B. A show of hands
- C. Telling a party official your choice
- D. A secret ballot ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians vote by secret ballot. Nobody can find out how another person voted.

**45. In an election for the House of Representatives, how do voters usually show their choice?**
- A. By numbering the candidates in their order of preference ✓
- B. By ticking only the sitting member
- C. By writing an essay
- D. By choosing a party colour
- *Explanation:* House of Representatives elections use preferential voting: voters number candidates 1, 2, 3 and so on in their preferred order.

**46. If a member of the House of Representatives resigns between general elections, how is the seat filled?**
- A. It stays empty until the next general election
- B. A by-election is held in that electorate ✓
- C. The Governor-General takes the seat
- D. The Senate takes the seat
- *Explanation:* A by-election is held in the electorate to choose a replacement member.

**47. Which court is the highest court in Australia?**
- A. The Federal Court
- B. A state Supreme Court
- C. The High Court of Australia ✓
- D. The Family Court
- *Explanation:* The High Court of Australia is the highest court in the country. Its decisions are final.

**48. What is the main role of the High Court of Australia?**
- A. To run federal elections
- B. To command the armed forces
- C. To set the rate of tax
- D. To interpret the Constitution and decide cases about its meaning ✓
- *Explanation:* The High Court interprets the Australian Constitution and settles disputes about what it means.

**49. Which of these statements about Australian judges is correct?**
- A. They are independent and governments cannot tell them how to decide cases ✓
- B. They are members of the governing party
- C. They are elected every year
- D. They take orders from the police
- *Explanation:* Judges and magistrates are independent of the government. They decide cases only on the law and the evidence.

**50. If a state law and an Australian (federal) law conflict, which law applies?**
- A. The state law always applies
- B. The federal law applies, under the Constitution ✓
- C. Both laws are cancelled
- D. The local council decides
- *Explanation:* Under the Constitution, if a valid federal law and a state law conflict, the federal law prevails.

**51. Which of these is a responsibility of the Australian Government?**
- A. Collecting household rubbish
- B. Running local libraries
- C. Citizenship and immigration ✓
- D. Repairing local roads
- *Explanation:* The Australian Government is responsible for citizenship and immigration, as well as defence, trade and currency.

**52. Which of these is mainly a state government responsibility?**
- A. Printing money
- B. Foreign affairs
- C. Defence
- D. Public hospitals ✓
- *Explanation:* State and territory governments run public hospitals, schools, police and public transport.

**53. Medicare, Australia's public health care system, is funded mainly by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government ✓
- B. Local councils
- C. Private sports clubs
- D. Each hospital on its own
- *Explanation:* The Australian Government funds Medicare, which helps all Australians with the cost of health care.

**54. Who is responsible for the police force in each state?**
- A. The Australian Government alone
- B. The state government ✓
- C. The local mayor personally
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* Each state and territory has its own police force, responsible to its state or territory government.

**55. As well as state police, Australia has a national police force. What is it called?**
- A. The Federal Bureau
- B. The Commonwealth Guard
- C. The Australian Federal Police ✓
- D. The National Constabulary
- *Explanation:* The Australian Federal Police (AFP) handles federal crimes and also provides policing for the ACT.

**56. How does the Australian Government pay for national services such as defence, health and education?**
- A. From donations only
- B. From fines only
- C. By borrowing from local councils
- D. Mainly from taxes ✓
- *Explanation:* Governments raise most of their money from taxes, which pay for defence, health, education and other services.

**57. Who decides the laws about taxation in Australia?**
- A. The Parliament, by passing laws ✓
- B. The Tax Office acting alone
- C. The Governor-General personally
- D. State mayors
- *Explanation:* Tax laws are made by Parliament. The Australian Taxation Office administers them, but cannot invent taxes.

**58. Which of these services is provided by local government?**
- A. The Australian Defence Force
- B. Local parks, swimming pools, libraries and rubbish collection ✓
- C. Passports
- D. The national currency
- *Explanation:* Local councils provide local parks, pools, libraries, childcare, rubbish collection and local road maintenance.

**59. Most state parliaments — except Queensland and the territories — have how many houses?**
- A. None
- B. Three
- C. Two — an upper house and a lower house ✓
- D. Five
- *Explanation:* Most states have two houses: a Legislative Assembly (lower house) and a Legislative Council (upper house).

**60. Which state parliament has only one house?**
- A. New South Wales
- B. Victoria
- C. Tasmania
- D. Queensland ✓
- *Explanation:* Queensland abolished its upper house in 1922 and has had a single-house parliament ever since.

**61. The Legislative Assembly of the ACT governs which territory?**
- A. The Australian Capital Territory ✓
- B. The Northern Territory
- C. Tasmania
- D. Norfolk Island
- *Explanation:* The ACT Legislative Assembly is the parliament of the Australian Capital Territory, based in Canberra.

**62. A person elected to a state parliament is responsible for what?**
- A. Sitting in the federal Senate
- B. Making laws for that state, on state matters ✓
- C. Automatically joining the federal Cabinet
- D. Judging cases in the High Court
- *Explanation:* State members of parliament make laws for their state on state matters like schools, hospitals and police.

**63. What is a jury in the Australian legal system?**
- A. A group of judges
- B. The prosecutors in every case
- C. A group of citizens who decide the facts in a serious court case ✓
- D. Lawyers who work for the government
- *Explanation:* A jury is a group of ordinary citizens who hear the evidence in a serious case and decide whether the accused is guilty.

**64. Being called to serve on a jury in Australia is what?**
- A. A form of punishment
- B. A job only for lawyers
- C. An honour only for celebrities
- D. A civic responsibility of citizens ✓
- *Explanation:* Jury service is an important civic responsibility. Citizens may be called to serve on juries in serious court cases.

**65. Which of these is a criminal offence in Australia?**
- A. Offering a bribe to a public official ✓
- B. Writing a letter to a public official
- C. Voting against the official's party
- D. Criticising an official peacefully
- *Explanation:* Bribery is a serious crime. Public officials must act honestly and in the public interest.

**66. If you are arrested in Australia, which of these rights do you have?**
- A. The right to refuse to give your name in every situation
- B. The right to remain silent and to contact a lawyer ✓
- C. The right to choose your own judge
- D. The right to leave the country at once
- *Explanation:* A person who is arrested has the right to remain silent and to seek legal advice before answering questions.

**67. What does legal aid in Australia provide?**
- A. Free holidays for lawyers
- B. Loans to government ministers
- C. Legal help for people who cannot afford a lawyer ✓
- D. Advice only to people living overseas
- *Explanation:* Legal aid services help people who cannot afford a lawyer, so that everyone can get a fair hearing.

**68. If a person in court does not understand English well, how can they be helped?**
- A. They cannot be helped
- B. A family member may shout translations
- C. The jury translates for them
- D. They can be given the help of an interpreter ✓
- *Explanation:* Courts can arrange interpreters so that everyone can understand the case and give their evidence fairly.

**69. What is the main purpose of Australia's laws?**
- A. To protect the community and the rights of everyone in it ✓
- B. To make life difficult for citizens
- C. To please the King
- D. To give Parliament something to debate
- *Explanation:* Laws keep the community safe, protect people's rights and freedoms, and set standards of behaviour for everyone.

**70. As the King's representative, the Governor-General is also formally what?**
- A. The head of the Federal Police
- B. The Commander-in-Chief of the Australian Defence Force ✓
- C. The owner of the armed forces
- D. The judge of all court cases
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General is the Commander-in-Chief of the Australian Defence Force, acting on the advice of the government.

**71. The Governor-General appoints government ministers on the advice of whom?**
- A. The Chief Justice
- B. The Senate
- C. The Prime Minister ✓
- D. The state governors
- *Explanation:* Acting on the Prime Minister's advice, the Governor-General formally appoints ministers to their portfolios.

**72. What is the role of the Speaker of the House of Representatives?**
- A. To command the army
- B. To lead the Opposition
- C. To write every Bill alone
- D. To chair the meetings of the House and keep them fair and orderly ✓
- *Explanation:* The Speaker runs the meetings of the House of Representatives, making sure debate is orderly and the rules are followed.

**73. The Leader of the Opposition leads which group?**
- A. The largest party or coalition not in government ✓
- B. The clerks of the Senate
- C. The public service
- D. All local councils
- *Explanation:* The Opposition, led by the Leader of the Opposition, holds the government to account and offers an alternative government.

**74. In Australian politics, what is a 'coalition'?**
- A. A type of election
- B. When two or more parties join together to govern or to form an opposition ✓
- C. A meeting of judges
- D. A public holiday
- *Explanation:* A coalition is an alliance of parties that join together, usually to form a government or the Opposition.

**75. What do government departments (the public service) do?**
- A. Make laws entirely on their own
- B. Elect the Prime Minister
- C. Carry out the decisions of the government and deliver its services ✓
- D. Run election campaigns for ministers
- *Explanation:* The public service advises the government and puts its decisions into practice, delivering services to the community.

**76. Which of these is a power of the Australian Government under the Constitution?**
- A. Running local libraries
- B. Issuing local building permits
- C. Writing state school curriculums
- D. Trade with other countries and foreign affairs ✓
- *Explanation:* The Constitution gives the Australian Government power over trade, foreign affairs, defence, immigration and currency.

**77. Powers that the Constitution does not give to the Australian Government generally belong to whom?**
- A. The states ✓
- B. The King
- C. Local councils
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* The states keep the powers they had at Federation, except for those the Constitution gives to the Commonwealth.

**78. Why does Australia divide government powers among three levels?**
- A. To confuse voters at elections
- B. To share responsibility and prevent any one level from having all the power ✓
- C. To create more politicians
- D. To give the King more duties
- *Explanation:* Sharing power between levels of government is a safeguard: no single level controls everything.

**79. In federal elections, voters in each voting district elect a member to the House of Representatives. What are these districts called?**
- A. States
- B. Shires
- C. Electorates ✓
- D. Parishes
- *Explanation:* Australia is divided into electorates (also called divisions). Each electorate elects one member of the House of Representatives.

**80. How many members does each federal electorate elect to the House of Representatives?**
- A. Two
- B. Five
- C. Twelve
- D. One ✓
- *Explanation:* Each electorate elects exactly one member to the House of Representatives.

**81. Why do federal electorates have roughly equal numbers of voters?**
- A. So that every vote carries about the same weight ✓
- B. To save paper
- C. So that big cities can dominate
- D. Because the King requires it
- *Explanation:* Electorates with similar numbers of voters mean each person's vote has about the same value.

**82. Most state elections in Australia are held how often?**
- A. Every year
- B. About every four years ✓
- C. Every ten years
- D. Twice a year
- *Explanation:* Most states now have fixed terms of about four years between elections.

**83. Who is allowed to enrol and vote in Australian elections?**
- A. Any visitor to Australia
- B. Permanent residents of any nationality
- C. Australian citizens aged 18 and over ✓
- D. Anyone aged 16 and over
- *Explanation:* Only Australian citizens aged 18 and over may enrol and vote in federal and state elections.

**84. What is compulsory for enrolled Australian citizens at election time?**
- A. Joining a political party
- B. Donating money to a campaign
- C. Putting up election posters
- D. Voting — turning up, having their name marked off and receiving a ballot paper ✓
- *Explanation:* Voting is compulsory in Australia. Enrolled citizens must attend a polling place (or vote by post or pre-poll) at each election.

**85. What can happen if an enrolled citizen does not vote and has no valid reason?**
- A. They may have to pay a fine ✓
- B. They lose their citizenship
- C. They go to prison for a year
- D. Absolutely nothing, ever
- *Explanation:* A person who fails to vote without a valid and sufficient reason can be fined.

**86. Why do postal voting and pre-poll voting exist?**
- A. So parties can check how people voted
- B. So people who cannot attend a polling place on election day can still vote ✓
- C. So counting can be kept secret from voters
- D. So only older people can vote
- *Explanation:* Postal and early (pre-poll) voting make sure people who are travelling, ill or working on election day can still have their say.

**87. On election day in Australia, polling places are usually found where?**
- A. Only in police stations
- B. Only in private homes
- C. In schools and public halls all over the country ✓
- D. Only on army bases
- *Explanation:* On election day, polling places open in schools, halls and other public buildings across Australia.

**88. Who counts the votes after a federal election?**
- A. The candidates themselves
- B. Volunteers chosen by the parties
- C. Television networks
- D. Officials of the Australian Electoral Commission ✓
- *Explanation:* Votes are counted by independent officials of the Australian Electoral Commission, watched by candidates' scrutineers.

**89. When people in a state vote in a Senate election, they are voting for whom?**
- A. Senators to represent their whole state ✓
- B. One member for their local electorate
- C. Their town mayor
- D. Judges of the state court
- *Explanation:* In Senate elections, voters choose senators to represent the whole state or territory, not a single local area.

**90. Why does every state elect the same number of senators, whatever its size?**
- A. So that big states always win
- B. So that smaller states have an equal voice in the Senate ✓
- C. So that territories control the Senate
- D. To make elections cheaper
- *Explanation:* Equal Senate representation for each state protects the smaller states. It was a key condition of Federation.

**91. What is a 'minority government'?**
- A. A government ruled by the Governor-General alone
- B. A government in which only minorities may vote
- C. A government whose party does not hold a majority of seats alone and needs the support of others to govern ✓
- D. A government formed after an election is cancelled
- *Explanation:* In a minority government, the governing party relies on the support of independents or smaller parties to pass laws.

**92. The Australian Government raises most of its revenue from what?**
- A. Selling national parks
- B. Traffic fines
- C. Donations from other countries
- D. Taxes, such as income tax and the goods and services tax (GST) ✓
- *Explanation:* Taxes — mainly income tax and the GST — provide most of the money the government spends on services.

**93. What is the GST in Australia?**
- A. A broad-based tax of 10 per cent on most goods and services ✓
- B. A state driver's licence fee
- C. A local council rate
- D. A type of visa charge
- *Explanation:* The GST is a 10 per cent tax on most goods and services. The money is distributed to the states and territories.

**94. Revenue collected by the Australian Government is shared with the states mainly to help fund what?**
- A. Political parties
- B. Public services such as hospitals and schools ✓
- C. Private companies
- D. The royal household
- *Explanation:* Commonwealth payments help the states and territories fund hospitals, schools, roads and other services.

**95. Criminal law — laws about offences such as theft and assault — is mainly made and enforced by which level of government?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The United Nations
- C. The states and territories ✓
- D. The Commonwealth alone
- *Explanation:* Most criminal law is state and territory law, enforced by state and territory police.

**96. What is everyone's duty regarding Australian laws?**
- A. To obey only the laws they agree with
- B. To obey only state laws
- C. To obey only laws made after 2000
- D. To obey all Australian laws, whether or not they agree with them ✓
- *Explanation:* Everyone in Australia must obey the law, even if they disagree with it. They may campaign peacefully to change it.

**97. New laws passed in Australia apply to whom?**
- A. Everyone in Australia ✓
- B. Only people who voted for the government
- C. Only new citizens
- D. Only people living in Canberra
- *Explanation:* New laws apply to everyone in Australia equally, from the day they start.

**98. State police and the Australian Federal Police work together in order to do what?**
- A. Write new laws
- B. Enforce the law across Australia ✓
- C. Choose the government
- D. Run federal elections
- *Explanation:* Australian police forces cooperate across borders to enforce the law and keep the community safe.

**99. What travel document does the Australian Government issue to its citizens for overseas travel?**
- A. Nothing — citizens cannot travel
- B. Visas to enter Australia
- C. Australian passports ✓
- D. International driving licences
- *Explanation:* The Australian Government issues Australian passports to citizens for travel overseas.

**100. Citizenship — including citizenship ceremonies — is the responsibility of which level of government?**
- A. Local councils only
- B. The state governments
- C. The courts
- D. The Australian Government ✓
- *Explanation:* Citizenship is a national matter, handled by the Australian Government. Councils often host the ceremonies.

**101. Since Federation, how successful have proposals to change the Constitution been at referendums?**
- A. Only a few have succeeded — less than a fifth of all proposals ✓
- B. Almost all of them have succeeded
- C. About half have succeeded
- D. None has ever succeeded
- *Explanation:* Referendums rarely succeed. More than 40 proposals have been put to the voters since 1901, but only eight have been approved.

**102. Who votes at a referendum in Australia?**
- A. Only members of Parliament
- B. All Australian citizens on the electoral roll ✓
- C. Only people who own land
- D. Only voters in Canberra
- *Explanation:* At a referendum, every enrolled Australian citizen votes, just as at an election.

**103. Is voting at a referendum compulsory for enrolled Australians?**
- A. No — referendums are voluntary
- B. Only in some states
- C. Yes — voting at a referendum is compulsory, like an election ✓
- D. Only for new citizens
- *Explanation:* Voting at a referendum is compulsory for enrolled citizens, the same as at an election.

**104. In the 1999 referendum, Australians were asked whether Australia should become a republic. What was the result?**
- A. Voters said yes and Australia became a republic
- B. The vote was cancelled
- C. Only Tasmania voted
- D. Voters said no — Australia stayed a constitutional monarchy ✓
- *Explanation:* In 1999, most voters said no to the republic proposal, so Australia remained a constitutional monarchy.

**105. Before Federation, how did the people of the colonies show they agreed to the new Constitution?**
- A. They voted to approve it at referendums held in the colonies ✓
- B. The King decided for them
- C. The army ordered it
- D. Only politicians voted
- *Explanation:* During the 1890s the draft Constitution was put to the people of the colonies, who voted in referendums to approve it.

**106. The Australian Constitution was originally part of an Act passed by which parliament?**
- A. The French Parliament
- B. The British Parliament, in 1900 ✓
- C. The New South Wales Parliament alone
- D. The United Nations
- *Explanation:* The Constitution was passed as part of a British Act of Parliament in 1900 and began on 1 January 1901.

**107. What did the Australia Act 1986 do?**
- A. It created the Senate
- B. It made Canberra the capital
- C. It ended the power of the British Parliament to make laws for Australia ✓
- D. It introduced the GST
- *Explanation:* The Australia Act 1986 removed the last powers of the British Parliament and British courts over Australia.

**108. What is the main job of the Australian Constitution?**
- A. It lists every law in Australia
- B. It names the Prime Minister
- C. It is a history book about Federation
- D. It sets out how Australia is governed, including the powers of the Parliament, the Executive, the courts and the states ✓
- *Explanation:* The Constitution is the framework for government: it creates the Parliament, the Executive and the Judicature, and divides power between the Commonwealth and the states.

**109. Australia's system of government is called a 'federation'. What does this mean?**
- A. Power is shared between one central government and the governments of the states ✓
- B. One state rules the others
- C. The army shares power with Parliament
- D. There is no central government
- *Explanation:* In a federation, power is divided between a central (national) government and regional governments — in Australia, the states.

**110. Under the separation of powers, what does the Judiciary (the courts) do?**
- A. Makes the laws
- B. Interprets the law and settles legal disputes in the courts ✓
- C. Carries out the laws
- D. Runs elections
- *Explanation:* The Parliament makes laws, the Executive carries them out, and the Judiciary interprets them and decides disputes.

**111. Why are the powers of government separated between different bodies in Australia?**
- A. To give politicians more jobs
- B. Because the King ordered it in 1901
- C. So that no single person or body has all the power — each part checks the others ✓
- D. To make elections cheaper
- *Explanation:* Separating the powers of Parliament, the Executive and the courts stops any one part of government becoming too powerful.

**112. Under the Constitution, in whom is the executive power of the Commonwealth formally vested?**
- A. In the High Court
- B. In the Leader of the Opposition
- C. In state premiers
- D. In the King, and it is exercised by the Governor-General ✓
- *Explanation:* The Constitution vests executive power in the King. In practice the Governor-General exercises it on the advice of ministers.

**113. What can the High Court do if it finds that a law made by a parliament goes beyond the powers in the Constitution?**
- A. Declare the law invalid ✓
- B. Rewrite the law itself
- C. Fine the Prime Minister
- D. Call a new election
- *Explanation:* The High Court can declare a law invalid (unconstitutional) if the parliament had no power to make it.

**114. What is the Federal Executive Council?**
- A. A committee of the Senate
- B. The formal body in which the Governor-General, acting on the advice of ministers, makes executive decisions ✓
- C. The staff of the High Court
- D. A council of state premiers
- *Explanation:* The Federal Executive Council is made up of the Governor-General and government ministers. It formally gives effect to executive decisions.

**115. Before a referendum can be held, the proposed change must normally be what?**
- A. Approved by the King personally
- B. Signed by all state premiers
- C. Passed by the federal Parliament and then put to the people ✓
- D. Published in a newspaper for a year
- *Explanation:* In most cases the federal Parliament first passes the proposed change, and then the people vote on it at a referendum.

**116. If a referendum proposal is not approved by the voters, what happens?**
- A. Parliament can change the Constitution anyway
- B. The King can change it
- C. The High Court changes it
- D. The Constitution is not changed ✓
- *Explanation:* If the voters say no, the Constitution stays as it is. There is no other way to change it.

**117. For a referendum to pass, a majority of voters must say yes in at least how many of the six states?**
- A. Four states ✓
- B. Two states
- C. All six states
- D. One state
- *Explanation:* The 'double majority' requires a national majority plus a majority in a majority of states — that is, at least four of the six states.

**118. In a referendum, how do votes from the Australian Capital Territory and the Northern Territory count?**
- A. They do not count at all
- B. They count in the national total, but not in the count of states ✓
- C. They count as two extra states
- D. They are counted twice
- *Explanation:* Territory votes help decide the national majority, but the territories are not counted among the 'majority of states'.

**119. Before a referendum, what official information is sent to voters?**
- A. Nothing is sent
- B. Only the government's opinion
- C. A pamphlet with arguments for the 'Yes' case and the 'No' case ✓
- D. A copy of the whole Constitution
- *Explanation:* Voters receive an official pamphlet setting out the arguments for voting 'Yes' and for voting 'No'.

**120. Which of these subjects does the Constitution give the Commonwealth Parliament power to make laws about?**
- A. Public schools
- B. Local roads
- C. State hospitals
- D. Marriage ✓
- *Explanation:* The Constitution lists the Commonwealth's law-making powers, including marriage, defence, trade, immigration and currency.

**121. Which of these is also a power of the Commonwealth Parliament under the Constitution?**
- A. Currency and coinage ✓
- B. Town planning
- C. State police forces
- D. Local libraries
- *Explanation:* Coinage and currency are Commonwealth powers. Libraries, planning and police are state or local matters.

**122. Taxation is an example of a 'concurrent' power. What does this mean?**
- A. Only the Commonwealth can tax
- B. Both the Commonwealth and the states can make tax laws, but a valid Commonwealth law prevails if they conflict ✓
- C. Only the states can tax
- D. Nobody is allowed to tax
- *Explanation:* Concurrent powers are shared. Both levels may use them, but if their laws conflict, the Commonwealth law prevails.

**123. Under the Constitution, customs and excise duties may be charged only by whom?**
- A. The states
- B. Local councils
- C. The Commonwealth ✓
- D. Private companies
- *Explanation:* Customs and excise are exclusive Commonwealth powers — the states gave them up at Federation.

**124. As well as the national Constitution, what does each Australian state have?**
- A. Its own king
- B. Its own currency
- C. Its own army
- D. Its own state constitution ✓
- *Explanation:* Each state kept its own constitution at Federation, setting out how the state parliament and government work.

**125. In which year did Australians first vote in an election for the new federal Parliament?**
- A. 1901 ✓
- B. 1788
- C. 1851
- D. 1945
- *Explanation:* The first federal elections were held in March 1901, soon after Federation.

**126. Who chairs meetings of the Senate and keeps order there?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. The President of the Senate ✓
- C. The Chief Justice
- D. The Governor-General
- *Explanation:* The President of the Senate chairs its meetings, much as the Speaker chairs the House of Representatives.

**127. What is Question Time in the Australian Parliament?**
- A. Time set aside for school visits
- B. When the public questions the King
- C. A regular time when members ask ministers questions about government actions ✓
- D. A quiz held for new members
- *Explanation:* At Question Time, members of Parliament — especially from the Opposition — question ministers about the government's work.

**128. What is Hansard?**
- A. The rule book for elections
- B. A list of all Australian laws
- C. The Parliament's budget
- D. The official written record of what is said in Parliament ✓
- *Explanation:* Hansard is the official report of debates in Parliament. Anyone can read it.

**129. What is the main work of parliamentary committees?**
- A. To examine Bills and important issues in detail, hearing evidence from experts and the public ✓
- B. To count votes on election day
- C. To choose government ministers
- D. To run the courts
- *Explanation:* Committees of members and senators study Bills and issues closely and report back to the Parliament.

**130. What happens at the first reading of a Bill in Parliament?**
- A. The Bill becomes law
- B. The Bill is introduced and its title is read out ✓
- C. The Governor-General signs it
- D. The people vote on it
- *Explanation:* At the first reading, a Bill is introduced. Debate on its ideas happens at the second reading stage.

**131. If the Senate passes a Bill but changes (amends) parts of it, what happens next?**
- A. The Bill is thrown away
- B. The King rewrites the Bill
- C. The Bill goes back to the House of Representatives to agree to the changes ✓
- D. The changes are ignored
- *Explanation:* Both houses must agree on exactly the same text before a Bill can be signed into law, so amendments go back to the other house.

**132. What happens if the Senate votes against (rejects) a Bill?**
- A. It becomes law anyway
- B. The House can sign it alone
- C. The Governor-General can pass it alone
- D. The Bill does not become law ✓
- *Explanation:* A Bill must pass both houses. If the Senate rejects it, it cannot become law in that form.

**133. In a 'double dissolution' election, what is dissolved (ended) by the Governor-General?**
- A. Both houses of Parliament — the House of Representatives and the whole Senate ✓
- B. Only the Cabinet
- C. All state parliaments
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* A double dissolution ends both houses at once and is used when the two houses are deadlocked over a Bill.

**134. After a double dissolution election, if the two houses still disagree on a Bill, what can happen?**
- A. The King decides the matter
- B. Both houses can meet together in a 'joint sitting' to vote on the Bill ✓
- C. The Bill is given to the states
- D. A referendum is held at once
- *Explanation:* After a double dissolution, a joint sitting of all members and senators can finally decide the deadlocked Bill.

**135. The Senate chamber in Parliament House is decorated mainly in which colour?**
- A. Green
- B. Blue
- C. Red ✓
- D. Gold
- *Explanation:* The Senate chamber is red and the House of Representatives chamber is green, following the Westminster tradition.

**136. What flies day and night above Parliament House in Canberra?**
- A. The flag of the United Nations
- B. The Union Jack only
- C. A flag for each state premier
- D. A very large Australian National Flag ✓
- *Explanation:* A huge Australian National Flag flies above Parliament House. It is lit at night and can be seen across Canberra.

**137. Before the present Parliament House opened in 1988, where did the federal Parliament meet in Canberra?**
- A. In the building now called Old Parliament House, from 1927 ✓
- B. In Sydney Town Hall
- C. In a building in Melbourne only
- D. In the High Court building
- *Explanation:* The federal Parliament met in what is now Old Parliament House from 1927 until the new Parliament House opened in 1988.

**138. What is 'The Lodge' in Canberra?**
- A. The home of the Chief Justice
- B. The official residence of the Prime Minister ✓
- C. A building where senators stay
- D. The national library
- *Explanation:* The Lodge, in Canberra, is the Prime Minister's official residence. There is also a Sydney residence, Kirribilli House.

**139. Government House in Canberra (Yarralumla) is the official residence of whom?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. The President of the Senate
- C. The Governor-General ✓
- D. The Leader of the Opposition
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General lives and works at Government House, Canberra, and also has an official residence in Sydney.

**140. Government ministers in Australia are chosen from where?**
- A. From judges of the High Court
- B. From state premiers only
- C. From senior police officers
- D. From the members of the Parliament (senators and members of the House of Representatives) ✓
- *Explanation:* Ministers are members of Parliament. The Constitution requires a minister who is not yet a member to become one within three months.

**141. In Australia's system of 'responsible government', ministers must do what?**
- A. Answer to the Parliament for the work of their departments ✓
- B. Answer only to the King
- C. Never speak in Parliament
- D. Obey the courts in all policy matters
- *Explanation:* Ministers are responsible to the Parliament: they must explain and defend their departments' actions, for example at Question Time.

**142. Who is the Auditor-General?**
- A. The government's chief lawyer
- B. An independent officer who checks how the Australian Government spends public money ✓
- C. The head of the army
- D. A minister chosen by the Senate
- *Explanation:* The Auditor-General independently audits government departments and reports to the Parliament on how public money is used.

**143. What does the Commonwealth Ombudsman do?**
- A. Runs federal elections
- B. Commands the Federal Police
- C. Investigates complaints about Australian Government departments and agencies ✓
- D. Writes the budget
- *Explanation:* The Ombudsman looks into complaints that a government department has treated someone unfairly.

**144. How can ordinary citizens formally ask the Parliament to take action on an issue?**
- A. By refusing to pay tax
- B. By writing to the King only
- C. By closing a public road
- D. By presenting a petition to Parliament ✓
- *Explanation:* Any citizen may petition the Parliament. Petitions are presented by a member and recorded by the Parliament.

**145. What is a royal commission?**
- A. A major public inquiry set up by the government to investigate an important matter ✓
- B. A meeting of the Cabinet
- C. A type of court case
- D. A ceremony at Parliament House
- *Explanation:* Royal commissions are powerful public inquiries. They hear evidence and report findings and recommendations to the government.

**146. Before a general election for the House of Representatives, who formally dissolves the House?**
- A. The Speaker
- B. The Governor-General, on the advice of the Prime Minister ✓
- C. The High Court
- D. The President of the Senate
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General dissolves the House of Representatives and issues the writs for an election, acting on the Prime Minister's advice.

**147. For about how long does a Governor-General usually serve?**
- A. For life
- B. One year
- C. Usually about five years ✓
- D. Twenty years
- *Explanation:* Governors-General are usually appointed for about five years, although the King can extend the term.

**148. Members of Parliament who are not ministers, shadow ministers or office-holders are commonly called what?**
- A. Scrutineers
- B. Magistrates
- C. Councillors
- D. Backbenchers ✓
- *Explanation:* Ordinary members without a special role are known as backbenchers, because they sit on the back benches of the chamber.

**149. When Parliament is not sitting in Canberra, what do most members of Parliament mainly do?**
- A. Work in their own electorate, meeting and helping local people ✓
- B. Take holidays overseas
- C. Sit as judges
- D. Work in state parliaments
- *Explanation:* Members spend much of their time in their electorate office, helping local people and community groups.

**150. The House of Representatives is sometimes called the 'people's house'. Why?**
- A. Anyone may walk in and vote on laws
- B. Its members are elected directly by the people in electorates across Australia ✓
- C. It is open to tourists every day
- D. The people built the building
- *Explanation:* The House is called the people's house because every member is chosen directly by voters in an electorate.

**151. Federal elections in Australia are always held on which day of the week?**
- A. Monday
- B. Sunday
- C. Saturday ✓
- D. Wednesday
- *Explanation:* By law, federal elections are held on a Saturday so that most people can get to a polling place.

**152. At what time do polling places close on election day?**
- A. Midnight
- B. 5 pm
- C. 9 pm
- D. 6 pm ✓
- *Explanation:* Polls close at 6 pm local time, and counting begins straight away.

**153. What are 'writs' at the start of a federal election?**
- A. Official documents that order an election to be held ✓
- B. Letters from voters
- C. Court orders against candidates
- D. Election posters
- *Explanation:* An election formally begins when writs (official orders) are issued. They set the key dates, including polling day.

**154. Who advises the Governor-General to call a federal election?**
- A. The Chief Justice
- B. The Prime Minister ✓
- C. The state premiers together
- D. The Australian Electoral Commission
- *Explanation:* The Prime Minister advises the Governor-General when to dissolve the House and call an election, within the three-year limit.

**155. What is an 'informal' vote?**
- A. A vote cast by post
- B. A vote made early
- C. A ballot paper that is not filled in correctly, so it cannot be counted ✓
- D. A vote by a young person
- *Explanation:* A ballot paper that is blank, wrongly numbered or marked in a way that does not show the voter's intention is informal and is not counted.

**156. What colour is the ballot paper for the House of Representatives?**
- A. White
- B. Yellow
- C. Pink
- D. Green ✓
- *Explanation:* The House of Representatives ballot paper is green. The much larger Senate ballot paper is white.

**157. On the large Senate ballot paper, voters may vote in which way?**
- A. 'Above the line' for a party or group, or 'below the line' for individual candidates ✓
- B. By writing an essay
- C. Only for one candidate with a tick
- D. By telephone
- *Explanation:* Senate voters can vote above the line for a group, or below the line by numbering individual candidates in order of preference.

**158. Senate elections use 'proportional representation'. What does this mean?**
- A. The biggest party wins all seats
- B. Each party wins a number of seats that matches its share of the vote ✓
- C. Every candidate gets the same number of votes
- D. The Governor-General divides the seats
- *Explanation:* Under proportional representation, a party with about a third of the vote wins about a third of the seats being contested.

**159. In a House of Representatives election, if no candidate has more than half the first-preference votes, what happens?**
- A. The election is cancelled
- B. The sitting member wins automatically
- C. The preferences of the lowest candidates are distributed until one candidate has a majority ✓
- D. The Governor-General chooses the winner
- *Explanation:* Counting continues by distributing the preferences of the candidates with the fewest votes, until one candidate has more than half the votes.

**160. Which voting system is NOT used in Australian federal elections?**
- A. Preferential voting
- B. Proportional representation
- C. Secret ballot
- D. 'First past the post' — winning with the most votes but less than half ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia does not use 'first past the post'. Winners must gain majority support through preferences or proportional counting.

**161. At what age can an Australian citizen put their name on the electoral roll (enrol), even though they cannot vote yet?**
- A. 16 or 17 years old — voting starts at 18 ✓
- B. 12 years old
- C. Only at 21
- D. There is no early enrolment
- *Explanation:* Citizens aged 16 or 17 may enrol provisionally. They can vote as soon as they turn 18.

**162. What must you do about your enrolment when you move to a new address?**
- A. Nothing — it never changes
- B. Update your enrolment details with the AEC ✓
- C. Ask the local council for permission
- D. Enrol again in every election year
- *Explanation:* You must update your address on the electoral roll so you are enrolled for the correct electorate.

**163. How can an Australian citizen who is overseas on election day vote?**
- A. They cannot vote at all
- B. By asking a friend to vote for them
- C. At an Australian embassy or by postal vote ✓
- D. Only by flying home
- *Explanation:* Australians overseas can vote at many embassies and high commissions, or apply for a postal vote.

**164. Who are 'scrutineers' at an election count?**
- A. AEC officials who count votes
- B. Police who guard ballot boxes
- C. Journalists who report results
- D. People appointed by candidates to watch the counting of votes ✓
- *Explanation:* Scrutineers watch the count on behalf of candidates to make sure everything is done properly and fairly.

**165. What is a 'redistribution' of electoral boundaries?**
- A. The redrawing of electorate boundaries by the AEC so each electorate keeps roughly the same number of voters ✓
- B. Giving seats to another state
- C. Moving polling places
- D. Changing election day
- *Explanation:* As populations change, boundaries are redrawn so that electorates stay roughly equal — one vote, one value.

**166. In a normal half-Senate election, only some Senate seats are contested. In a double dissolution election, what is different?**
- A. No Senate seats are contested
- B. All Senate seats are up for election ✓
- C. Only territory seats are contested
- D. Senators are chosen by state parliaments
- *Explanation:* A double dissolution puts every Senate seat — all 12 from each state — up for election at once.

**167. If a senator resigns in the middle of their term, how is the vacancy usually filled?**
- A. A by-election is held across the state
- B. The seat stays empty for six years
- C. The parliament of that senator's state chooses a replacement ✓
- D. The Governor-General takes the seat
- *Explanation:* A casual Senate vacancy is filled by the state parliament choosing a replacement senator, normally from the same party.

**168. After a normal election, the six-year terms of state senators begin on which date?**
- A. 1 January
- B. 26 January
- C. Election day itself
- D. 1 July ✓
- *Explanation:* State senators' terms run from 1 July. Territory senators' terms are linked to the House of Representatives.

**169. When counting is finished, who formally declares which candidates have been elected?**
- A. The Australian Electoral Commission ✓
- B. The winning party
- C. Television presenters
- D. The Prime Minister
- *Explanation:* AEC returning officers officially declare the results in each electorate and state.

**170. Voting is compulsory not only at federal elections. It is also compulsory at what?**
- A. Club elections
- B. State and territory elections ✓
- C. School elections
- D. Union elections
- *Explanation:* Voting is compulsory at federal, state and territory elections for enrolled citizens.

**171. As well as voting itself, what else is compulsory for Australian citizens aged 18 and over?**
- A. Joining a party
- B. Donating to a campaign
- C. Enrolling — putting your name on the electoral roll ✓
- D. Watching the election coverage
- *Explanation:* Enrolment is compulsory. Once enrolled, you must vote at each election or have a valid reason.

**172. What are 'mobile polling' teams?**
- A. Teams that move polling places during the day
- B. Party workers with loudspeakers
- C. Police patrols on election day
- D. AEC teams that take voting to hospitals, nursing homes and remote areas ✓
- *Explanation:* Mobile polling lets people vote where they are — for example patients in hospital and residents of aged-care homes.

**173. To nominate as a candidate for the federal Parliament, a person must be what?**
- A. An Australian citizen aged 18 or over ✓
- B. A member of the government party
- C. Born in Australia
- D. A former minister
- *Explanation:* Any Australian citizen aged 18 or over may nominate as a candidate. Voters then decide.

**174. As well as senators, what do voters in the territories elect at a federal election?**
- A. Their own governor
- B. Members of the House of Representatives for their territory electorates ✓
- C. Judges of the High Court
- D. Members of state parliaments
- *Explanation:* The ACT and the Northern Territory also elect members to the House of Representatives.

**175. Why does Australia use preferential voting for the House of Representatives?**
- A. So counting is faster
- B. So big parties always win
- C. So the winning candidate has the support of a majority of voters in the electorate ✓
- D. Because the King required it
- *Explanation:* Preferential voting means the winner is the candidate preferred by a majority of voters after all preferences are counted.

**176. A state Governor is appointed by the King on the advice of whom?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. The Governor-General
- C. The Chief Justice
- D. The Premier of that state ✓
- *Explanation:* State Governors are appointed on the advice of the state Premier, just as the Governor-General is appointed on the Prime Minister's advice.

**177. Who gives royal assent to Bills passed by a state parliament?**
- A. The Governor of the state ✓
- B. The Governor-General
- C. The Premier personally
- D. The King in person
- *Explanation:* A state Bill becomes law when the state Governor signs it — gives royal assent — on behalf of the King.

**178. The Premier of a state is the leader of what?**
- A. The state police force
- B. The party or group with a majority in the lower house of the state parliament ✓
- C. The Legislative Council
- D. All local councils in the state
- *Explanation:* The Premier leads the government in a state, in the same way the Prime Minister leads the Australian Government.

**179. What document sets out how each state is governed?**
- A. A by-law of the capital city council
- B. The federal budget
- C. The state's own constitution ✓
- D. The Governor's handbook
- *Explanation:* Each state has its own constitution, which sets out its parliament, executive and courts.

**180. The parliament of the Northern Territory, the Legislative Assembly, is different from most state parliaments because it has what?**
- A. No elected members
- B. Three houses
- C. Members chosen by the King
- D. Only one house ✓
- *Explanation:* The Northern Territory and ACT parliaments each have a single chamber, like the Queensland Parliament.

**181. Which parliament can make laws for Australia's territories and can even override (cancel) a territory law?**
- A. The Australian (Commonwealth) Parliament ✓
- B. The New South Wales Parliament
- C. The British Parliament
- D. No parliament can do this
- *Explanation:* Under the Constitution, the Commonwealth Parliament has full power to make laws for the territories.

**182. How did the ACT and the Northern Territory get self-government?**
- A. They declared it themselves in 1901
- B. The Commonwealth Parliament passed laws giving it to them ✓
- C. The King granted it in 1953
- D. A referendum of all Australians
- *Explanation:* Self-government was granted by Commonwealth laws — the Northern Territory in 1978 and the ACT in 1988.

**183. The King's representative in the Northern Territory has which title?**
- A. The Governor
- B. The Premier
- C. The Administrator ✓
- D. The Lord Mayor
- *Explanation:* The Administrator represents the King in the Northern Territory, performing a role like a state Governor.

**184. State elections are run by which bodies?**
- A. The Australian Electoral Commission
- B. Local councils
- C. The state police
- D. Each state's own electoral commission ✓
- *Explanation:* Each state and territory has its own electoral commission to run its elections. The AEC runs only federal elections.

**185. Laws about land use and town planning (where buildings and farms can go) are mainly made by which level of government?**
- A. State governments ✓
- B. The Australian Government
- C. The United Nations
- D. Local courts
- *Explanation:* Land and planning are state responsibilities. Councils then apply state planning laws to local decisions.

**186. Most national parks and nature reserves in Australia are managed by whom?**
- A. The Australian Army
- B. State and territory governments ✓
- C. Private companies only
- D. Local councils only
- *Explanation:* States and territories manage most national parks. A few parks, such as Kakadu, are managed by the Australian Government with their Traditional Owners.

**187. Ambulance services in Australia are run by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government
- B. Local councils
- C. State and territory governments ✓
- D. The Red Cross alone
- *Explanation:* Ambulance services are state and territory responsibilities, like hospitals and police.

**188. Trains, buses, trams and ferries in Australia's cities are the responsibility of which level of government?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The Australian Government
- C. Private charities
- D. State governments ✓
- *Explanation:* Public transport is run or organised by state governments in each capital city.

**189. Driver licences and the registration of cars are handled by which level of government?**
- A. State and territory governments ✓
- B. The Australian Government
- C. Local councils
- D. The Australian Federal Police
- *Explanation:* You get your driver licence and register your car with your state or territory government.

**190. 'Stamp duty', a tax paid when you buy a house, is collected by which level of government?**
- A. The local council
- B. The state government ✓
- C. The Australian Government
- D. The Reserve Bank
- *Explanation:* Stamp duty on property is a state tax. The Australian Government collects taxes such as income tax and the GST.

**191. Public housing (government-owned homes rented to people in need) is mainly provided by which level of government?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The Australian Government
- C. State and territory governments ✓
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* State and territory governments provide most public and community housing.

**192. Fire brigades and the State Emergency Service (SES) are the responsibility of which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government
- B. Local councils
- C. The Australian Defence Force
- D. State and territory governments ✓
- *Explanation:* Fire, rescue and emergency services are run by the states and territories, often with many volunteers.

**193. TAFE colleges, which teach trades and technical skills, are run by which level of government?**
- A. State and territory governments ✓
- B. The Australian Government
- C. Local councils
- D. Private schools only
- *Explanation:* TAFE (technical and further education) is provided by state and territory governments.

**194. Under whose laws must children in Australia attend school?**
- A. A law of the King
- B. State and territory laws ✓
- C. Council by-laws
- D. There is no such law
- *Explanation:* Education is a state and territory responsibility, and their laws require children to attend school.

**195. In most states, the upper house of the state parliament has which name?**
- A. The Senate
- B. The House of Representatives
- C. The Legislative Council ✓
- D. The Executive Council
- *Explanation:* Most states have a Legislative Council (upper house) and a Legislative Assembly (lower house). Queensland has no upper house.

**196. Tasmania's lower house of parliament has a different name from most other states. It is called what?**
- A. The Senate
- B. The Legislative Council
- C. The People's Assembly
- D. The House of Assembly ✓
- *Explanation:* Tasmania and South Australia call their lower house the House of Assembly. The other states use 'Legislative Assembly'.

**197. Just like the Commonwealth, what does each Australian state have of its own?**
- A. Its own flag and coat of arms (badges) ✓
- B. Its own currency
- C. Its own national anthem
- D. Its own citizenship
- *Explanation:* Each state has its own flag, coat of arms, and floral, bird and animal emblems.

**198. Where does a state parliament meet?**
- A. In Canberra
- B. In the Parliament House of the state capital city ✓
- C. In a different town each year
- D. In the local council chambers
- *Explanation:* Each state parliament sits in Parliament House in its own capital — for example, Spring Street in Melbourne and Macquarie Street in Sydney.

**199. Local councils in Australia exist because of laws made by whom?**
- A. The Australian Constitution directly
- B. The King
- C. State parliaments ✓
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* Local government is created under state law. It is the only level of government not set up by the Australian Constitution.

**200. Prisons in Australia are run by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Federal Police
- B. Local councils
- C. The Australian Government's army
- D. State and territory governments (corrective services) ✓
- *Explanation:* Prisons and the supervision of offenders are run by state and territory corrective services.

**201. What are council 'rates'?**
- A. Charges paid by local property owners that help pay for local services ✓
- B. The price of a library card
- C. A federal income tax
- D. Fees paid by tourists
- *Explanation:* Rates are a property-based charge. Together with fees and grants, they pay for local roads, waste, parks and libraries.

**202. Who chooses the councillors of a local council?**
- A. The state Premier
- B. The people who live in the council area, at council elections ✓
- C. The mayor alone
- D. The Australian Government
- *Explanation:* Councillors are elected by local residents, usually for terms of about four years.

**203. If a person wants to build a house or a large building in a local area, whose approval do they usually need first?**
- A. The Prime Minister
- B. The state Governor
- C. The local council ✓
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* Most building and development applications are assessed by the local council under state planning laws.

**204. Registering pet dogs and cats is a job of which level of government?**
- A. State parliaments
- B. The Australian Government
- C. The Australian Federal Police
- D. Local councils ✓
- *Explanation:* Councils keep pet registration records and run animal control services in their area.

**205. Local streets, footpaths and local bridges are maintained by whom?**
- A. Local councils ✓
- B. The Australian Government
- C. State railway departments
- D. The Australian Defence Force
- *Explanation:* Councils look after the local road network. State governments look after main roads and highways.

**206. Community halls, local sports grounds and recreation centres in a town are usually provided by whom?**
- A. The Australian Government
- B. The local council ✓
- C. The state Governor
- D. The federal Parliament
- *Explanation:* Councils provide and maintain community facilities such as halls, ovals, pools and leisure centres.

**207. Is local government mentioned (recognised) in the Australian Constitution?**
- A. Yes, it has been there since 1901
- B. Yes, it was added in 1967
- C. No — referendums in 1974 and 1988 to recognise it did not succeed ✓
- D. Only in Queensland
- *Explanation:* Local government is not in the Constitution. Voters rejected proposals to recognise it at referendums in 1974 and 1988.

**208. Council areas in Australia can have names such as a city, a town, a municipality or what?**
- A. A state
- B. A territory
- C. A province
- D. A shire ✓
- *Explanation:* Local government areas are called cities, towns, municipalities, shires or councils, depending on the state.

**209. How is a mayor chosen in Australia?**
- A. In many areas voters elect the mayor directly; in others the councillors choose one of themselves ✓
- B. Always by the state Premier
- C. Always by the King
- D. Always by the Australian Government
- *Explanation:* Both methods are used in Australia. Some mayors are elected by the people; others are chosen by their fellow councillors.

**210. Local stormwater drains — the drains that carry rainwater away from streets — are the responsibility of whom?**
- A. The Australian Navy
- B. Local councils ✓
- C. The Bureau of Meteorology
- D. State hospitals
- *Explanation:* Councils build and maintain local drainage and stormwater systems.

**211. Who checks that local cafes and food shops follow hygiene (food safety) rules?**
- A. The Australian Taxation Office
- B. The federal Parliament
- C. Local council officers, under state laws ✓
- D. The police in every case
- *Explanation:* Council environmental health officers inspect food businesses and enforce food safety standards.

**212. Who can change the boundaries of a local council area or merge two councils into one?**
- A. The council itself, whenever it likes
- B. The Australian Government
- C. The local newspaper
- D. The state government ✓
- *Explanation:* Because councils are created by state law, state governments decide council boundaries and amalgamations.

**213. Council meetings, where decisions about local plans and budgets are made, are usually what?**
- A. Open to the public, so residents can attend and listen ✓
- B. Secret, with no records kept
- C. Held in Canberra
- D. Run by state ministers
- *Explanation:* Council meetings are generally open to the public, and their decisions and minutes are published.

**214. What is a council's 'planning' role?**
- A. Planning the federal budget
- B. Deciding how land in the area can be used — for example where houses, shops and factories can be built ✓
- C. Planning military operations
- D. Planning school holidays
- *Explanation:* Through planning schemes, councils control land use and development in their local area.

**215. Street lights and street name signs in local streets are looked after by whom?**
- A. The Australian Government
- B. State police
- C. Local councils ✓
- D. The Post Office
- *Explanation:* Councils provide local street lighting and signage, along with footpaths and local roads.

**216. If you park your car illegally in a local street, who usually enforces the parking rules?**
- A. The Australian Federal Police
- B. The High Court
- C. The Governor-General
- D. The local council's officers (rangers) ✓
- *Explanation:* Council rangers and parking officers enforce local parking rules and can issue fines.

**217. If you have a problem with a local matter, such as a dangerous tree or a noisy venue, who is a good first person to contact?**
- A. One of your local councillors or the council office ✓
- B. The Prime Minister
- C. The Chief Justice
- D. The King
- *Explanation:* Local councillors represent their community. Contacting the council is the first step for local issues.

**218. In some rural council areas, the elected head of the council is not called a mayor. What other title is used?**
- A. Premier
- B. Shire President ✓
- C. Governor
- D. Senator
- *Explanation:* The head of a shire council is often called the Shire President instead of Mayor.

**219. As well as rates, where does a local council get some of its money?**
- A. By printing its own money
- B. From the King
- C. Grants from the state and Australian governments, for example for roads ✓
- D. From selling passports
- *Explanation:* Councils receive state and federal grants, including road funding, as well as rates, fees and charges.

**220. Local government is often described in which way?**
- A. As the most powerful level
- B. As part of the court system
- C. As a branch of the army
- D. As the level of government closest to the people ✓
- *Explanation:* Local government deals with the everyday services in people's own neighbourhoods, so it is called the level closest to the people.

**221. Council 'rangers' are officers who do what?**
- A. Enforce local rules, such as animal control and parking ✓
- B. Guide tourists in national parks only
- C. Fight fires in the bush
- D. Guard Parliament House
- *Explanation:* Rangers enforce council by-laws — for example about stray animals, parking, litter and noise.

**222. If a local council fails badly, what can the state government do?**
- A. Nothing at all
- B. Step in — it can investigate the council and even dismiss it and appoint an administrator ✓
- C. Ask the King to close it
- D. Send the matter to the United Nations
- *Explanation:* State governments supervise councils and can dismiss a failing council and appoint an administrator until new elections.

**223. In most of Australia, councillors are elected for terms of about how long?**
- A. One year
- B. Ten years
- C. About four years ✓
- D. For life
- *Explanation:* Council terms are about four years in most states and territories.

**224. Which of these is NOT a job of a local council?**
- A. Collecting household rubbish
- B. Running local libraries
- C. Looking after local parks
- D. Issuing Australian passports ✓
- *Explanation:* Passports are issued by the Australian Government. Rubbish, libraries and parks are classic council services.

**225. Many local councils support community life by doing what?**
- A. Organising or supporting local festivals, markets and community events ✓
- B. Running the national railways
- C. Setting interest rates
- D. Judging court cases
- *Explanation:* Councils support festivals, markets, Australia Day events and other activities that bring the local community together.

**226. Which list shows Australian courts in the correct order, from the lowest to the highest?**
- A. High Court, Supreme Court, District Court, Magistrates Court
- B. Magistrates Court, then District or County Court, then Supreme Court, then the High Court ✓
- C. Magistrates Court, High Court, Supreme Court, District Court
- D. Supreme Court, Magistrates Court, High Court, District Court
- *Explanation:* Cases usually start in a lower court and can be appealed upwards. The High Court of Australia is the final court.

**227. The most serious criminal cases in a state, such as murder, are tried in which court?**
- A. The Magistrates Court
- B. A council tribunal
- C. The Supreme Court of the state ✓
- D. The Federal Court in every case
- *Explanation:* State Supreme Courts are the highest courts in each state and hear the most serious crimes and large civil claims.

**228. What kinds of cases does the Federal Court of Australia hear?**
- A. Only parking fines
- B. Only state criminal cases
- C. Disputes between neighbours
- D. Cases that arise under federal (Australian Government) laws ✓
- *Explanation:* The Federal Court hears cases about federal laws — for example trade practices, bankruptcy, industrial and migration matters.

**229. A person charged with a serious crime usually appears first in which court?**
- A. A magistrates (local) court ✓
- B. The High Court
- C. The Senate
- D. A state parliament
- *Explanation:* Almost all criminal cases begin in a magistrates court, which decides whether serious charges go to a higher court for trial.

**230. Less serious (minor) offences are usually decided by whom, without a jury?**
- A. A jury of twelve
- B. A magistrate ✓
- C. The Chief Justice
- D. The police commissioner
- *Explanation:* Magistrates hear minor offences and disputes quickly, deciding the case themselves without a jury.

**231. In a criminal trial, the prosecution must prove the accused person's guilt to what standard?**
- A. Only a little bit
- B. On the balance of probabilities
- C. Beyond reasonable doubt ✓
- D. It does not need proof
- *Explanation:* Guilt in a criminal case must be proved beyond reasonable doubt — a much higher standard than in civil cases.

**232. In a civil case (a private dispute), the court decides on what standard of proof?**
- A. Beyond reasonable doubt
- B. The judge's first impression
- C. A vote by the public
- D. The balance of probabilities — which side is more likely to be right ✓
- *Explanation:* Civil cases are decided on the balance of probabilities: the court decides which version of the facts is more probable.

**233. What is a 'civil' case?**
- A. A dispute between people or organisations, such as a contract or compensation claim, rather than a criminal charge ✓
- B. A case heard only by soldiers
- C. A case about voting
- D. A case in the Senate
- *Explanation:* Civil cases are disputes between private parties — for example over contracts, property or compensation — not crimes against the community.

**234. In serious criminal cases, who presents the case against the accused person in court?**
- A. The victim's family
- B. An independent public prosecutor (the Director of Public Prosecutions) ✓
- C. The arresting police officer acting as judge
- D. A member of Parliament
