- *Explanation:* Serious charges are prosecuted by the Director of Public Prosecutions (DPP), an independent office acting for the whole community.

**235. If a person believes a court made a wrong decision in their case, what can they usually do?**
- A. Ignore the decision completely
- B. Ask the Prime Minister to change it
- C. Appeal to a higher court ✓
- D. Demand a referendum
- *Explanation:* Court decisions can be appealed to a higher court, which checks whether the law was applied correctly.

**236. From which court is there no further appeal in Australia?**
- A. A Magistrates Court
- B. A state Supreme Court
- C. The Federal Court
- D. The High Court of Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* The High Court is Australia's final court of appeal. Its decisions bind all other Australian courts.

**237. What does a coroner do?**
- A. Investigates deaths that are sudden, violent or unexplained ✓
- B. Performs marriages
- C. Counts votes
- D. Commands a police station
- *Explanation:* Coroners are judicial officers who investigate certain deaths, and sometimes fires, to find out what happened.

**238. Divorce and most family law matters are dealt with under the laws of which level of government?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The Australian (federal) Government ✓
- C. State governments only
- D. The United Nations
- *Explanation:* Marriage and divorce are Commonwealth powers in the Constitution, so family law is mainly federal law.

**239. Small disputes, such as a bond dispute between a tenant and a landlord, are often handled by what?**
- A. The High Court
- B. A royal commission
- C. A state tribunal, which is quicker and cheaper than a court ✓
- D. The Australian Senate
- *Explanation:* State tribunals handle many everyday disputes — tenancy, consumer and small claims — with simpler procedures than courts.

**240. Laws built up over time from judges' decisions in cases are known as what?**
- A. By-laws
- B. Statute law
- C. Martial law
- D. The common law ✓
- *Explanation:* Judge-made law is called common law. Laws made by parliaments are called statutes or Acts.

**241. What is the difference between the job of the police and the job of the courts?**
- A. Police investigate crime and charge people; courts decide whether a person is guilty ✓
- B. Police decide guilt; courts arrest people
- C. They do exactly the same job
- D. Courts patrol the streets
- *Explanation:* Police enforce the law and bring charges. Independent courts then hear the evidence and decide the case.

**242. Who sets the maximum penalty for a criminal offence?**
- A. The police
- B. The parliament that made the law — the court then chooses a sentence up to that maximum ✓
- C. The victim
- D. The jury in each case
- *Explanation:* Parliaments set penalties in the law. Judges and magistrates choose a fair sentence within those limits.

**243. The High Court of Australia has its own building in which city?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Melbourne
- C. Canberra ✓
- D. Perth
- *Explanation:* The High Court building stands on the shore of Lake Burley Griffin in Canberra.

**244. The High Court can settle disputes between which parties?**
- A. Between neighbours only
- B. Between football clubs
- C. Between Australia and other countries in war
- D. Between the Commonwealth and the states, and between states ✓
- *Explanation:* Settling disputes between governments is one of the High Court's original jobs under the Constitution.

**245. How do people become judges in Australia?**
- A. They are appointed by the government; judges are not elected ✓
- B. They are elected every four years
- C. They inherit the job
- D. They are chosen by the police
- *Explanation:* Judges and magistrates are appointed, usually from experienced lawyers, and are independent once appointed.

**246. Why are most court hearings in Australia open to the public?**
- A. So television can film everything
- B. So that justice is seen to be done fairly and openly ✓
- C. Because courts have nothing else to do
- D. Only lawyers may attend
- *Explanation:* Open courts are an important safeguard: the public and the media can watch that trials are fair.

**247. Before giving evidence in court, a witness must usually do what?**
- A. Pay a fee
- B. Prove they were born in Australia
- C. Promise to tell the truth, by taking an oath or an affirmation ✓
- D. Pass a written exam
- *Explanation:* Witnesses swear an oath (on a holy book) or make an affirmation to tell the truth. Lying in court is the crime of perjury.

**248. What can happen if a person deliberately disobeys an order of a court?**
- A. Nothing — court orders are optional
- B. The court apologises
- C. Only the King can act
- D. They can be punished for contempt of court ✓
- *Explanation:* Disobeying or disrupting a court is contempt of court, which can be punished with a fine or prison.

**249. In a civil case, what might the court order the losing side to pay to the winning side?**
- A. Compensation, called 'damages' ✓
- B. A fine to the police
- C. Council rates
- D. A tax refund
- *Explanation:* In civil cases the usual remedy is damages — money paid to compensate the person who was wronged.

**250. In a serious criminal trial, a jury usually has how many members?**
- A. 3
- B. 12 ✓
- C. 25
- D. 100
- *Explanation:* A criminal jury usually has 12 members, chosen at random from the community.

**251. The Australian Defence Force is made up of which three services?**
- A. The Army, the Police and the SES
- B. The Navy, the Marines and the Air Force
- C. The Australian Army, the Royal Australian Navy and the Royal Australian Air Force ✓
- D. The Army, the Border Force and the Navy
- *Explanation:* The three services are the Army, the Royal Australian Navy (RAN) and the Royal Australian Air Force (RAAF).

**252. Which statement about serving in the Australian Defence Force is correct?**
- A. All men must serve at 18
- B. Only citizens born in Australia may serve
- C. Members are chosen by lot
- D. Members are volunteers — Australia does not have compulsory military service ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia's defence force is a volunteer force. Compulsory service (conscription) has not applied since the 1970s.

**253. As well as defending Australia, what does the Defence Force often do inside Australia?**
- A. Help communities during bushfires, floods and other natural disasters ✓
- B. Run local councils
- C. Count votes at elections
- D. Collect taxes
- *Explanation:* When states ask for help in disasters, the ADF assists with rescues, transport, supplies and clean-up work.

**254. Which of these crimes is mainly investigated by the Australian Federal Police?**
- A. A noisy party in a local street
- B. Importing illegal drugs into Australia ✓
- C. An unpaid parking fine
- D. A dispute about a fence
- *Explanation:* The AFP investigates crimes against the Commonwealth, such as drug importation, people smuggling and terrorism.

**255. As well as federal crimes, the Australian Federal Police provides everyday community policing for which place?**
- A. Sydney
- B. Tasmania
- C. The Australian Capital Territory ✓
- D. Every capital city
- *Explanation:* The AFP's community policing arm, ACT Policing, is the everyday police force of the Australian Capital Territory.

**256. Who enforces road rules, such as speed limits and drink-driving laws?**
- A. The Australian Taxation Office
- B. Local council mayors
- C. The Australian Army
- D. State and territory police ✓
- *Explanation:* Road rules and traffic enforcement are state and territory matters, handled by their police forces.

**257. The Australian Border Force is responsible for what?**
- A. Protecting Australia's borders and checking people and goods at airports and seaports ✓
- B. Building roads near the border
- C. Guarding state parliaments
- D. Flying the Air Force's planes
- *Explanation:* The Australian Border Force is the Commonwealth's frontline border agency, working at ports, airports and at sea.

**258. Income tax paid by workers and businesses is collected by which agency?**
- A. Each local council
- B. The Australian Taxation Office (ATO) ✓
- C. The Reserve Bank
- D. State police
- *Explanation:* Parliament makes the tax laws, and the ATO administers and collects the taxes for the Australian Government.

**259. What must most people who earn an income in Australia do every year?**
- A. Vote in a tax referendum
- B. Visit the Tax Office in Canberra
- C. Lodge a tax return with the ATO ✓
- D. Pay tax directly to their local council
- *Explanation:* Each year workers lodge a tax return so the ATO can check the correct amount of tax was paid.

**260. Australia's banknotes are issued, and the official interest rate is guided, by which independent body?**
- A. The Australian Senate
- B. Local councils
- C. The High Court
- D. The Reserve Bank of Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* The Reserve Bank of Australia is the central bank. It issues banknotes and sets the official cash rate.

**261. Australia's coins are made by which organisation, located in Canberra?**
- A. The Royal Australian Mint ✓
- B. The Reserve Bank
- C. Australia Post
- D. The Australian Federal Police
- *Explanation:* All Australian coins are produced by the Royal Australian Mint in Canberra.

**262. Australia Post, which delivers mail around the country, is owned by whom?**
- A. A foreign company
- B. The Australian Government ✓
- C. The states jointly
- D. Local councils
- *Explanation:* Australia Post is a government-owned corporation — it belongs to the Australian Government.

**263. The ABC (Australian Broadcasting Corporation) is best described as what?**
- A. A private television company
- B. A state police radio station
- C. Australia's national public broadcaster, funded by the Australian Government ✓
- D. A council newspaper
- *Explanation:* The ABC provides national television, radio and online services, funded by the government but editorially independent.

**264. Centrelink payments and Medicare services are delivered to the public by which Australian Government agency?**
- A. The Australian Electoral Commission
- B. The High Court
- C. State hospitals
- D. Services Australia ✓
- *Explanation:* Services Australia is the agency that delivers social security payments (Centrelink) and Medicare services.

**265. Universities in Australia receive most of their public funding from which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government ✓
- B. Local councils
- C. State governments only
- D. The United Nations
- *Explanation:* Higher education is mainly a Commonwealth responsibility, while states run schools and TAFE.

**266. What does Medicare do for Australians in everyday life?**
- A. It pays people's rent
- B. It helps pay for visits to the doctor and treatment in public hospitals ✓
- C. It provides free cars
- D. It replaces private health insurance by law
- *Explanation:* Medicare gives Australians access to free treatment in public hospitals and subsidised visits to doctors.

**267. The school curriculum — what is taught in schools — is mainly decided by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government alone
- B. Local councils
- C. State and territory governments ✓
- D. The High Court
- *Explanation:* States and territories run education systems and set the curriculum, within a national framework agreed with the Commonwealth.

**268. Managing Australia's money — the dollar — is the responsibility of which level of government?**
- A. State governments
- B. Local councils
- C. Private banks alone
- D. The Australian (Commonwealth) Government ✓
- *Explanation:* Currency is an exclusive Commonwealth responsibility under the Constitution.

**269. Australian embassies in other countries, which can help Australians travelling overseas, are run by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government ✓
- B. State governments
- C. Local councils
- D. The United Nations
- *Explanation:* Foreign affairs is a Commonwealth responsibility. Australian embassies and consulates help citizens overseas.

**270. Decisions about visas for people who want to come to Australia are made by which level of government?**
- A. Local councils
- B. The Australian Government ✓
- C. State governments
- D. The courts in each case
- *Explanation:* Immigration and visas are decided by the Australian Government through the Department of Home Affairs.

**271. Which statement about Australia's major highways is correct?**
- A. They are built by local councils alone
- B. They are owned by the Army
- C. They are built and maintained mainly by state governments, with funding help from the Australian Government ✓
- D. They are run by the United Nations
- *Explanation:* States build and maintain highways, and the Commonwealth helps pay for roads of national importance.

**272. In most Australian cities, tap water supply and sewerage are provided by which level of government?**
- A. Local councils in every case
- B. The Australian Government directly
- C. Private households themselves
- D. State or territory governments, through their water utilities ✓
- *Explanation:* Large city water and sewerage services are provided by state or territory government utilities.

**273. The SES (State Emergency Service), whose volunteers help in storms and floods, is organised by which level of government?**
- A. State and territory governments ✓
- B. The Australian Government
- C. Local councils alone
- D. The Australian Red Cross alone
- *Explanation:* Each state and territory runs its own SES, staffed mostly by trained volunteers.

**274. Each state and territory police force is headed by a senior officer with which title?**
- A. Chief Justice
- B. Commissioner of Police ✓
- C. Governor
- D. Premier
- *Explanation:* A Commissioner of Police leads each state and territory force. The AFP is led by its own Commissioner too.

**275. When you dial 000 in an emergency, you can reach the police, fire and ambulance services. These services are organised mainly by which level of government?**
- A. The Australian Government
- B. Local councils
- C. State and territory governments ✓
- D. The United Nations
- *Explanation:* 000 connects callers to state and territory emergency services — police, fire and ambulance.

**276. If you want to become an Australian citizen by conferral (the usual path for migrants), to whom do you apply?**
- A. Your local council
- B. Your state government
- C. The High Court
- D. The Australian Government's Department of Home Affairs ✓
- *Explanation:* Citizenship applications are made to the Department of Home Affairs, the Australian Government department for immigration and citizenship.

**277. Most adults who apply for citizenship must sit the citizenship test. This applies to applicants in which age group?**
- A. Aged 18 to 59 ✓
- B. Only people under 25
- C. Only people over 60
- D. Children under 16
- *Explanation:* Most applicants aged 18 to 59 sit the test. Different arrangements apply for children and older applicants.

**278. How is the Australian citizenship test taken?**
- A. As an oral exam in any language
- B. On a computer, answering 20 multiple-choice questions in English ✓
- C. As a written essay
- D. At a citizenship ceremony
- *Explanation:* The test is made up of 20 multiple-choice questions, answered on a computer, in English.

**279. To pass the citizenship test, an applicant must score at least 75 per cent overall, and also do what?**
- A. Answer every question correctly
- B. Finish in 10 minutes
- C. Answer all five Australian values questions correctly ✓
- D. Pass a medical test
- *Explanation:* The pass mark is 75 per cent overall, and all five questions about Australian values must be answered correctly.

**280. What happens if an applicant does not pass the citizenship test?**
- A. They can never apply again
- B. They must leave Australia at once
- C. They must wait 10 years
- D. They can book and sit the test again ✓
- *Explanation:* Failing the test does not end an application. Applicants can sit the test again, with help to study the book if needed.

**281. All the questions in the citizenship test come from which part of the official book 'Our Common Bond'?**
- A. The testable section of the book ✓
- B. Any book about Australia
- C. Newspapers
- D. The Australian Constitution itself
- *Explanation:* Test questions are based only on the testable part of 'Australian Citizenship: Our Common Bond'.

**282. To be eligible for citizenship by conferral, an applicant must meet a residence requirement. In general, they must have done what?**
- A. Lived in Australia for one month
- B. Lived in Australia for four years, including at least one year as a permanent resident ✓
- C. Owned a house in Australia
- D. Worked for the government
- *Explanation:* The general residence requirement is four years living in Australia, including the last 12 months as a permanent resident.

**283. Children can be included in a parent's citizenship application if they are what?**
- A. Over 18
- B. Born in Australia only
- C. Under 16 years of age ✓
- D. Married
- *Explanation:* Children under 16 can usually be included on a parent's application at no extra cost.

**284. There are two versions of the Australian citizenship pledge. What is the difference between them?**
- A. One is for men and one for women
- B. One is in French
- C. There is no difference
- D. One version includes the words 'under God' and the other does not — new citizens choose ✓
- *Explanation:* New citizens may choose the pledge with the words 'under God', or the version without them. Both have the same legal meaning.

**285. At a citizenship ceremony, a person becomes an Australian citizen at the moment they do what?**
- A. Make the citizenship pledge ✓
- B. Arrive at the venue
- C. Sing the national anthem
- D. Receive a passport
- *Explanation:* Making the pledge of commitment is the moment a person becomes an Australian citizen.

**286. What document do new citizens receive at their citizenship ceremony?**
- A. An Australian passport
- B. An Australian citizenship certificate ✓
- C. A driver's licence
- D. A Medicare card
- *Explanation:* New citizens are presented with their citizenship certificate at the ceremony. A passport can be applied for afterwards.

**287. Many citizenship ceremonies around Australia are held on which special day?**
- A. Anzac Day, 25 April
- B. New Year's Day
- C. Australia Day, 26 January ✓
- D. The King's birthday holiday
- *Explanation:* Australia Day, 26 January, is the most popular day for citizenship ceremonies, when thousands of people become citizens.

**288. A child born overseas to an Australian citizen parent can become an Australian citizen how?**
- A. Automatically, with no application
- B. Only by sitting the citizenship test at age 5
- C. Only if the family moves to Canberra
- D. By applying for citizenship by descent ✓
- *Explanation:* People born overseas to an Australian citizen parent may register for citizenship by descent.

**289. What is Australia's position on dual citizenship?**
- A. Australia allows its citizens to also hold the citizenship of another country ✓
- B. It is a crime to hold two citizenships
- C. Only people born overseas may hold two
- D. It was banned in 1901
- *Explanation:* Australia allows dual citizenship. Whether the other country allows it depends on that country's laws.

**290. Australian citizenship as a separate legal status began in which year?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1949 ✓
- C. 1788
- D. 2001
- *Explanation:* Before 1949 Australians were British subjects. A separate Australian citizenship was created by law in 1949.

**291. Who may nominate to stand for election to the Australian Parliament?**
- A. Any permanent resident
- B. Any visitor
- C. Only Australian citizens aged 18 or over ✓
- D. Only people born in Australia
- *Explanation:* Only citizens aged 18 or over can stand for the federal Parliament — one reason citizenship matters.

**292. If an Australian citizen gets into serious trouble overseas, who can they ask for consular help?**
- A. Their local council
- B. A state premier
- C. The High Court
- D. An Australian embassy or consulate ✓
- *Explanation:* Australian embassies and consulates can help citizens in trouble overseas, for example after losing a passport.

**293. The Aboriginal Flag and the Torres Strait Islander Flag have what official status in Australia?**
- A. They are official flags of Australia, recognised under the Flags Act ✓
- B. They are unofficial decorations
- C. They may only be used in Queensland
- D. They are flags of political parties
- *Explanation:* Both flags were officially proclaimed as flags of Australia in 1995 and are flown with the national flag at official places.

**294. The Australian Red Ensign (a red version of the national flag) is flown by whom?**
- A. The Australian Army
- B. Australian merchant and civil ships ✓
- C. State parliaments
- D. Schools
- *Explanation:* The Red Ensign is the flag of Australia's merchant navy and is flown by Australian-registered civil ships.

**295. Where the Governor-General is staying or present, a special flag is flown. Whose flag is it?**
- A. The flag of the Prime Minister
- B. The flag of the Senate
- C. The flag of the Governor-General of Australia ✓
- D. The flag of the ACT
- *Explanation:* The Governor-General has a personal flag, flown at Government House and wherever the Governor-General is present.

**296. As a mark of respect and mourning, for example after the death of an important person, the Australian National Flag may be flown how?**
- A. Upside down
- B. Only at night
- C. Not at all for a year
- D. At half-mast ✓
- *Explanation:* Flying a flag at half-mast is the traditional sign of mourning and respect.

**297. At official events in Australia, three flags are commonly flown together. Which three?**
- A. The Australian National Flag, the Aboriginal Flag and the Torres Strait Islander Flag ✓
- B. The flags of three states
- C. The Australian, British and American flags
- D. The flags of the Army, Navy and Air Force
- *Explanation:* The national flag is flown together with the Aboriginal Flag and the Torres Strait Islander Flag at many official buildings.

**298. Parliament House in Canberra was specially designed so that members of the public can do what?**
- A. Swim in a pool on the roof
- B. Walk on the grassed roof above the building, symbolising that the people are above the Parliament ✓
- C. Park their cars inside the chambers
- D. Vote from the galleries
- *Explanation:* The building is covered by lawns that people can walk on — a symbol that Parliament belongs to the people.

**299. The federal Parliament first met in Melbourne. In which year did it move to Canberra?**
- A. 1901
- B. 1915
- C. 1927 ✓
- D. 1988
- *Explanation:* Parliament moved to Canberra in 1927, sitting in the provisional Parliament House (now Old Parliament House) until 1988.

**300. Which statement best sums up how Australia is governed?**
- A. The Prime Minister has all the power
- B. The states rule the Commonwealth
- C. The Governor-General makes all decisions
- D. Three levels of elected government share power under the Constitution, and independent courts interpret the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia is a federation and a parliamentary democracy: three levels of government share power, and the courts are independent.

## Topic 4: Australian values

**1. Which set of values is at the heart of Australian society?**
- A. Freedom, respect, fairness and equality of opportunity ✓
- B. Wealth, fame and power
- C. Strict obedience to tradition
- D. Keeping to your own group only
- *Explanation:* Australian society is built on freedom, respect, fairness (a fair go) and equality of opportunity for everyone.

**2. What does the Australian value of 'a fair go' mean?**
- A. The strongest person should always win
- B. Everyone should have an equal opportunity to succeed in life ✓
- C. No one should ever have to work
- D. Only sport needs to be fair
- *Explanation:* A fair go means everyone gets an equal chance to succeed, whatever their background, wealth or connections.

**3. Australians believe that what a person achieves in life should mainly depend on what?**
- A. Their family's connections
- B. Their religion
- C. Their talents, hard work and effort ✓
- D. The suburb they were born in
- *Explanation:* In Australia, success should come from your talents and effort — not from your wealth, class or who your family is.

**4. Which of these best expresses the value of mutual respect?**
- A. Respect only people older than you
- B. Respect only people who are like you
- C. Other people must earn your respect first
- D. Treat other people the way you would like to be treated ✓
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect means treating others with the same courtesy and dignity you expect for yourself.

**5. When Australians disagree with each other, what are they expected to do?**
- A. Discuss the problem respectfully and peacefully ✓
- B. Shout until the other person gives in
- C. Never speak to each other again
- D. Ask the police to punish the other person's opinion
- *Explanation:* Disagreement is normal in a democracy. Australians are expected to discuss differences respectfully and without violence.

**6. Equality of opportunity in Australia means what?**
- A. Everyone must finish with the same income
- B. Everyone should have a fair chance in life, whatever their background ✓
- C. Only citizens are allowed opportunities
- D. Competition should be banned
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity means a fair start and a fair chance for everyone — not identical results.

**7. Which of these statements about men and women is true in Australia?**
- A. Men and women have different basic legal rights
- B. Men and women must be given separate laws
- C. Men and women have equal rights and deserve equal respect ✓
- D. Men and women cannot legally work in the same jobs
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women is a core Australian value. Both have the same rights under the law.

**8. Can a woman in Australia choose her own education, job and whom she marries?**
- A. Only with her father's permission
- B. Only if she was born in Australia
- C. No
- D. Yes — women make their own choices, equal to men ✓
- *Explanation:* Women in Australia are free to study, work and marry by their own choice, with the same legal rights as men.

**9. Australian values say that violence against another person is what?**
- A. Never acceptable — it is against the law ✓
- B. Acceptable inside the family
- C. Acceptable if you are very angry
- D. Always a private matter
- *Explanation:* Violence against any person is against Australian values and against the law — including inside families.

**10. If you see someone being seriously hurt or threatened, what can you do?**
- A. Ignore it and walk away
- B. Call the police on 000 in an emergency to get help ✓
- C. Film it for entertainment
- D. Join in on the stronger side
- *Explanation:* In an emergency, call 000 for police, fire or ambulance. Australians help people in danger where they safely can.

**11. The Australian value of compassion means what?**
- A. Feeling sorry for yourself
- B. Giving gifts at Christmas
- C. Caring about people in need and helping them ✓
- D. Agreeing with everyone
- *Explanation:* Compassion means noticing when others are suffering or in need, and helping them — including strangers.

**12. In Australian values, what does 'mateship' mean?**
- A. Only helping your own relatives
- B. A business partnership
- C. A type of team uniform
- D. Helping and standing by others, especially in tough times ✓
- *Explanation:* Mateship means loyalty and practical help — standing by people when times are hard, not only when things are easy.

**13. After bushfires and floods, what do Australians often do that shows their values?**
- A. Volunteer, donate and help strangers rebuild their lives ✓
- B. Wait for other countries to help
- C. Blame the people affected
- D. Help only their own street
- *Explanation:* After disasters, Australians volunteer in huge numbers and donate to help people they have never met.

**14. Which language helps all Australians communicate with each other, whatever their background?**
- A. The language of each person's ancestors
- B. English, the national language ✓
- C. Only local slang
- D. Latin
- *Explanation:* English is Australia's national language and the shared language of public life, even though many other languages are spoken.

**15. Why do Australians value learning English?**
- A. Because other languages are banned
- B. Because English is the language of the King
- C. Because it helps people take part in society, work, study and live independently ✓
- D. Because television is only in English
- *Explanation:* Speaking English helps new Australians get jobs, study, use services and take a full part in community life.

**16. What are new migrants in Australia encouraged to do about language?**
- A. Never speak their first language again
- B. Stop their children learning English
- C. Refuse all English classes
- D. Learn English, while keeping their own languages and cultures ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia supports migrants learning English, and also values the many languages and cultures migrants bring.

**17. Australian values include respect for what?**
- A. The freedom and dignity of every individual ✓
- B. Only the majority group
- C. Only citizens
- D. Only government officials
- *Explanation:* Respect for the freedom and dignity of the individual is the first of Australia's shared values.

**18. Freedom of religion, as an Australian value, means what?**
- A. Everyone must follow the same religion
- B. People are free to follow any religion, or none, within the law ✓
- C. Religion is banned in public
- D. Only one religion is protected by law
- *Explanation:* Australians are free to follow any religion or no religion. The government is separate from religion.

**19. Which Australian value means that laws are made by an elected parliament, not imposed by force?**
- A. A fair go
- B. Mateship
- C. Parliamentary democracy ✓
- D. Compassion
- *Explanation:* Parliamentary democracy means governments are elected by the people and laws are made by their representatives.

**20. Commitment to the rule of law, as an Australian value, means what?**
- A. Laws are optional for citizens
- B. Police never have to obey laws
- C. Private rules can replace the law
- D. Everyone — citizens, leaders and police — must obey the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Under the rule of law, nobody is above the law. The law applies equally to leaders, officials and citizens.

**21. Australian values say people of all backgrounds and religions should be what?**
- A. Treated equally under the law ✓
- B. Given different laws
- C. Ranked by their beliefs
- D. Kept apart from each other
- *Explanation:* Equality under the law means your background, race or religion does not change how the law treats you.

**22. Which of these is an Australian value?**
- A. Revenge for insults
- B. Peacefulness — living together without violence ✓
- C. Avoiding all strangers
- D. Settling arguments by force
- *Explanation:* Peacefulness is an Australian value: we resolve our differences through discussion and law, not violence.

**23. The value called 'pursuit of the public good' means what?**
- A. Only politicians matter to the country
- B. Making money by any means possible
- C. Contributing to the good of the whole community, beyond yourself and your family ✓
- D. Public land belongs to nobody
- *Explanation:* Pursuit of the public good means working for the benefit of the whole community, not just yourself.

**24. Volunteering — for example in surf lifesaving, junior sport or charities — is an example of which value?**
- A. Obedience
- B. Competition
- C. Privacy
- D. Helping others and contributing to the community ✓
- *Explanation:* Millions of Australians volunteer. Giving your time for others is central to Australian community life.

**25. Tolerance, as an Australian value, means what?**
- A. Accepting that people may live and believe differently from you, within the law ✓
- B. Hiding all of your own opinions
- C. Pretending to agree with everyone
- D. Avoiding your neighbours
- *Explanation:* Tolerance means living alongside people whose beliefs and customs differ from yours, respectfully and within the law.

**26. Which of these behaviours goes against Australian values?**
- A. Volunteering at a local club
- B. Refusing to serve a customer in a shop because of their race ✓
- C. Learning to speak English
- D. Helping a neighbour move house
- *Explanation:* Refusing service because of race is discrimination. It is against Australian values and against the law.

**27. If you disagree with an Australian law, what do Australian values say you should do?**
- A. Secretly break it whenever you can
- B. Threaten politicians
- C. Campaign peacefully to change it and vote for change ✓
- D. Leave Australia at once
- *Explanation:* You must obey the law even if you disagree with it — and you are free to campaign peacefully to have it changed.

**28. Which of these is part of respecting the law in Australia?**
- A. Obeying only the laws you personally like
- B. Offering money to a police officer
- C. Lying in court
- D. Paying the taxes you owe and obeying the road rules ✓
- *Explanation:* Respect for the law shows in everyday life: paying tax honestly, obeying road rules and being truthful with officials.

**29. When an Australian employer chooses someone for a job, what do our values say should matter?**
- A. The person's skills and experience — not their background, race or religion ✓
- B. Only employing local-born people
- C. The applicant's religion first
- D. Family connections above everything
- *Explanation:* Jobs should go on merit — skills and experience. Discrimination because of background is against our values and the law.

**30. Equality under the law means that if a rich person and a poor person break the same law, what happens?**
- A. Only the poor person is punished
- B. Both are treated the same way by the police and the courts ✓
- C. The rich person is praised for success
- D. Neither person is punished
- *Explanation:* The law treats everyone the same, rich or poor, powerful or unknown.

**31. Is it acceptable in Australia for a husband to control his wife's money, phone and movements against her will?**
- A. Yes, always
- B. Yes, if the whole family agrees
- C. No — women have the same rights and freedoms as men ✓
- D. Yes, but only inside the home
- *Explanation:* Controlling a partner is a form of family violence. Women in Australia have exactly the same rights and freedoms as men.

**32. What do Australian values and laws say about children?**
- A. Children may be punished in any way parents choose
- B. Children do not need an education
- C. Children's safety does not matter to the law
- D. Children must be protected from harm and have the right to be safe ✓
- *Explanation:* Children have the right to be safe and protected. Australian laws protect children from abuse and neglect.

**33. Which of these reflects Australian values about family life?**
- A. Families should care for each other, and violence in the family is against the law ✓
- B. Family problems justify violence
- C. Children have no rights in a family
- D. Nobody outside a family may ever help it
- *Explanation:* Families are places of care and support. Violence within families is a crime, and help is available.

**34. At what age can a person generally marry in Australia?**
- A. 16 years old in all cases
- B. 18 years old ✓
- C. 21 years old
- D. Any age, if friends agree
- *Explanation:* The marriage age in Australia is 18. Marriage of a person under 18 is generally not allowed.

**35. Is forced marriage legal in Australia?**
- A. Yes, it is allowed
- B. Yes, between citizens
- C. No — forcing anyone to marry is a crime ✓
- D. Yes, in some states only
- *Explanation:* Forced marriage is a criminal offence in Australia. Every person must freely consent to their marriage.

**36. An arranged marriage is lawful in Australia only when what is true?**
- A. The two families have agreed between themselves
- B. One family has paid the other family
- C. Both people are over 30 years old
- D. Both people freely choose and fully consent to the marriage ✓
- *Explanation:* Arranged marriages are fine in Australia only when both people are adults who freely and fully consent.

**37. Can a person in Australia legally have more than one spouse at the same time?**
- A. No — having more than one spouse (polygamy) is against the law ✓
- B. Yes, up to two spouses
- C. Yes, if the marriages happened overseas
- D. Yes, for citizens only
- *Explanation:* Marriage in Australia is between two people only. Having more than one spouse is against the law.

**38. If a marriage breaks down in Australia, what may either person do?**
- A. Keep the children with no say from any court
- B. Apply for a divorce through the legal system ✓
- C. Force the other person to stay married
- D. Take all the property automatically
- *Explanation:* Either person may apply for a divorce. Courts decide fairly about property and the care of children.

**39. Domestic and family violence in Australia is what?**
- A. A private matter that the law ignores
- B. An acceptable cultural practice
- C. A serious crime — help is available and victims are protected by law ✓
- D. Wrong only if the neighbours complain
- *Explanation:* Family violence is a serious crime in Australia. Police and support services help victims, whatever their background.

**40. What can a person experiencing family violence in Australia do?**
- A. Nothing — they must stay quiet
- B. Only ask their relatives for help
- C. Wait until it happens many times
- D. Get help from police and support services — help is available in many languages ✓
- *Explanation:* Help is available: call 000 in an emergency, and support services can provide interpreters and safe accommodation.

**41. Australian values about work say that everyone should have what?**
- A. Fair treatment and a safe workplace ✓
- B. Periods of working without pay
- C. Dangerous conditions they must accept silently
- D. Jobs only for their relatives
- *Explanation:* Everyone deserves fair pay, fair treatment and a workplace where their safety is protected.

**42. Bullying or harassing someone — at work, at school or online — because of their race, religion or gender is what?**
- A. Just a joke
- B. Against Australian values and possibly against the law ✓
- C. Encouraged in Australian culture
- D. The right of managers
- *Explanation:* Harassment and bullying are unacceptable. Depending on the behaviour, they can also be criminal offences.

**43. Respecting other people's privacy and property — for example, not reading their mail or taking their things — shows which value?**
- A. A fair go
- B. Ambition
- C. Mutual respect ✓
- D. Patriotism
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect includes respecting other people's privacy, property and personal space.

**44. A football club welcomes new players from every background and selects its teams only on effort and skill. This is an example of what?**
- A. Favouritism
- B. Exclusion
- C. Compulsory voting
- D. A fair go ✓
- *Explanation:* That is the fair go in action: everyone gets a chance, judged on effort and ability, not background.

**45. Which of these actions best shows Australian values in daily life?**
- A. Helping an elderly neighbour with their shopping ✓
- B. Dumping rubbish in the bush
- C. Refusing to wait your turn in a queue
- D. Cheating in an exam
- *Explanation:* Small everyday kindnesses — helping neighbours and strangers — are Australian values in practice.

**46. Respect for other people in Australia includes respecting what?**
- A. Their expensive clothes
- B. Their religious beliefs and cultural practices, even if different from yours ✓
- C. Their social media posts
- D. Their choice of car
- *Explanation:* Respect means accepting other people's beliefs and customs, even when they are different from your own.

**47. Australians value the idea that people who are able to work should do what?**
- A. Be given a job by the government automatically
- B. Stay home unless they feel like working
- C. Contribute to the community by working and supporting themselves when they can ✓
- D. Work only in a family business
- *Explanation:* Australia supports people in need, and values everyone contributing through work when they are able to.

**48. People who become Australian citizens make a pledge. In that pledge they promise to do what?**
- A. Give up all of their original culture
- B. Pay a loyalty fee each year
- C. Follow only the laws of their old country
- D. Share Australia's democratic beliefs, respect its rights and liberties, and uphold and obey its laws ✓
- *Explanation:* The citizenship pledge commits new citizens to Australia's democratic beliefs, rights and liberties, and to upholding its laws.

**49. The Australian Values Statement, signed by visa applicants, commits them to what?**
- A. Respecting Australian values and obeying Australian laws ✓
- B. Giving up their first language
- C. Joining a political party
- D. Buying property in Australia
- *Explanation:* Everyone who applies for an Australian visa agrees to respect Australian values and obey Australian laws.

**50. Which of these is NOT one of Australia's shared values?**
- A. A fair go
- B. Obedience to a single ruler ✓
- C. Mutual respect
- D. Equality of opportunity
- *Explanation:* Australians do not value obedience to one ruler. Our values are freedom, respect, fairness and equality under democratic law.

**51. Why is the Australian values part of the citizenship test treated so strictly?**
- A. Because it has the most questions in the test
- B. Because it is the easiest part
- C. Because in the real test you must answer all five values questions correctly to pass ✓
- D. Because it replaces the rest of the test
- *Explanation:* In the real test, all five Australian values questions must be answered correctly — even one wrong answer means you do not pass.

**52. Helping others 'in a spirit of mateship' includes helping whom?**
- A. Only your closest friends
- B. Only people who can repay you
- C. Only people from your own ethnic community
- D. Strangers in the wider community, not just friends ✓
- *Explanation:* Mateship extends beyond friends and family — to neighbours, workmates and strangers in need.

**53. What does the large number of volunteers in Australia show about our values?**
- A. People freely give their time for the good of the community ✓
- B. Volunteers are secretly paid
- C. Only retired people volunteer
- D. Volunteering replaces paying tax
- *Explanation:* Volunteering shows Australians putting community before self — a key Australian value.

**54. Respect for the dignity of the individual means what?**
- A. Famous people's lives matter most
- B. Every person's life and worth deserve respect ✓
- C. Only citizens have dignity
- D. Dignity depends on income
- *Explanation:* Every person has dignity and worth, regardless of wealth, status, background or beliefs.

**55. Which situation shows equality of opportunity working properly?**
- A. A school that only accepts one suburb
- B. Jobs offered only by word of mouth to friends
- C. A scholarship open to all students and awarded on merit ✓
- D. Exams with different rules for different students
- *Explanation:* Opportunities open to everyone and awarded on merit are equality of opportunity in practice.

**56. In Australia, a person's race or religion should NOT do what?**
- A. Appear on their birth certificate
- B. Feature in cultural festivals
- C. Be spoken about in public
- D. Stop them getting a fair chance in education, work and services ✓
- *Explanation:* Race and religion must never be a barrier to education, employment or government services in Australia.

**57. Which of these does Australian law forbid?**
- A. Refusing a job applicant because of their religion ✓
- B. Asking a job applicant about their skills
- C. Holding competitive entry exams
- D. Offering English classes at work
- *Explanation:* It is unlawful to refuse someone a job because of their religion, race, sex or other protected attributes.

**58. Sexual harassment in Australia is what?**
- A. Normal workplace banter
- B. Unacceptable behaviour, and against the law ✓
- C. Wrong only in large offices
- D. Best dealt with by ignoring it
- *Explanation:* Sexual harassment is against the law in Australia. Everyone has the right to be safe and respected.

**59. Respect within a relationship means what?**
- A. One partner makes all the decisions
- B. Jealousy justifies checking a partner's phone
- C. Both partners are equal and live free from fear and control ✓
- D. Arguments must be kept secret from everyone forever
- *Explanation:* Healthy relationships are equal: both partners are free, safe and able to make their own decisions.

**60. If someone in Australia is treated unfairly because of their race, what can they do?**
- A. Accept it silently, always
- B. Retaliate with violence
- C. Leave the country immediately
- D. Complain to bodies such as the Australian Human Rights Commission ✓
- *Explanation:* People who experience discrimination can complain to the Australian Human Rights Commission or state anti-discrimination bodies.

**61. Freedom of association, an Australian value and right, means what?**
- A. People can freely join groups, unions, parties and clubs ✓
- B. Groups may plan crimes in private
- C. Clubs never have to follow the law
- D. Only political parties may exist
- *Explanation:* Freedom of association means people may form and join unions, parties, clubs and community groups of their choosing.

**62. Freedom of speech, as an Australian value, means Australians can do what?**
- A. Say anything at all, including threats
- B. Discuss ideas and criticise governments peacefully ✓
- C. Publish other people's private details
- D. Shout down every speaker they dislike
- *Explanation:* Australians may debate ideas and criticise governments openly and peacefully. Threats and inciting violence are not protected.

**63. Why is tolerance especially important in Australian society?**
- A. It ends all disagreement
- B. It makes everyone think the same way
- C. It allows people of many cultures and faiths to live together peacefully ✓
- D. It replaces the need for laws
- *Explanation:* Australia is home to people from many cultures and faiths. Tolerance lets everyone live together peacefully.

**64. Which of these behaviours shows a lack of respect for Australian values?**
- A. Waiting patiently in a queue
- B. Greeting your neighbours
- C. Returning a lost wallet to its owner
- D. Spreading racist insults about a community ✓
- *Explanation:* Racism and abuse are the opposite of mutual respect, and have no place in Australian life.

**65. During an emergency such as a flood, 'looking out for others' means what?**
- A. Following safety advice and helping others where you safely can ✓
- B. Ignoring official warnings
- C. Taking emergency supplies meant for others
- D. Filming events instead of helping anyone
- *Explanation:* In emergencies, Australians follow official advice and look out for neighbours and strangers alike.

**66. Paying tax honestly is part of Australian values because what?**
- A. Tax is optional for generous people
- B. Taxes fund services that benefit the whole community ✓
- C. Only businesses benefit from taxes
- D. It is a loyalty test applied only to migrants
- *Explanation:* Honest taxes pay for hospitals, schools, roads and defence that everyone uses. Cheating tax cheats the community.

**67. Australians value honesty. When dealing with government and officials, this means what?**
- A. Telling officials whatever gets approved fastest
- B. Always keeping secrets from the government
- C. Giving true and honest information ✓
- D. Honesty matters only in court
- *Explanation:* Honesty with officials — on forms, applications and claims — is expected of everyone in Australia.

**68. If you make a mistake that harms another person, Australian values of respect suggest you should do what?**
- A. Blame someone else
- B. Hide what happened
- C. Deny everything
- D. Take responsibility and apologise ✓
- *Explanation:* Taking responsibility and apologising when you are wrong is part of treating others with respect.

**69. Caring for Australia's environment and wildlife is part of what?**
- A. Caring for the country and the community we all share ✓
- B. Only park rangers' jobs
- C. Tourist advertising
- D. Nothing of importance
- *Explanation:* Looking after the land, water and wildlife is part of contributing to the country we share.

**70. Which of these is an example of compassion for those in need?**
- A. Ignoring a person sleeping rough
- B. A community raising money for a sick child's treatment ✓
- C. Putting prices up sharply after a disaster
- D. Helping only when television cameras are present
- *Explanation:* Compassion means acting to help people who are suffering or in need, without expecting anything back.

**71. Treating police, officials and service staff with courtesy reflects which Australian value?**
- A. Fear of authority
- B. Wealth
- C. Mutual respect ✓
- D. Party loyalty
- *Explanation:* Courtesy towards everyone — including officials and service workers — is mutual respect in everyday life.

**72. Which statement about religious dress and customs in Australia is true?**
- A. Religious dress is banned in public
- B. Only Christian customs are allowed in public
- C. All customs must be kept hidden at home
- D. People may practise their religion and wear religious dress, within the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia protects freedom of religion: people may worship and wear religious dress, subject to the same laws as everyone.

**73. Australian values say adults are free to choose their own partner, which means what?**
- A. Adults choose for themselves whom to marry, and forced marriage is a crime ✓
- B. Parents always choose the partner
- C. Community leaders must approve every marriage
- D. Your choice of partner is limited by your religion
- *Explanation:* Choosing your own partner is a basic freedom in Australia. Forcing someone to marry is a criminal offence.

**74. What do Australians believe about the education of girls and boys?**
- A. Boys should be educated first
- B. Girls and boys have an equal right to education ✓
- C. Education is only for citizens
- D. Girls need less schooling than boys
- *Explanation:* Girls and boys have the same right to a full education in Australia.

**75. Which of these is an example of pursuing the public good?**
- A. Dumping waste in a river to save money
- B. A business hiding safety problems from the public
- C. Residents working together to clean up and protect a local beach ✓
- D. Blocking a public footpath for private use
- *Explanation:* Working together to protect shared places is pursuing the public good.

**76. Which of these is a shared responsibility of everyone living in Australia?**
- A. Reporting your neighbours' private beliefs
- B. Joining a political party
- C. Speaking only English at home
- D. Obeying the law and helping to keep the community safe ✓
- *Explanation:* Everyone in Australia shares responsibility for obeying the law and keeping the community safe and peaceful.

**77. If you witness a crime in Australia, what do our community values suggest you do?**
- A. Report it to the police ✓
- B. Publish the suspects' names online first
- C. Confront armed offenders yourself
- D. Ignore it completely
- *Explanation:* Witnesses help the community by reporting crime to the police, rather than taking the law into their own hands.

**78. How should a dispute between neighbours in Australia be handled?**
- A. With threats until one side gives in
- B. By peaceful discussion, and mediation services if needed ✓
- C. By damaging the other person's property
- D. By involving children in the argument
- *Explanation:* Neighbours should talk through problems calmly. Free mediation services can help when discussion is not enough.

**79. In community sport, which of these fits Australian values?**
- A. Teams limited to one ethnic group
- B. Winning justifies cheating the rules
- C. Players from every background getting a fair chance to be selected on merit ✓
- D. Selection decided by family name
- *Explanation:* Sport in Australia is open to all: selection on merit and effort gives everyone a fair go.

**80. Which of these best shows respect for the equal value of men and women?**
- A. Paying a woman less for exactly the same work
- B. A woman needing permission to study
- C. Men making all household decisions alone
- D. A workplace that promotes the best person for the job, man or woman ✓
- *Explanation:* Equal value means equal treatment: the best person gets the job, the promotion and the pay for the work.

**81. Australian values reject which of these ideas?**
- A. That one race or culture is superior to all others ✓
- B. That people can be proud of their heritage
- C. That migrants can keep their first language
- D. That communities can celebrate their festivals
- *Explanation:* Australia rejects racism. Pride in your own heritage is welcome — claiming it makes you superior to others is not.

**82. What does it mean to describe Australia as a peaceful society?**
- A. Australia has no police force
- B. Problems, protests and changes of government happen without violence ✓
- C. Australians never disagree about anything
- D. Newspapers avoid reporting problems
- *Explanation:* Peaceful means conflicts are settled by discussion, courts and elections — never by violence.

**83. Peaceful protest is protected in Australia because of which idea?**
- A. Protesters always get what they want
- B. Traffic disruption is a public good
- C. People may express their views and seek change without violence ✓
- D. Protests replace the need for elections
- *Explanation:* Peaceful protest is part of free expression in a democracy, provided it stays within the law.

**84. Which of these is the right way to express a very strong opinion in Australia?**
- A. Threatening those who disagree with you
- B. Damaging the property of your opponents
- C. Harassing the families of officials
- D. Arguing your case peacefully in public debate ✓
- *Explanation:* Strong opinions are welcome in Australia — expressed through peaceful argument, writing, and voting, not threats.

**85. Respecting the rights of other people includes what?**
- A. Letting others speak, even when you disagree with them ✓
- B. Listening only to your own side of a debate
- C. Censoring newspapers you dislike
- D. Banning peaceful meetings of opponents
- *Explanation:* Your freedom exists alongside everyone else's. Respect means defending others' right to speak, too.

**86. Which statement about Australian values and laws is correct?**
- A. Values are just suggestions; laws never matter
- B. Australian values underpin the laws, and the laws must be obeyed by everyone ✓
- C. Values apply only to new migrants
- D. Laws apply only to citizens
- *Explanation:* Our laws are built on our values, and they apply to everyone in Australia — citizens, residents and visitors alike.

**87. What is expected of new Australian citizens?**
- A. To forget their first language completely
- B. To change their religion
- C. To live by Australian values and contribute to the community ✓
- D. To work only for the government
- *Explanation:* New citizens are welcomed as equals who share Australian values and contribute to community life.

**88. Which of these is a responsibility that comes with the privilege of Australian citizenship?**
- A. Owning a house
- B. Supporting a football team
- C. Speaking with an Australian accent
- D. Voting in elections, and serving on a jury if called ✓
- *Explanation:* Citizenship brings responsibilities: obeying the law, voting, and serving on a jury when called.

**89. Which of these is one of the privileges of Australian citizenship?**
- A. Applying for an Australian passport and voting in elections ✓
- B. Never having to pay tax
- C. Ignoring state laws
- D. Automatic employment by the government
- *Explanation:* Citizens can vote, stand for parliament, apply for an Australian passport and seek government help overseas.

**90. Why do Australians value the idea that 'no one is above the law'?**
- A. It makes good newspaper headlines
- B. It means leaders and officials are accountable, just like everyone else ✓
- C. It means police run the country
- D. It applies only in Canberra
- *Explanation:* If leaders break the law, they can be investigated, charged and removed — the law binds the powerful too.

**91. Donating blood, coaching a junior sports team and joining a volunteer fire brigade are all examples of what?**
- A. Compulsory duties enforced by fines
- B. Paid government employment
- C. Australians contributing to their community ✓
- D. Political campaigning
- *Explanation:* Everyday volunteering is how Australians put their values into action in the community.

**92. Which action best follows the value of helping others in need?**
- A. Walking past an injured person without calling for help
- B. Charging neighbours for help during an emergency
- C. Helping only people who share your religion
- D. Calling 000 for a stranger who has collapsed ✓
- *Explanation:* Helping a stranger in trouble — even just by calling 000 — is mateship and compassion in action.

**93. Australian values say that a person's worth is NOT measured by what?**
- A. Their wealth, background or status ✓
- B. Their kindness
- C. Their effort
- D. Their contribution to others
- *Explanation:* A person's worth does not depend on money, birth or status. Everyone is valued equally.

**94. Which of these reflects 'respect for the freedom and dignity of the individual'?**
- A. Forcing people to reveal how they voted
- B. Letting people make their own life choices, within the law ✓
- C. Telling families how many children to have
- D. Requiring approval for everyone's hobbies
- *Explanation:* Freedom and dignity mean each person chooses their own path in life, within the law and respecting others.

**95. In Australia, changing your religion — or choosing not to follow one — is what?**
- A. Forbidden by law
- B. Allowed only once in a lifetime
- C. A personal freedom in our society ✓
- D. Limited to Australian citizens
- *Explanation:* What you believe is your own choice. Australia protects freedom of religion and freedom from religion.

**96. Which of these groups is entitled to a 'fair go' in Australia?**
- A. Only Australian citizens
- B. Only people born in Australia
- C. Only the majority culture
- D. Everyone, whatever their background ✓
- *Explanation:* The fair go belongs to everyone in Australia — no matter where they were born or what they believe.

**97. What is the underlying message of Australian values for new citizens?**
- A. Australia welcomes you: share our values and obey our laws, and you belong here as an equal ✓
- B. Newcomers will always be second-class
- C. Values are a formality to sign and forget
- D. Only families who arrived long ago truly belong
- *Explanation:* Australian values are an invitation: everyone who shares them and obeys the laws belongs as an equal member of society.

**98. Which of these behaviours is a breach of trust in the Australian community?**
- A. Returning extra change a shop gave you by mistake
- B. Cheating the welfare system by giving false information ✓
- C. Questioning a council decision at a public meeting
- D. Starting a small business
- *Explanation:* Welfare is there to help people in genuine need. Cheating it steals from the whole community.

**99. Standing up against bullying when you see it reflects which values?**
- A. Obedience and silence
- B. Wealth and status
- C. Respect, compassion and looking out for others ✓
- D. Avoiding all involvement
- *Explanation:* Defending someone being bullied shows respect and compassion — core Australian values.

**100. Which of these sentences best sums up Australian values?**
- A. Everyone should look after themselves only
- B. Tradition matters more than people
- C. Obey without ever questioning
- D. Freedom, respect, fairness, equality of opportunity — a fair go for all ✓
- *Explanation:* Everything in the values statement comes back to this: freedom, respect, fairness and equality of opportunity for everyone.

**101. A new worker joins your team wearing religious dress. A workmate says she should not have been hired because of how she dresses. What is the right view?**
- A. She has the same right to work as anyone — people are judged on their work, not their religion or dress ✓
- B. The workmate is right — everyone should dress the same
- C. She should be moved to a job where customers cannot see her
- D. New workers should hide their religion at work
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion and equality mean a person can practise their faith and wear religious dress at work. What matters is the quality of their work.

**102. Your employer tells you to pay a newly arrived migrant worker less than the legal minimum wage 'because he is lucky to have a job'. What should happen?**
- A. It is fine — he agreed to take the job
- B. He must be paid at least the legal minimum, like every other worker ✓
- C. He can be paid less until his English improves
- D. He should be grateful and not complain
- *Explanation:* Australian law gives every worker the same minimum pay and conditions. Paying someone less because of their background is against the law and against a fair go.

**103. Two people apply for a promotion. One is clearly better qualified, but the other is the manager's close friend. Who should get the job?**
- A. The manager's friend — loyalty matters most
- B. Whoever has worked there longest, whatever their skills
- C. The better qualified person — jobs and promotions should go on merit ✓
- D. Neither — promotions cause jealousy
- *Explanation:* A fair go means jobs and promotions are decided by skills and merit, not by friendships or connections.

**104. A machine at your workplace is faulty and could injure someone. Your supervisor says to keep using it and say nothing. What is the right thing to do?**
- A. Keep working — the supervisor knows best
- B. Warn only your friends to stay away from it
- C. Use it yourself so others do not have to
- D. Stop and report the danger — everyone's safety at work matters ✓
- *Explanation:* Everyone has the right to a safe workplace, and everyone shares responsibility for safety. Dangers must be reported, not hidden.

**105. During Ramadan, a workmate is fasting. At a team lunch, others tease him and wave food in his face. What should you do?**
- A. Treat him with respect — teasing someone about their religion is wrong ✓
- B. Join in — it is only a joke
- C. Tell him to skip fasting while at work
- D. Eat somewhere else so you do not have to see it
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect means respecting other people's religious practices. Mocking someone's faith is a form of harassment.

**106. A woman returns to work after having a baby. Her manager stops giving her important tasks, saying 'her mind is on the baby now'. Is this fair?**
- A. Yes — mothers cannot be trusted with big tasks
- B. No — she should be treated on her ability, like everyone else ✓
- C. Yes — it protects her from stress
- D. Only if she complains about it
- *Explanation:* Men and women have equal rights at work. Assuming a mother is less capable is discrimination and denies her a fair go.

**107. You see a supervisor repeatedly shouting at and humiliating a young worker in front of others. What is the right response?**
- A. Stay out of it — it is the supervisor's business
- B. Film it and share it online for laughs
- C. Speak up or report it through the proper channels — bullying at work is never acceptable ✓
- D. Tell the young worker to shout back
- *Explanation:* Bullying and humiliation at work are against Australian values and can be against the law. Concerns should be raised safely through proper channels.

**108. You are on an interview panel. A well-qualified candidate uses a wheelchair, and another panel member says she 'would not fit in here'. What is the fair approach?**
- A. Reject her — first impressions matter
- B. Hire her only for a back-room job
- C. Ask her to prove she can cope before considering her
- D. Assess her skills for the role, like every other candidate ✓
- *Explanation:* People with disability have the same right to a fair go. Candidates must be judged on their ability to do the job, not on their disability.

**109. An apprentice makes an expensive mistake on his second day. How should a good Australian workplace respond?**
- A. Teach him what went wrong and support him to learn ✓
- B. Shout at him so he never forgets
- C. Make him pay for the damage from his pocket
- D. Give him only dirty jobs as punishment
- *Explanation:* Respect and a fair go include helping new workers learn. Humiliation and punishment are not how Australians treat people at work.

**110. A workmate asks to take short breaks to pray during the day. What is the reasonable response?**
- A. Refuse — religion must stay outside work completely
- B. Discuss it respectfully and accommodate it where reasonably possible ✓
- C. Agree only if everyone else gets extra breaks too
- D. Tell him to pray before he arrives and not again
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion applies at work too. Reasonable requests to practise a faith should be treated with respect.

**111. You notice the payroll office has accidentally been paying a casual worker less than the award rate for months. What should happen?**
- A. Nothing — she should have checked herself
- B. Wait to see if she ever notices
- C. Tell payroll so the mistake is fixed and she is back-paid ✓
- D. Offer her extra shifts instead of the missing money
- *Explanation:* Honesty and fairness at work mean mistakes in pay are corrected. Every worker is entitled to their full legal pay.

**112. A new worker speaks very little English. The safety training is only given in fast spoken English, and he clearly does not understand. What should the employer do?**
- A. Let him sign the form anyway
- B. Give him the most dangerous jobs so he learns quickly
- C. Send him home permanently
- D. Make sure he understands — for example with an interpreter, pictures or a buddy — because safety is for everyone ✓
- *Explanation:* A safe workplace means every worker understands the safety rules. Language barriers must be overcome, not ignored.

**113. At an office party, workmates pressure a colleague to drink alcohol even though his religion forbids it. What is the respectful thing to do?**
- A. Accept his choice — no one should be pressured to act against their beliefs ✓
- B. Keep pressuring him — one drink will not hurt
- C. Leave him out of future parties
- D. Spike his soft drink as a prank
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect includes respecting a person's religious beliefs and personal choices. Pressuring someone to drink is a form of harassment.

**114. A manager says a woman should not be made a supervisor because 'customers prefer dealing with a man'. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — the customer is always right
- B. It is discrimination — she must be judged on her ability to do the job ✓
- C. She should change jobs if she wants respect
- D. Supervising is not real work anyway
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women means women can hold any job, including leadership roles. Refusing a woman a role because of her sex is against the law.

**115. Your team leader asks you to sign a timesheet saying you worked hours you did not work, 'because the budget allows it'. What should you do?**
- A. Sign it — everyone does it
- B. Sign it only if you share the money
- C. Refuse — claiming pay for hours not worked is dishonest ✓
- D. Sign it and take extra days off instead
- *Explanation:* Honesty is expected in Australian workplaces. False claims for pay are a form of fraud.

**116. A skilled engineer who migrated to Australia is told her overseas qualifications 'probably are not real' and is offered only cleaning work. What is the fair response?**
- A. Accept it — overseas training never counts
- B. Offer her the cleaning job at half pay
- C. Ask her to change her name to sound more local
- D. Assess her actual skills and qualifications fairly, like any other applicant ✓
- *Explanation:* A fair go means a person's skills are assessed on their merits. Dismissing someone because of where they come from is discrimination.

**117. A colleague quietly tells you that another worker keeps sending her unwanted sexual messages. What is the right response?**
- A. Take her seriously and support her to report it — sexual harassment is against the law ✓
- B. Tell her to take it as a compliment
- C. Spread the story around the office
- D. Advise her to send messages back so he stops
- *Explanation:* Sexual harassment is unlawful in Australia. A person who reports it deserves support, and workplaces must act on complaints.

**118. In a meeting, your team leader presents your idea as his own and takes all the credit. Which response fits Australian values of fairness and respect?**
- A. Steal his ideas back next time
- B. Raise it calmly and honestly — people's work should be acknowledged fairly ✓
- C. Say nothing and stop sharing ideas forever
- D. Complain about him anonymously online
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect and honesty include giving credit where it is due. Problems at work should be raised openly and calmly.

**119. A customer in your shop is screaming abuse at a teenage cashier over a small mistake. What should you do?**
- A. Join in — the customer is always right
- B. Quietly leave so you are not involved
- C. Step in calmly and support the cashier — abuse of workers is never acceptable ✓
- D. Tell the teenager to scream back
- *Explanation:* Everyone deserves respect at work, including young workers. Abuse and threats are never an acceptable way to solve a problem.

**120. A worker with a disability asks for a modified desk and screen so she can do her job properly. What should the employer do?**
- A. Refuse — special treatment is unfair to others
- B. Move her to a job with no computer
- C. Tell her to buy the equipment herself
- D. Provide reasonable adjustments so she can do her job on an equal footing ✓
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity means removing barriers where reasonable, so people with disability can work to their full ability.

**121. Two workmates hold opposite political views and start debating at lunch. The debate stays friendly and calm. Is this a problem?**
- A. No — people are free to discuss different views respectfully ✓
- B. Yes — politics should be punished at work
- C. Yes — only one view should be allowed
- D. The boss should decide who is right
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech means people may hold and discuss different opinions, as long as they treat each other with respect.

**122. A business owner says he will only hire people from one ethnic background because 'they fit our culture'. What do Australian values and law say?**
- A. It is his business, so it is fine
- B. It is discrimination — hiring must be based on merit, not background ✓
- C. It is fine as long as he pays them well
- D. It is fine if customers prefer it
- *Explanation:* Choosing workers by race or background instead of merit is unlawful discrimination and denies others a fair go.

**123. You see a workmate slip on a wet floor and hurt his back. Your supervisor says not to record the injury. What should happen?**
- A. Agree — paperwork causes trouble
- B. Record it only if he needs hospital treatment
- C. The injury should be reported and recorded honestly, and the hazard fixed ✓
- D. Blame the worker for not watching his step
- *Explanation:* Workplace injuries must be reported honestly so workers get help and hazards are fixed. Hiding injuries puts others at risk.

**124. A senior tradesman refuses to train a new female apprentice, saying 'this is a man's trade'. What is the right view?**
- A. He is right — some jobs suit men only
- B. Give the apprentice to someone junior instead
- C. Send the apprentice to a different company
- D. He is wrong — women can learn and do any trade, and she deserves a fair go ✓
- *Explanation:* Men and women have equal rights to learn skills and work in any job. Refusing to train someone because she is a woman is discrimination.

**125. A café owner offers you a job paid in cash with no tax and no superannuation, 'so everyone wins'. What is the problem?**
- A. It is dishonest and against the law — work should be paid properly, with tax and super as required ✓
- B. There is no problem — cash is simpler
- C. It is only a problem if the café is caught
- D. It is the worker's win, so the law does not matter
- *Explanation:* Paying and being paid 'off the books' breaks tax law and leaves the worker without protections and superannuation. Honest dealing is an Australian value.

**126. A girl who has just arrived in Australia joins your class. Some students laugh every time she speaks because of her accent. What should happen?**
- A. She should stay quiet until her accent changes
- B. The class should welcome her — laughing at someone's accent is bullying ✓
- C. She should be placed in a class of her own
- D. The teacher should tell her to speak only at home
- *Explanation:* New Australians deserve a fair go at school. Mocking a person's accent or background is bullying and against the value of mutual respect.

**127. A relative tells a bright 15-year-old girl that girls in their family do not need much education and she should leave school early. What do Australian values say?**
- A. The relative is right — family custom comes first
- B. She can learn everything she needs at home
- C. She has the same right to education as any boy, and her schooling matters ✓
- D. Education is only for children who want office jobs
- *Explanation:* Girls and boys have an equal right to education in Australia. A girl's learning and future are just as important as a boy's.

**128. A boy finds out his classmates have created a group chat to share cruel jokes and edited photos of him. What is the right thing for him to do?**
- A. Create a worse chat about them
- B. Say nothing and hope it stops
- C. Leave school and never come back
- D. Tell a trusted adult, such as a parent or teacher — online bullying is not acceptable ✓
- *Explanation:* Bullying is wrong wherever it happens, including online. Schools and parents can act to stop it, and serious online abuse can be reported.

**129. The night before an assignment is due, your friend offers to let you copy hers and hand it in as your own work. What is the honest choice?**
- A. Refuse and hand in your own work, even if it is not perfect ✓
- B. Copy it — everyone does it sometimes
- C. Copy it but change a few words
- D. Pay her for it so it is fair
- *Explanation:* Honesty matters at school. Handing in someone else's work as your own is cheating and is unfair to students who did their own work.

**130. At a school sports day, a father demands that girls be removed from the running races because he believes girls should not compete with boys. What should the school do?**
- A. Remove the girls to keep the peace
- B. Let the girls compete — girls and boys have equal rights to take part ✓
- C. Hold the girls' races where nobody can watch
- D. Let girls race only against much younger boys
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women starts at school. Girls have the same right as boys to learn, play and compete.

**131. A student who uses a wheelchair wants to go on the school camp, but it would require some extra planning. What is the fair approach?**
- A. Leave him behind with a teacher
- B. Cancel the camp for everyone
- C. Plan the camp so he can take part like everyone else ✓
- D. Let him come only if his parents do all the extra work
- *Explanation:* Equality of opportunity means making reasonable adjustments so students with disability can join in school life fully.

**132. Students notice their teacher always gives higher marks to students from her own cultural background, whatever the quality of the work. Why is this wrong?**
- A. It is not wrong — teachers may favour their own
- B. Marks do not matter, so no harm is done
- C. Other students should try to change their background
- D. Marks should be earned by the quality of the work — favouring one group is unfair discrimination ✓
- *Explanation:* A fair go at school means results come from effort and ability. Favouritism based on background is discrimination.

**133. A boy in your class never joins school excursions because his family cannot afford them, and some students call him 'the poor kid'. What fits Australian values?**
- A. He should be treated with the same respect as everyone — a person's worth is not measured by money ✓
- B. He should change schools
- C. Poor students should expect less
- D. He should hide his family's situation and lie about it
- *Explanation:* No one should be looked down on because of their family's wealth. Everyone at school deserves equal respect and a fair chance to join in.

**134. For a group project, a student refuses to work with a classmate because the classmate follows a different religion. What should happen?**
- A. Let them swap — religion is a good reason
- B. They should work together respectfully — we do not exclude people for their beliefs ✓
- C. Put all students of that religion in one group
- D. Give the refusing student a solo project with easier marking
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion and mutual respect mean students of all faiths and none learn and work together.

**135. During an exam, the student next to you whispers and asks for your answers. What is the right thing to do?**
- A. Whisper the answers — helping friends comes first
- B. Show your paper but cover the hard questions
- C. Refuse politely and do your own exam — cheating is dishonest and unfair ✓
- D. Give wrong answers to teach them a lesson
- *Explanation:* Honesty in exams is fair to everyone. Cheating devalues the work of students who studied.

**136. At school, some boys grab at a Sikh student's turban and call it a game. Why must this stop?**
- A. Only because a teacher might see them
- B. The turban might get dirty
- C. Games are banned at school
- D. It is bullying and shows disrespect for his religion — everyone has the right to feel safe at school ✓
- *Explanation:* Religious dress must be respected. Touching or mocking it is bullying and an attack on a person's freedom of religion.

**137. Your school is electing a student council. A classmate says you should not vote for a certain candidate 'because her family came here as refugees'. What is the fair view?**
- A. Judge every candidate on their ideas and character, not their background ✓
- B. Vote only for students born in Australia
- C. Refugee families should not be involved in school life
- D. It is safer to vote for the most popular student
- *Explanation:* Democracy and a fair go mean people are judged on what they offer, not where they or their families came from.

**138. A girl wins the school science prize. A boy in her class says, 'Science is for boys — she must have cheated.' What is wrong with this attitude?**
- A. Nothing — he is just disappointed
- B. It denies her achievement and assumes girls are less capable — girls and boys are equal ✓
- C. Girls should share prizes to avoid upsetting boys
- D. Science prizes should be given to boys and girls in turn
