- *Explanation:* Girls are just as capable as boys in every subject. Dismissing a girl's success because of her sex is discrimination.

**139. A parent offers a school office worker money to move their child into a popular class ahead of other children. What is this?**
- A. A generous donation
- B. Good parenting
- C. A bribe — places should be allocated fairly, by the rules, not bought ✓
- D. Acceptable if the money helps the school
- *Explanation:* Offering money for favours is bribery. Fair systems give everyone an equal chance under the same rules.

**140. Auditions are held for the school play. The teacher casting the lead role should choose whom?**
- A. The child of the school's biggest donor
- B. The most popular student
- C. A student from the teacher's own background
- D. The student who auditions best for the role, whatever their background ✓
- *Explanation:* A fair go means opportunities at school are decided on talent and effort, not wealth, popularity or background.

**141. Some children in the playground imitate and laugh at a classmate who stutters when he speaks. What should a bystander do?**
- A. Not join in, and tell the children to stop or get a teacher — mocking a disability is bullying ✓
- B. Laugh quietly so he does not notice
- C. Record it to show him later
- D. Tell him to speak less so it stops
- *Explanation:* Everyone deserves to feel safe and respected at school. Standing against bullying shows respect and compassion.

**142. A migrant mother cannot read the English notes sent home from school and keeps missing important information. What should the school do?**
- A. Fine the family for not reading the notes
- B. Help her access the information, for example with translations or an interpreter ✓
- C. Send the notes only to the child to translate
- D. Stop sending her notes at all
- *Explanation:* Parents have the right to take part in their children's education. Helping families overcome language barriers shows respect and inclusion.

**143. A 16-year-old works weekends at a fast-food shop. The manager asks him to clean a deep fryer alone without training. What do Australian values and laws say?**
- A. Young workers must prove themselves with dangerous jobs
- B. It is fine — he is nearly an adult
- C. He has the same right to a safe workplace as any worker — untrained staff must not be given dangerous tasks ✓
- D. He should refuse all cleaning work
- *Explanation:* Workplace safety laws protect young workers too. Employers must train staff properly and keep everyone safe.

**144. A group of older students waits near the school gate each day demanding lunch money from younger students, threatening them if they refuse. What is this?**
- A. A normal part of growing up
- B. A fair tax on younger students
- C. Just joking around
- D. Bullying and extortion — it should be reported so the school can stop it ✓
- *Explanation:* Demanding money with threats is bullying and can be a crime. Every student has the right to feel safe at school.

**145. A girl tells the school counsellor she wants to study engineering. The counsellor says, 'That is not really a subject for girls — try something else.' What is wrong here?**
- A. Girls can study and work in any field — steering her away because she is a girl is discrimination ✓
- B. Nothing — counsellors know best
- C. Engineering schools do not accept girls
- D. She should choose what her family picks for her
- *Explanation:* Equality means girls are free to choose any subject or career. No job or field of study is closed to women in Australia.

**146. In a class debate, two students strongly disagree about an issue. One starts shouting insults at the other. What does respectful debate require?**
- A. The loudest student wins
- B. Argue the ideas, not the person — and listen to each other ✓
- C. Students should never disagree in class
- D. The teacher must punish whichever view is unpopular
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech works through respectful discussion. Disagreeing with ideas is fine; abusing people is not.

**147. A student's family celebrates a festival that most of the class has never heard of. The teacher invites the student to explain it to the class. What does this show?**
- A. The teacher is wasting lesson time
- B. Only Australian-born customs belong in class
- C. Respect for the different cultures that make up Australian society ✓
- D. The student should keep their culture private instead
- *Explanation:* Australia is a multicultural society. Learning about each other's cultures builds mutual respect and understanding.

**148. After his team loses the school final, a teenage boy is crying. His teammates tell him to stop 'crying like a girl'. What is the kinder and fairer response?**
- A. Agree — boys should hide their feelings
- B. Film him so the team can laugh later
- C. Leave him alone in the change room
- D. Support him — everyone, boys and girls, has feelings and deserves respect ✓
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect includes how we treat people when they are upset. Mocking someone's emotions, or using 'girl' as an insult, is disrespectful.

**149. A father who speaks little English wants to help at the school fete, but says he is embarrassed about his English. What fits Australian values?**
- A. Welcome his help — everyone can contribute to the school community, whatever their English level ✓
- B. Tell him to stay home until his English is perfect
- C. Give him a job where no one can hear him speak
- D. Only parents born in Australia should volunteer
- *Explanation:* Community life is richer when everyone can take part. A person's value to the community does not depend on their level of English.

**150. A student is caught taking money from the school canteen. Some teachers suggest parading him in front of assembly to shame him. What is the fair way to handle it?**
- A. Parade him — shame teaches a lesson
- B. Deal with it through the school's fair discipline process — everyone deserves fair treatment, even when they do wrong ✓
- C. Beat him so he remembers
- D. Expel him with no chance to explain
- *Explanation:* Fairness applies to people who break rules too. Consequences should follow a fair process, not public humiliation or violence.

**151. Your friend confides that her boyfriend reads all her messages, tells her which friends she can see, and she is frightened of his anger. What is this?**
- A. A sign that he loves her very much
- B. Normal behaviour in a new relationship
- C. Controlling behaviour, which is a form of family violence — she deserves support and help is available ✓
- D. Her fault for making him jealous
- *Explanation:* Controlling a partner's movements, contacts and phone is abuse, not love. Support services can help people in this situation.

**152. At a family barbecue, an uncle says a woman who leaves a violent husband is 'destroying the family'. What is the right view?**
- A. He is right — family unity matters more than safety
- B. She should leave only if the children are also hit
- C. She should stay and try to change him
- D. No one should have to stay with a violent partner — violence is a crime and leaving to be safe is her right ✓
- *Explanation:* Family violence is a serious crime in Australia. A person's safety always matters more than appearances or 'keeping the family together'.

**153. Your 17-year-old cousin is upset: her parents are planning to take her overseas to marry a man she has never met, and she does not want to go. What is important to know?**
- A. Forcing someone to marry is a crime in Australia, even if the wedding is overseas — help is available ✓
- B. She must obey — parents always decide
- C. It is fine as long as the man is kind
- D. She can only refuse after she turns 21
- *Explanation:* Forced marriage is a criminal offence in Australia, including taking someone overseas for marriage. The person must freely agree.

**154. An elderly man tells you his adult son has taken his bank card and withdraws money for himself, leaving the old man short of money for food. What is this?**
- A. A family matter that is nobody else's business
- B. Elder abuse and theft — he can get help to protect himself and his money ✓
- C. Normal — children inherit everything anyway
- D. The father's fault for trusting his son
- *Explanation:* Taking an older person's money by pressure or without permission is abuse and may be a crime. Elder abuse support services can help.

**155. A married couple disagrees about a big purchase. The husband says, 'I earn more, so I decide.' What do Australian values say?**
- A. He is right — the higher earner rules the family
- B. They should let the husband's parents decide
- C. Partners are equals — big decisions should be made together, with mutual respect ✓
- D. The wife should hide her own purchases in revenge
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women applies inside marriage. Neither partner has the right to control the other.

**156. Late at night you hear shouting, crying and the sound of things breaking in the house next door, and you think someone is being assaulted. What should you do?**
- A. Turn up your television — it is a private family matter
- B. Go over and threaten the people inside yourself
- C. Wait a few days to see if it happens again
- D. Call the police on 000 — someone may be in danger ✓
- *Explanation:* Family violence is a crime, not a private matter. Calling 000 in an emergency can protect someone from serious harm.

**157. A father stays home full-time to care for the children while his partner works. A relative mocks him, saying 'that is women's work'. What is the right view?**
- A. He and his partner are free to arrange their family life as they choose — caring for children is valuable work for any parent ✓
- B. The relative is right — he should get a 'proper' job
- C. Children suffer when fathers care for them
- D. He should pretend he works outside the home
- *Explanation:* Men and women are equal and free to choose their roles. There is no rule that caring for children belongs to one sex.

**158. A woman wants to open her own bank account and manage some of her own earnings. Her husband says wives in his family have never needed their own accounts. What are her rights?**
- A. She needs his permission first
- B. She has the same financial rights as any adult — she can open an account in her own name ✓
- C. She can only have an account if she earns more than him
- D. She should ask her father to open one for her
- *Explanation:* Women in Australia have full and equal legal and financial rights. No adult needs a spouse's permission to have a bank account.

**159. A single father takes his toddler to a community playgroup. Another parent says, 'Playgroups are really for mothers.' What is the fair response?**
- A. He should leave quietly
- B. Fathers should run their own separate groups only
- C. Fathers are equal parents and are welcome — parenting is not only women's business ✓
- D. He can stay only if a mother vouches for him
- *Explanation:* Men and women share equally in raising children. Fathers have the same right to be involved in their children's lives and activities.

**160. A 24-year-old woman wants to marry a man from a different religion. Her parents threaten to cut her off unless she marries someone of their choosing instead. What do Australian values say?**
- A. She must obey — parents choose in their culture
- B. She should marry in secret and never tell them
- C. Religion should decide everyone's marriage
- D. She is an adult and free to choose her own partner — no one may force or pressure her into a different marriage ✓
- *Explanation:* Adults in Australia choose their own partners. Pressuring or forcing someone into a marriage is against our values and can be a crime.

**161. A young man tells his family he is not ready to marry and wants to wait several years. Relatives keep pressuring him to marry now. What is his right?**
- A. Marriage must be his free choice — no one should be pressured into marrying before they are ready ✓
- B. He must marry when the family decides
- C. He can refuse only if he pays the family compensation
- D. He should marry and divorce quickly to satisfy them
- *Explanation:* In Australia, marriage is the free choice of the two people marrying. Family pressure must never replace genuine consent.

**162. After a divorce, a court order says the children spend weekends with their mother. The father refuses to hand them over, saying 'the court does not run my family'. What must happen?**
- A. He is right — fathers outrank courts
- B. He must obey the court order — everyone must follow the law, and orders can only be changed through the court ✓
- C. The mother should take the children by force
- D. Court orders are optional suggestions
- *Explanation:* Commitment to the rule of law means court decisions must be obeyed, even when we disagree with them. The legal process is the way to seek a change.

**163. An elderly grandmother who migrated to Australia likes to wear her traditional clothing and speak her first language with her family at home. A neighbour complains this is 'not Australian'. What is the right view?**
- A. She should only speak English everywhere
- B. She should stay indoors so she is not seen
- C. She is free to keep her culture and language — Australia respects the customs of its people, within the law ✓
- D. Traditional clothing should be banned in public
- *Explanation:* Australia is a multicultural society. People may keep their languages, dress and customs while sharing Australian values and obeying Australian law.

**164. A woman is promoted and now earns more than her husband. His relatives say she should quit her job so he is not embarrassed. What do Australian values say?**
- A. She should quit — a wife must not out-earn her husband
- B. She should give her pay to her husband to manage
- C. She should apologise for her success
- D. Her success is hers to enjoy — women have the same right to work and succeed as men ✓
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women means a woman's career and earnings are worth the same as a man's. No one should be held back to protect someone else's pride.

**165. Two families arrange for their adult children to meet, hoping they might like each other. Both young people are happy to meet and know they can say no afterwards. Is this acceptable in Australia?**
- A. Yes — introductions are fine when both people are completely free to accept or refuse ✓
- B. No — families may never be involved at all
- C. Yes — but afterwards they must marry
- D. Only if the families sign a contract
- *Explanation:* What matters is free consent. Meeting through family is fine; being forced, pressured or unable to refuse is not.

**166. A husband refuses to let his wife learn to drive or leave the house without him, saying 'I decide where my wife goes'. What is this behaviour?**
- A. Good care — he is protecting her
- B. Controlling behaviour — his wife has the same freedom of movement as any adult ✓
- C. Normal in every marriage
- D. Acceptable if he drives her wherever she asks
- *Explanation:* Adults in Australia are free to move about, learn and live independently. Controlling a partner's movements is a form of abuse.

**167. You learn that a six-year-old in your street is left home alone every evening while her parents work night shifts. Why is this a serious concern?**
- A. Children should be seen and not heard
- B. It is only a problem if neighbours complain
- C. Young children must be properly supervised and kept safe — leaving them alone puts them at risk ✓
- D. The parents should send her to live with strangers
- *Explanation:* Australian law and values put children's safety first. Parents must arrange proper care and supervision for young children.

**168. A couple adopts a young child whose birth family came from another country. What does that child deserve?**
- A. To be treated as less than children born into the family
- B. To forget where they came from completely
- C. Fewer opportunities so expectations stay low
- D. The same love, safety, respect and opportunities as any other child ✓
- *Explanation:* Every child deserves to be safe, valued and given a fair go, whatever their background or how they joined their family.

**169. A married woman decides she does not want to have children. Relatives tell her she is failing in her duty as a wife. What do Australian values say?**
- A. It is her personal choice — adults are free to decide about their own lives and families ✓
- B. The relatives are right — every wife must have children
- C. Her husband should decide for her
- D. She should have children to keep the family happy
- *Explanation:* Respect for the freedom and dignity of the individual means personal decisions about having children belong to the person themselves.

**170. A neighbour tells you that when her partner's children misbehave, he hits them with a belt, leaving bruises. She says, 'That is just discipline.' What is the right view?**
- A. It is his right as a parent figure
- B. Children must be protected from harm — violence that injures children is against the law, and help is available ✓
- C. Bruises are fine if the children were naughty
- D. It is a private matter for their house only
- *Explanation:* Children have the right to be safe. Violence that harms children is never acceptable discipline in Australia.

**171. When their father dies, two brothers tell their sister she should give up her share of the inheritance 'because her husband provides for her'. What are her rights?**
- A. She has no rights — sons inherit first
- B. She should give it up to keep the peace
- C. She has the same legal rights as her brothers — her share is hers by law ✓
- D. Her husband should claim it on her behalf
- *Explanation:* Men and women have equal rights under Australian law, including in property and inheritance. No one can pressure a woman out of what is legally hers.

**172. New parents agree to share the night feeds, housework and care of their baby equally. Which value does this best show?**
- A. Obedience
- B. Competition between partners
- C. Keeping up appearances
- D. Equality of men and women in everyday family life ✓
- *Explanation:* Equality is lived at home as well as in law: partners sharing work and decisions as equals.

**173. A 19-year-old tells you his father takes all of his wages and hits him when he asks for money of his own. Why is this wrong?**
- A. Young adults have rights too — taking someone's earnings by force and using violence is abuse ✓
- B. Parents own their children's wages until they marry
- C. He should just leave with no support or plan
- D. It is fine — he lives in his father's house
- *Explanation:* Violence and financial control are abuse at any age. Support services can help people in this situation find safety.

**174. A woman needs to see a doctor, but her partner insists she cannot go without his permission and refuses to let her leave. What do Australian values say?**
- A. She must wait until he agrees
- B. She is free to seek medical care — no adult needs a partner's permission to look after their health ✓
- C. Only unmarried women can see doctors freely
- D. She should ask her partner's parents instead
- *Explanation:* Every adult in Australia can make their own decisions about their health. Controlling a partner's access to medical care is abuse.

**175. A friend says her partner 'only pushes and shoves her — he never actually hits her, so it is not violence'. What is the truth?**
- A. She is right — pushing does not count
- B. It only counts if it happens in public
- C. Pushing and shoving is violence too — any physical abuse in a relationship is wrong and against the law ✓
- D. It is acceptable if he apologises afterwards
- *Explanation:* Family violence includes all physical abuse — pushing, shoving, grabbing and hitting. Help is available, whatever form the violence takes.

**176. A family that has recently migrated to Australia moves into your street. Another neighbour suggests the street 'keep an eye on them' and leave them out of street events. What fits Australian values?**
- A. Agree — new people must earn trust over years
- B. Only invite them if they cook Australian food
- C. Warn them to keep to themselves
- D. Welcome them like any new neighbours — everyone deserves a fair go and a friendly start ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia welcomes people from all over the world. New neighbours deserve the same friendliness and inclusion as anyone else.

**177. Your neighbour's dog barks loudly early every morning and it is disturbing your sleep. What is the Australian way to handle it?**
- A. Speak to your neighbour politely first — and use council processes if it continues ✓
- B. Throw things at the dog
- C. Bark back with loud music at midnight
- D. Let the dog out onto the road
- *Explanation:* Problems between neighbours should be handled calmly and lawfully: a friendly conversation first, then mediation or council help if needed.

**178. Your elderly neighbour struggles to wheel her heavy bins to the kerb each week. What is a simple act of mateship?**
- A. Complain to the council about her bins
- B. Offer to take her bins out and bring them in for her ✓
- C. Move her bins so the truck misses them
- D. Tell her to pay for a private service
- *Explanation:* Mateship is shown in small, practical kindnesses — helping neighbours with everyday things they find hard.

**179. A house in your street plays very loud music until 2 am several nights a week, keeping everyone awake. What is the right approach?**
- A. Smash their letterbox in protest
- B. Accept it — people may do anything at home
- C. Ask them politely to turn it down, and if it continues, report it — noise rules apply to everyone ✓
- D. Hold an even louder party the next night
- *Explanation:* Everyone must follow local noise laws, and everyone deserves a reasonable night's sleep. Disputes are handled politely and through proper channels.

**180. A family in your street holds small prayer meetings at their home. A neighbour wants the council to ban the meetings 'because it is not our religion'. What do Australian values say?**
- A. The council should ban them
- B. Only the largest religion may meet in homes
- C. They should meet only in secret
- D. People are free to practise their religion, including at home, as long as they follow the same laws as everyone else ✓
- *Explanation:* Freedom of religion protects everyone's worship, not just the majority's. The same laws about noise and parking apply equally to all.

**181. Children from next door keep kicking their ball into your garden and climbing the fence to fetch it. What is the best first step?**
- A. Talk calmly with the children and their parents to work something out ✓
- B. Keep the ball and puncture it
- C. Shout threats at the children
- D. Fine the children for entering your garden
- *Explanation:* Most neighbour problems are solved by calm conversation. Threats and damaging property only make things worse.

**182. Your neighbourhood watch group suggests reporting anyone who 'looks foreign' walking in the street, even if they are doing nothing wrong. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — it keeps the street safe
- B. People must never be treated as suspicious because of their appearance or race — only behaviour matters ✓
- C. It should apply only at night
- D. New residents should carry papers to show the group
- *Explanation:* Judging people by their race or appearance is discrimination. Safety concerns are about what people do, never about how they look.

**183. You find a wallet full of cash on the footpath near your home. No one is around. What does honesty require?**
- A. Keep the cash — finders keepers
- B. Take the cash and throw the wallet away
- C. Hand it in to the police or try to return it to its owner ✓
- D. Keep it for a week and spend it if nobody asks
- *Explanation:* Honesty and respect for others' property mean making a real effort to return what is not yours.

**184. You see a teenager in the park next door deliberately injuring a protected native bird for fun. What is the right response?**
- A. Join in — it is only a bird
- B. Ignore it — teenagers will be teenagers
- C. Film it and post it for views
- D. Stop it if it is safe, and report it — cruelty to animals is wrong and native wildlife is protected by law ✓
- *Explanation:* Australia's native animals are protected, and cruelty to animals is an offence. We share responsibility for caring for our wildlife.

**185. At a street party, a new Australian family brings dishes from their own culture to share. Some residents whisper that they should have brought 'normal food'. What fits our values?**
- A. Welcome their contribution — sharing food from many cultures is part of Australian community life ✓
- B. Ask them to bring Australian food next time
- C. Put their dishes at a separate table
- D. Tell them street parties are for locals only
- *Explanation:* Multiculturalism means the foods, festivals and customs of all Australians enrich community life and are welcomed.

**186. A neighbour who uses a wheelchair asks the body corporate to install a ramp at the building entrance. One owner objects because 'it will change the look of the building'. What matters most?**
- A. The look of the building
- B. Her right to access her own home — reasonable access for people with disability is a matter of equality ✓
- C. She should move to a different building
- D. She can use the back entrance through the car park forever
- *Explanation:* Equality means people with disability can access their homes and community like everyone else. Reasonable adjustments should be made.

**187. Before an election, your neighbour puts a large sign for a political party you dislike in their front yard. What is their right?**
- A. They must remove it if neighbours object
- B. Only the most popular party may display signs
- C. They are free to express their political views peacefully — that is part of democracy ✓
- D. You may paint over the sign
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech includes supporting the party or candidate of your choice. Disagreeing never justifies damaging someone's property.

**188. You realise your new security camera also records your neighbour's backyard and their children playing. What does mutual respect suggest you do?**
- A. Keep recording — it is your camera
- B. Sell the footage to a website
- C. Point another camera at their windows
- D. Adjust the camera to respect their privacy, and talk with them about it ✓
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect includes respecting other people's privacy in their own home. Small courtesies protect good neighbourly relations.

**189. During a long drought, your town is placed on water restrictions. You notice a neighbour watering his lawn with a hose in the middle of the day, well outside the allowed times. Why does this matter?**
- A. Water in a drought is a shared resource — the restrictions apply to everyone so there is enough for all ✓
- B. It does not matter — one lawn makes no difference
- C. Only farmers need to save water
- D. He should water at night so nobody sees
- *Explanation:* Looking after shared resources is part of the public good. Rules like water restrictions work only when everyone follows them.

**190. A young driver keeps racing at high speed down your quiet street where children play. What is the right thing to do?**
- A. Throw something at the car as it passes
- B. Note the details and report the dangerous driving to the police ✓
- C. Race him to teach him a lesson
- D. Say nothing — it is his car and his street too
- *Explanation:* Dangerous driving puts lives at risk. Reporting it to police protects the whole community, especially children.

**191. An Aboriginal family moves into your street. Someone leaves a note in their letterbox telling them to 'go back where you came from'. What is the right response from the street?**
- A. Ignore it — the note is probably a joke
- B. Advise the family to move somewhere else
- C. Reject the abuse — Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples are the First Peoples of this land, and racism has no place in the street ✓
- D. Ask the family to keep a low profile
- *Explanation:* Racist abuse is never acceptable. Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples are Australia's first inhabitants and equal members of every community.

**192. A neighbour asks you to sign a petition to stop a place of worship for another faith being built in your suburb, saying 'we should keep them out'. What do Australian values say?**
- A. Sign it — the majority should decide who worships where
- B. Sign it only if the faith is small
- C. Faith groups should build only in their own suburbs
- D. Refuse — freedom of religion means every faith community has the same rights under the law ✓
- *Explanation:* No faith may be excluded from a community. Planning and building laws apply equally to places of worship of all religions.

**193. You borrow your neighbour's ladder and accidentally break one of its rungs. Nobody saw it happen. What should you do?**
- A. Tell your neighbour honestly and offer to repair or replace it ✓
- B. Return it quietly and say nothing
- C. Blame the ladder for being old
- D. Avoid your neighbour until they forget
- *Explanation:* Honesty means owning up to damage you caused, even when nobody saw it, and putting it right.

**194. Smoke from your neighbour's backyard fire drifts into your house most evenings. What is the best approach?**
- A. Spray their fire with your hose without asking
- B. Talk to them politely about it — and know that local rules about fires apply to everyone ✓
- C. Start a bigger fire of your own
- D. Complain about them anonymously but never speak to them
- *Explanation:* Calm conversation solves most neighbourhood issues. Local fire and smoke rules exist to protect everyone's health and safety.

**195. Your neighbour's visitors keep parking across your driveway, so you cannot get your car out. What is the right approach?**
- A. Let their tyres down
- B. Block their cars in so they learn
- C. Ask them politely to keep the driveway clear — parking across driveways is also against the road rules ✓
- D. Say nothing and miss your appointments
- *Explanation:* Respect works both ways: neighbours should park considerately, and problems should be raised politely and lawfully.

**196. Your neighbours are away on holiday. You notice their mail piling up and water leaking from a burst pipe onto the footpath. What would a good neighbour do?**
- A. Ignore it — their house, their problem
- B. Read their mail to find a contact number
- C. Go inside and help yourself to their tools to fix it
- D. Contact them if you can, and if the leak is serious, turn off the water at the meter or call for help ✓
- *Explanation:* Looking out for each other is part of community life. Acting to prevent serious damage to a neighbour's home is mateship in action.

**197. A private social media group for your street decides to exclude the families who rent their homes, saying 'renters are not real locals'. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Everyone who lives in the community belongs in it — renters deserve the same respect and inclusion as owners ✓
- B. Nothing — owners matter more
- C. Renters should be grateful to live there
- D. The group should charge renters to join
- *Explanation:* A person's worth in a community does not depend on owning property. Inclusion and respect are for everyone.

**198. A neighbour offers you cash to help him dump a trailer-load of building waste in the bush at night, 'to save tip fees'. What should you do?**
- A. Help him — tip fees are too high
- B. Refuse — illegal dumping damages the environment we all share, and it is against the law ✓
- C. Help him but wear gloves so there are no fingerprints
- D. Dump only half and leave the rest
- *Explanation:* Our bush and waterways belong to everyone. Illegal dumping is an offence and harms the country and community we share.

**199. A man in your street loses his job. Without being asked, neighbours organise a roster to mow his lawn, drop off meals and help him look for work. This is an example of what?**
- A. Interfering in someone's private life
- B. A publicity stunt
- C. Mateship and compassion — Australians look out for each other in hard times ✓
- D. Something only family should do
- *Explanation:* Helping neighbours through tough times, without being asked or expecting anything back, is the spirit of mateship.

**200. The fence between your house and your neighbour's is falling down and needs replacing. What is the fair Australian approach?**
- A. Build it yourself and send them the whole bill without discussion
- B. Let it fall down and blame them
- C. Build a much taller fence to block them out
- D. Discuss it with your neighbour and share the cost fairly, following the fencing rules ✓
- *Explanation:* Shared problems are solved by talking and sharing costs fairly. Fencing laws exist so neighbours can resolve these issues justly.

**201. In a group chat with friends, one person keeps posting racist memes 'just as a joke'. What is the right response?**
- A. Do not share them, and say they are not funny — racist content demeans people, even as a 'joke' ✓
- B. Forward the funniest ones to more friends
- C. Stay silent — it is only humour
- D. Post memes about his background in return
- *Explanation:* Racism is never harmless entertainment. Mutual respect applies online just as it does face to face.

**202. A video of a student being beaten up at a bus stop is being shared around your school online. What should you do with it?**
- A. Share it before it gets taken down
- B. Do not forward it — report it and check whether the victim is getting help ✓
- C. Add funny captions and repost it
- D. Tag the victim so they can see it
- *Explanation:* Sharing violence for entertainment adds to the victim's harm. The right response is to report it and support the person hurt.

**203. A friend is applying for a government payment online. He suggests exaggerating his situation 'because the form cannot check everything'. What is this?**
- A. Smart use of the system
- B. Victimless, so it is acceptable
- C. Fraud — claims to government must be honest, based on true information ✓
- D. Fine if he only does it once
- *Explanation:* Public money supports people in genuine need. Lying on a claim steals from the whole community and is a criminal offence.

**204. In an online gaming forum, a man tells a woman to 'get out — gaming is for men'. What do Australian values say?**
- A. He is entitled to defend his hobby
- B. Women should use male names online
- C. She should leave to keep the peace
- D. Women belong in every space men do, online and offline — abuse based on sex is wrong ✓
- *Explanation:* Equality of men and women applies everywhere, including online communities. Harassment is never acceptable.

**205. After an argument, your neighbour posts your home address and photos of your house online, urging people to 'teach you a lesson'. What is this?**
- A. Dangerous harassment — it should be reported and removed ✓
- B. A fair way to win an argument
- C. Free speech with no limits
- D. Fine if the photos are from the street
- *Explanation:* Publishing someone's address to invite trouble puts them at risk. Disputes must be handled lawfully and peacefully, never by intimidation.

**206. You see a dramatic story shared online claiming a group of newcomers is 'taking over' a suburb, but it comes from an unknown page with no evidence. What is the responsible thing to do?**
- A. Share it quickly before it is deleted
- B. Check reliable sources before believing or sharing it — spreading fear about a group harms community harmony ✓
- C. Add your own angry comment to boost it
- D. Assume it is true because it feels true
- *Explanation:* A healthy community depends on truth and fairness. Unverified claims that target a group can spread fear and division.

**207. A 14-year-old tells you an adult she met in a game chat has asked her to send private photos of herself. What must happen?**
- A. She should send one photo so he leaves her alone
- B. She should keep it secret so she is not in trouble
- C. It is a serious crime for an adult to request such photos — she should tell a trusted adult and report it to police ✓
- D. She should ask him for photos first
- *Explanation:* Adults who seek sexual images from children are committing a serious offence. Children in this situation are never to blame and must be protected.

**208. An online seller offers you a nearly new phone at a quarter of the normal price, saying 'no questions, no receipt'. It is probably stolen. What should you do?**
- A. Buy it — the price is too good to refuse
- B. Buy it and resell it quickly
- C. Buy it only if it is a gift for someone else
- D. Refuse — buying stolen goods is against the law and supports crime ✓
- *Explanation:* Receiving stolen property is an offence. Honest dealing means not profiting from someone else's loss.

**209. A workmate is secretly filmed struggling with a task, and the video is posted online with mocking captions about his disability. What is wrong with this?**
- A. It violates his privacy and dignity — mocking a person with disability is bullying and cruelty ✓
- B. Nothing — filming in public is always allowed
- C. He should learn to laugh at himself
- D. It is only wrong if his boss sees it
- *Explanation:* Every person deserves dignity and privacy. Using someone's image to mock them is a breach of respect and may breach the law.

**210. The administrator of a community page for your suburb quietly deletes every post written by members of one particular background. What is this?**
- A. Good management of the page
- B. Discrimination — in a shared community space, everyone deserves a fair hearing ✓
- C. Acceptable if the admin owns the page
- D. Fine, because those members can start their own page
- *Explanation:* Excluding people because of their background is against the value of a fair go, in community spaces online just as offline.

**211. During a heated online argument about politics, a man posts, 'I know where you live — watch yourself.' What is this?**
- A. Strong but normal debate
- B. His democratic right
- C. A threat — threatening violence is a crime, and political disagreement never justifies it ✓
- D. Acceptable if he does not really mean it
- *Explanation:* Freedom of speech protects peaceful argument, never threats. Threatening another person is a criminal offence in Australia.

**212. Your friend livestreams herself to social media while driving on a busy road, reading comments as she goes. What should you do?**
- A. Send her comments to keep the stream lively
- B. Share the stream with more people
- C. Do the same next time you drive
- D. Tell her to stop — using a phone while driving endangers everyone and is against the law ✓
- *Explanation:* Road safety is a shared responsibility. Distracted driving kills, and helping a friend stop dangerous behaviour is an act of care.

**213. Your elderly neighbour tells you she is about to send her savings to a kind man she met online who 'needs help with a business emergency'. What is the right response?**
- A. Gently warn her this is a common scam, and help her check and report it before she sends anything ✓
- B. Mind your own business — it is her money
- C. Suggest she send half now and half later
- D. Offer to send the money for her to save fees
- *Explanation:* Compassion includes protecting vulnerable people from harm. Online romance and investment scams steal life savings from older Australians every year.

**214. After a flood, a social media page appears asking for donations, using photos stolen from news sites. The organiser is unknown. What is the wise response?**
- A. Donate quickly — questions waste time
- B. Donate through well-known, registered charities instead — fake fundraisers steal from victims twice ✓
- C. Share the page so others can decide
- D. Donate small amounts to every page you see
- *Explanation:* Generosity is an Australian strength, but honesty matters too. Giving through trusted charities ensures help reaches people in need.

**215. In your online sports team chat, players keep mocking a teammate's accent whenever he posts a voice message. What should you do?**
- A. Mute him so you do not have to hear it
- B. Join in so the group stays fun
- C. Speak up — mocking someone's accent is bullying, and everyone on the team deserves respect ✓
- D. Tell him to type instead of speaking
- *Explanation:* A person's accent reflects their story, not their worth. Teams, online or on the field, are built on mutual respect.

**216. You strongly disagree with a new government policy, so you post your criticism online and join a peaceful online campaign to change it. Is this acceptable in Australia?**
- A. No — criticising the government online is forbidden
- B. Only journalists may criticise policies
- C. Only during election campaigns
- D. Yes — peaceful criticism of government is a normal and protected part of democracy ✓
- *Explanation:* Australians are free to criticise governments and campaign for change, peacefully and lawfully. That freedom keeps democracy healthy.

**217. In a parents' chat group, someone spreads an unverified claim that a teacher at your school has been 'stealing money', and parents start abusing the teacher online. What is the right response?**
- A. Do not spread it — unverified claims can destroy a person's reputation, and real concerns belong with the school or police ✓
- B. Share it so parents can protect their children
- C. Abuse is fine if the claim might be true
- D. Ask the teacher to prove innocence in the chat
- *Explanation:* Fairness means people are not condemned by rumour. Genuine concerns are reported to the proper authorities, not tried by a chat group.

**218. A popular online personality urges thousands of followers to flood a small café with abusive messages because the owner made an honest mistake with an order. What should followers do?**
- A. Join in — the café deserves it
- B. Refuse to take part — mass abuse is harassment, and mistakes should be handled fairly ✓
- C. Send only one abusive message each
- D. Boycott the café forever without hearing its side
- *Explanation:* No mistake justifies mob harassment. Australian values require fairness: hearing both sides and treating people with respect.

**219. Your friend asks you to write glowing five-star reviews for his restaurant, even though you have never eaten there. What does honesty require?**
- A. Write them — friends help friends
- B. Write one review and stop there
- C. Refuse — reviews should be truthful accounts from real customers ✓
- D. Write the reviews under a false name so it is safe
- *Explanation:* Honesty applies in business and online life. Fake reviews mislead the public and are unfair to honest businesses.

**220. On a dating app, a person quickly declares strong feelings and starts pressuring their match to lend money and keep the relationship secret. What does a respectful relationship require instead?**
- A. Speed — real love moves fast
- B. Generosity — lend the money to prove feelings
- C. Secrecy — couples owe others no explanation
- D. Respect, honesty and freedom from pressure — no one should be pushed into giving money or anything else ✓
- *Explanation:* Healthy relationships are built on equality and respect. Pressure, secrecy and demands for money are warning signs of manipulation and scams.

**221. An anonymous account sends a 13-year-old football fan repeated abusive messages calling her worthless and telling her to harm herself. What is this?**
- A. Serious cyberbullying and abuse — it should be reported to the platform and police, and she should be supported ✓
- B. Part of being online — she should toughen up
- C. Her fault for having an account
- D. A joke that has gone too far, nothing more
- *Explanation:* Abuse that targets a child is never acceptable. Serious online abuse can be reported to the eSafety Commissioner and the police.

**222. A community page shares photographs of strangers' children playing in a park, with captions making fun of their clothes and bodies. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — parks are public places
- B. It violates the children's dignity and safety — children should never be mocked or exposed online for entertainment ✓
- C. It is fine if the parents never find out
- D. The children should dress better
- *Explanation:* Children deserve protection and respect, including online. Using images of other people's children to mock them is cruel and wrong.

**223. After a breakup, a man threatens to post private intimate photos of his former partner online unless she gets back together with him. What is this?**
- A. A fair bargaining tool
- B. Understandable — he is hurt
- C. Image-based abuse and coercion — sharing or threatening to share intimate images without consent is a crime ✓
- D. Legal if he took the photos
- *Explanation:* Threatening to share intimate images is a serious offence in Australia. No one owns another person's body or images.

**224. After a severe storm, residents use a local online group to organise help: checking on elderly neighbours, sharing tools and clearing driveways. Which value does this show?**
- A. Showing off for likes
- B. Interfering with emergency services
- C. Only helping people who post thanks
- D. Helping others and mateship — community members looking out for one another ✓
- *Explanation:* Technology can strengthen community. Using it to organise genuine help for neighbours is mateship in the digital age.

**225. A worker posts screenshots of a client's private details online because the client was rude to her. Why is this wrong?**
- A. People's private information must be respected and protected — being rude does not cancel a person's right to privacy ✓
- B. It is only wrong if the client complains
- C. Private details may be shared after one week
- D. She should have removed only the client's name
- *Explanation:* Respect for privacy is part of mutual respect. Sharing someone's personal information as revenge is a serious breach of trust.

**226. A junior football coach picks his own son as team captain every week, although several other children train harder and play better. What is unfair about this?**
- A. Nothing — coaches may choose family first
- B. Team honours should be earned on effort and merit, not family connections ✓
- C. Captains should be the tallest children
- D. The son should be captain of a different team
- *Explanation:* A fair go in sport means selection and honours are based on effort and ability, not on who you are related to.

**227. At a children's soccer match, parents on the sideline scream abuse at the 14-year-old referee after a decision they dislike. What should happen?**
- A. The referee should quit if he cannot take it
- B. The parents should scream louder until he changes it
- C. The abuse must stop — officials, including young ones, deserve respect, and decisions are accepted peacefully ✓
- D. The teams should refuse to play until the parents win
- *Explanation:* Sport cannot work without officials. Abusing them, especially children, is against the values of respect and fair play.

**228. A soccer player dives, pretending to be fouled, wins a penalty and later boasts to friends about fooling the referee. What does this say about his attitude?**
- A. He is clever — winning is all that matters
- B. It is the referee's job to catch everyone
- C. All professional players do it, so it is fine
- D. It is cheating — deliberately deceiving officials is dishonest and unfair to opponents ✓
- *Explanation:* Honesty and fair play are at the heart of Australian sport. Winning by deception is not a real win.

**229. After a hard-fought match, the winning captain shakes hands with every opponent and thanks the umpires and volunteers. Which value is he showing?**
- A. Respect — for opponents, officials and everyone who makes the game possible ✓
- B. Weakness — winners should celebrate alone
- C. Superstition
- D. Only good manners for the cameras
- *Explanation:* Respect for opponents and gratitude to volunteers and officials are hallmarks of Australian sporting culture.

**230. A skilled teenage girl asks to join her local Australian rules football club, and an official says, 'We do not take girls here.' What is the right view?**
- A. The official is right — football is a boys' game
- B. She has the same right to play as any boy — excluding her because she is a girl is discrimination ✓
- C. She may join only as a water runner
- D. She should start her own club with her own money
- *Explanation:* Girls and women play every sport in Australia. No one may be excluded from sport because of their sex.

**231. During a cricket match, a spectator repeatedly shouts racist abuse at a player from a migrant background. What should those around him do?**
- A. Move seats and ignore him
- B. Laugh along to avoid trouble
- C. Not accept it — racist abuse should be reported to officials so it is dealt with ✓
- D. Shout abuse back at the spectator's team
- *Explanation:* Racism has no place in Australian sport or society. Standing against it, and reporting it, protects everyone's right to belong.

**232. A man with an intellectual disability wants to join a weekly community walking group. Some members worry he will 'slow everyone down'. What fits Australian values?**
- A. Suggest he walk alone at another time
- B. Accept him only if a carer walks for him
- C. Let him join but walk far behind
- D. Welcome him — community activities are for everyone, and inclusion matters more than speed ✓
- *Explanation:* Community life is for everybody. People with disability have the same right to join in and belong.

**233. The treasurer of a local sports club has been taking money from club fundraising for his own use, hoping no one notices. What is this?**
- A. Stealing — dishonesty that betrays the whole club and must be reported ✓
- B. A fair payment for his volunteering
- C. Acceptable if he pays it back one day
- D. Only wrong if the club needed the money
- *Explanation:* People who handle community money hold a position of trust. Taking it is theft, however it is dressed up.

**234. On the train home after a close game, fans of the two rival teams argue heatedly. What must never happen?**
- A. Fans of the losing team feel disappointed
- B. The argument turning into violence — sporting rivalry must always stay peaceful ✓
- C. Fans discussing the referee's decisions
- D. Supporters singing their team songs loudly
- *Explanation:* Passionate support is part of Australian sport, but peacefulness is a core value. Rivalry must never become violence.

**235. A coach pressures a 16-year-old player with a knee injury to keep playing an important match, against the doctor's advice. What should come first?**
- A. The coach's reputation
- B. Winning the match
- C. The young player's health and safety ✓
- D. The crowd's entertainment
- *Explanation:* Young people's health always comes before winning. No game is worth a serious, lasting injury.

**236. On a fishing trip, a man catches several undersized fish and keeps them, saying 'nobody checks out here anyway'. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — the ocean has plenty of fish
- B. He should share them so everyone breaks the rules equally
- C. Fishing rules only apply near patrol boats
- D. Fishing rules protect our shared environment — obeying the law matters even when no one is watching ✓
- *Explanation:* Commitment to the rule of law means following rules because they are right and protect shared resources, not only when we might be caught.

**237. While surfing, you accidentally cut in front of another surfer and cause a collision in which he is knocked off his board. What should you do?**
- A. Check he is all right and apologise — take responsibility for your mistake ✓
- B. Paddle away quickly before he sees you
- C. Blame him for being in your way
- D. Laugh it off — collisions are part of surfing
- *Explanation:* Taking responsibility for our mistakes and making sure others are safe is part of treating people with respect.

**238. At the gym, a group of regulars laughs at an overweight man who is exercising for the first time in years. What is the right attitude?**
- A. He should exercise at home instead
- B. He deserves encouragement and respect like every other member ✓
- C. New members should expect some teasing
- D. He can earn respect when he loses weight
- *Explanation:* Everyone has the right to improve their health without mockery. Respect is owed to people as they are, not only when they change.

**239. Every year, hundreds of people give up their Sunday mornings to hand out water and direct runners at a charity fun run. What does this show?**
- A. They could not find anything better to do
- B. Volunteers expect payment in free merchandise
- C. Australians freely give their time to help others and support their community ✓
- D. Only students should volunteer at events
- *Explanation:* Volunteering is woven into Australian life. Big community events happen because ordinary people give their time for others.

**240. A wealthy parent offers the junior team's selector a cash 'donation' if his daughter is chosen for the representative side. What is this?**
- A. Generous support for the club
- B. Smart parenting
- C. Fair, if other parents may also pay
- D. An attempted bribe — representative places must be earned on merit ✓
- *Explanation:* Paying for selection corrupts sport. Every child deserves a fair go based on their own effort and ability.

**241. A team photo is being organised. One player suggests leaving out the teammate who has a disability, 'so the photo looks better'. What is the right response?**
- A. Include him like everyone else — he is a full member of the team ✓
- B. Put him at the very back so he is barely seen
- C. Take two photos, one without him
- D. Ask his family whether he minds being excluded
- *Explanation:* Inclusion is not negotiable. Every team member belongs in the photo and in the team, equally.

**242. During a junior cricket match, a bowler repeatedly insults the young batter about his family and his body, calling it 'sledging'. Where is the line in sport?**
- A. Anything said on the field is acceptable
- B. Personal abuse is never acceptable — competition must stay respectful, especially with children ✓
- C. Insults are fine if they help you win
- D. Only the captain may insult opponents
- *Explanation:* Gamesmanship never excuses abuse. Respect for opponents applies at every level of sport, above all in children's sport.

**243. At a skate park, a group of older teenagers orders younger children to leave, saying the park 'belongs' to them. What is the truth?**
- A. Seniority gives them ownership
- B. Younger children should pay them for access
- C. Public parks are for everyone — no group owns a shared public space ✓
- D. The younger children should find a private park
- *Explanation:* Public spaces belong to the whole community. Sharing them fairly is part of living together with respect.

**244. A woman refereeing a men's football match is abused by players who say women 'cannot understand the game'. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — players may abuse referees
- B. She should referee only women's games
- C. The league should replace her with a man
- D. It is sexist abuse — women can hold any role in sport, and officials must be respected ✓
- *Explanation:* Women are players, coaches and officials at every level of Australian sport. Abuse based on sex is discrimination and disrespect.

**245. On a bushwalk, you find a hiker who has fallen and broken his leg, far from the car park. What would mateship look like?**
- A. Stay with him, give first aid if you can, and call 000 for help ✓
- B. Walk on — someone else will find him
- C. Tell him to try to stand and follow you
- D. Take a photo for your social media first
- *Explanation:* Mateship means helping people in trouble, especially strangers. In remote areas, staying with an injured person and calling for help can save a life.

**246. You catch a beautiful fish that you realise is a protected species. Keeping it would make a great photo and meal. What must you do?**
- A. Keep it — one fish makes no difference
- B. Release it carefully — protected species must be returned, and the rules protect our wildlife ✓
- C. Keep it hidden in your esky
- D. Sell it to another fisher
- *Explanation:* Caring for Australia's environment includes obeying the rules that protect vulnerable species, even at personal cost.

**247. The president of a bowls club refuses a membership application from a family, explaining, 'They are not from around here — they only arrived from overseas last year.' What is this?**
- A. Protecting the club's traditions
- B. Acceptable — locals come first
- C. Discrimination — community clubs should be open to everyone on fair terms ✓
- D. Fair if the family can join next year
- *Explanation:* New Australians are part of the community from the day they arrive. Excluding people because of their origin denies them a fair go.

**248. After his team loses a grand final, a player waits in the car park to confront and threaten the referee. What do Australian values say?**
- A. Referees should expect this after big games
- B. It is fine if he does not actually touch him
- C. Losing a final justifies anger at officials
- D. Threats and violence are never acceptable — results and decisions must be accepted peacefully ✓
- *Explanation:* However disappointing a result, officials must be safe. Peacefulness and respect do not stop at the final whistle.

**249. A local swimming club runs free swimming lessons for refugee children who have never learned to swim. Which values does this best demonstrate?**
- A. Compassion and a fair go — helping newcomers gain a life-saving skill and belong ✓
- B. A waste of pool time
- C. Lessons should be for local children first
- D. Refugee children should learn in their own countries
- *Explanation:* Swimming is a vital safety skill in Australia. Sharing it freely with newcomers shows compassion and helps everyone belong.

**250. A tennis player believes the umpire has made several wrong calls against her. What is the respectful way to deal with it?**
- A. Throw her racquet and abuse the umpire
- B. Use the proper challenge or complaint process, and accept the final decision ✓
- C. Refuse to shake hands after the match
- D. Get the crowd to boo the umpire
- *Explanation:* Disagreements in sport are handled through rules and processes, not abuse. Accepting decisions peacefully is part of fair play.

**251. A heatwave hits your town. Your 85-year-old neighbour lives alone in a house with no air-conditioning. What is a simple act of looking out for others?**
- A. Nothing — heat is part of Australian life
- B. Tell her to move to a smaller house
- C. Check on her, and offer water, a cool place or help if she needs it ✓
- D. Complain that her garden is dying
- *Explanation:* Extreme heat is dangerous for older people. Checking on vulnerable neighbours during heatwaves saves lives and is basic mateship.

**252. You are first on the scene of a serious car crash. A driver is trapped and bleeding. What should you do first?**
- A. Film the scene for social media
- B. Pull the driver out by yourself no matter what
- C. Drive on — ambulances will come eventually
- D. Call 000 and help in whatever safe way you can until emergency services arrive ✓
- *Explanation:* In an emergency, calling 000 quickly and helping safely can save lives. Australians stop and help people in danger.

**253. At a crowded beach, a small child is standing alone, crying, clearly lost. The lifesavers' tower is nearby. What should you do?**
- A. Stay with the child and take them to the lifesavers or staff so their family can be found ✓
- B. Take the child to your car to wait
- C. Assume the parents will turn up and walk away
- D. Ask the child to search the beach alone
- *Explanation:* Protecting children is everyone's business. Staying with a lost child and getting help from lifesavers keeps the child safe.

**254. A fire starts in a flat in your building. The occupant runs door to door knocking loudly to warn everyone before going outside. What does this show?**
- A. Panic
- B. Looking out for others — warning neighbours in an emergency can save their lives ✓
- C. Interfering — fires are for firefighters only
- D. He should have saved his furniture first
- *Explanation:* In emergencies, Australians look out for one another. A quick warning gives neighbours precious time to escape.

**255. A man on your train suddenly becomes pale, clutches his chest and collapses into his seat. Passengers nearby just stare. What should you do?**
- A. Move to another carriage to avoid the scene
- B. Wait to see if he recovers on his own
- C. Ask if he is all right, alert the driver or staff, and call 000 if he needs urgent help ✓
- D. Take his photo in case the news wants it
- *Explanation:* A person in medical trouble needs fast help, not an audience. Calling 000 and assisting within your ability is the right response.

**256. After days of rain, floodwater covers the road ahead. Another driver waves you through, saying, 'It does not look deep.' What is the safe and responsible choice?**
- A. Follow the other driver closely
- B. Drive through slowly with your windows down
- C. Let your passengers get out and walk through first
- D. Never drive through floodwater — turn around and follow official advice ✓
- *Explanation:* Floodwater is one of Australia's biggest killers in natural disasters. The rule is simple: if it is flooded, forget it.

**257. After a storm, you find an injured man lying near fallen powerlines that are sparking on the ground. What should you do?**
- A. Keep well away from the powerlines, call 000, and warn others to stay back ✓
- B. Run in and drag him away by his feet
- C. Move the powerlines with a stick
- D. Pour water on the sparking wires
- *Explanation:* Helping safely means not becoming a second victim. Fallen powerlines can kill — emergency services have the training and equipment.

**258. A tropical cyclone is approaching. Your neighbour laughs at the warnings and refuses to secure loose items or prepare at all. What is the community-minded response?**
- A. Hope his roof lands in someone else's yard
- B. Encourage him to follow the emergency services' advice — loose items in a cyclone endanger the whole street ✓
- C. Secure his things yourself without asking, at night
- D. Report him for having an untidy yard
- *Explanation:* Preparing for disasters protects not only your own household but your neighbours too. Official warnings should be taken seriously.

**259. On a bushwalk, your friend is bitten by a snake. Mobile reception works. What is the right first response?**
- A. Suck the venom out of the bite
- B. Walk her quickly back to the car to 'keep the blood moving'
- C. Keep her still, apply a firm bandage, and call 000 ✓
- D. Cut the bite open to let the venom out
- *Explanation:* Correct first aid and a fast call to 000 save lives after snakebite. Staying calm and still slows the venom's spread.

**260. A long blackout hits your suburb at night. You remember your neighbour relies on an electric machine to help her breathe while sleeping. What should you do?**
- A. Nothing — the power company knows her situation
- B. Knock once, and if there is no answer, go to bed
- C. Post about it in the street group chat and wait
- D. Check on her promptly, and help her contact the power company or emergency services if she needs power urgently ✓
- *Explanation:* Emergencies hit vulnerable people hardest. Knowing your neighbours and checking on them can be a matter of life and death.

**261. You walk past a house and see thick smoke pouring from a window while children inside shout for help. What must you do immediately?**
- A. Call 000 straight away ✓
- B. Open the front door to let the smoke out
- C. Go inside and search the house yourself
- D. Knock and wait to see if an adult answers
- *Explanation:* Fire services must be called immediately in an emergency. Entering a burning building is extremely dangerous — firefighters have the training and equipment.

**262. A small boat capsizes on a lake near where you are picnicking, and the people in the water are too far out to reach the shore. What is the best help you can give from land?**
- A. Swim out alone, however far it is
- B. Call 000, keep watching them and direct rescuers, and throw anything that floats if they come close enough ✓
- C. Wave and shout encouragement only
- D. Wait until they reach the shore by themselves
- *Explanation:* Water rescues are dangerous even for strong swimmers. Calling 000 fast and guiding rescuers is the safest and most effective help.

**263. On a hiking trail, you hear someone calling for help from a gully, but you cannot see them and the ground looks unstable. What is the responsible action?**
- A. Climb down alone whatever the risk
- B. Call out that help is their own problem
- C. Call 000 with your location, keep calling back to the person, and let rescue services reach them safely ✓
- D. Mark the spot and finish your hike first
- *Explanation:* Rescue services are trained and equipped for difficult terrain. Giving an accurate location and staying in contact saves both the lost person and would-be rescuers.

**264. During a shopping centre evacuation after a fire alarm, what is the right way to behave on the stairs?**
- A. Push past slower people to get out first
- B. Run and use your elbows if needed
- C. Let only your own family go first
- D. Stay calm and move steadily, helping people who are slower or struggling ✓
- *Explanation:* Even under pressure, Australians are expected to stay calm and consider others. Panic and selfishness cost lives in evacuations.

**265. In a car park, you reverse into another car, denting its door. Nobody saw it happen and there are no cameras. What does honesty require?**
- A. Leave your contact details on the car or report it — you are responsible for the damage you caused ✓
- B. Drive away — no witness, no problem
- C. Swap number plates with another car
- D. Blame the other driver for parking badly if you are caught
- *Explanation:* Taking responsibility for our actions, even when no one saw, is basic honesty and respect for others.

**266. A swimmer is caught in a rip current beyond the flags and is signalling for help. You are a weak swimmer. What should you do?**
- A. Swim out and try to pull her in yourself
- B. Alert the lifesavers immediately and call 000 — do not attempt a rescue beyond your ability ✓
- C. Shout instructions at her from the beach
- D. Wait — rips usually bring people back to shore
- *Explanation:* Many would-be rescuers drown in Australia every year. Raising the alarm fast is the help that saves lives.

**267. Your street has no power for three days after a big storm. You have a generator. What is an act of mateship?**
- A. Rent your power to neighbours at high prices
- B. Run your generator only for your own house and say nothing
- C. Offer neighbours help — for example charging phones or keeping essential medicine cold ✓
- D. Use the blackout to catch up on sleep
- *Explanation:* Mateship means sharing what you have when others are doing it tough. Small help in a crisis means a lot.

**268. At a highway rest stop, you meet a tourist who is badly sunburnt, confused and unsteady after a day in extreme heat. What should you do?**
- A. Tell him summer in Australia is just like this
- B. Give him directions and drive on
- C. Offer him a beer to cheer him up
- D. Help him into shade, give him water, and call for medical help if his condition is serious ✓
- *Explanation:* Heat illness can be life-threatening. Helping a stranger in medical trouble is a simple act of compassion.

**269. You smell strong chemicals and see liquid leaking from drums behind a factory fence, near where children are playing. What should you do?**
- A. Move the children away and call 000 — chemical leaks are a job for emergency services ✓
- B. Open a drum to check what is inside
- C. Let the children keep playing — factories are always safe
- D. Take photos and post them without telling anyone
- *Explanation:* Hazards like chemical spills must be reported immediately and left to trained responders. Protecting children comes first.

**270. At 2 am you see someone smashing a car window in your street and pulling out a bag. What is the right response?**
- A. Run out and tackle him
- B. Call the police and note what you saw — do not put yourself in danger ✓
- C. Shine a torch so he can see what he is doing
- D. Wait until morning to mention it
- *Explanation:* Witnesses help by reporting crime quickly and safely. Confronting offenders yourself can end in violence.

**271. An elderly man falls on an escalator in a shopping centre and cannot get up, while the steps keep moving. What should bystanders do?**
- A. Step over him to keep the escalator moving
- B. Wait for centre management to notice eventually
- C. Press the emergency stop if you can, help him if it is safe, and call for assistance or 000 ✓
- D. Pull him up by one arm as fast as possible
- *Explanation:* Quick, sensible help protects people in everyday emergencies. Everyone deserves assistance when they are hurt or in danger.

**272. A storm drops a large tree across your street, blocking it completely. An ambulance may need this road. What is the community-minded action?**
- A. Leave it — the council will find it eventually
- B. Drag it yourself with your car, whatever the risk
- C. Set it on fire to clear it faster
- D. Report it to the council or SES, and warn other road users if you can do so safely ✓
- *Explanation:* Keeping emergency routes clear is part of looking after your community. The SES and council have the equipment to clear trees safely.

**273. On a day of extreme bushfire danger, thick smoke is covering your town. Your neighbour has asthma and no car, and the fire service advises people in her area to leave. What could you do?**
- A. Offer her a lift or help her follow the fire agency's advice to get to safety ✓
- B. Tell her smoke is not really dangerous
- C. Leave early yourself without mentioning it
- D. Suggest she wait and see how bad it gets
- *Explanation:* In bushfire country, neighbours can save lives by helping vulnerable people act on warnings early.

**274. A toddler has swallowed some household cleaning liquid and is coughing and crying. What should the adults do immediately?**
- A. Make her drink milk and wait an hour
- B. Call 000 or the Poisons Information Centre at once for urgent advice ✓
- C. Make her vomit to get it out
- D. Post in a parents' group and wait for replies
- *Explanation:* Poisoning is an emergency. Expert advice from 000 or the Poisons Information Centre must be sought immediately, not from social media.

**275. After heavy rain, part of your neighbour's roof is leaking badly and more rain is coming tonight. You have a ladder and a spare tarpaulin. What is mateship here?**
- A. Charge him a high price for the tarpaulin
- B. Wait until morning to see the damage
- C. Offer to help him cover the roof before the rain returns ✓
- D. Tell him he should have maintained his roof better
- *Explanation:* Mateship is practical: helping a neighbour protect his home when trouble strikes, without expecting anything in return.

**276. At a government service office, a long queue is moving slowly. A man starts shouting abuse at the staff member serving him. What is wrong with his behaviour?**
- A. Nothing — staff are paid to accept abuse
- B. He should shout louder so the queue moves
- C. Everyone should join in to speed things up
- D. Staff deserve courtesy like everyone else — frustration never justifies abuse ✓
- *Explanation:* Mutual respect applies to the people who serve the public. Problems can be raised through complaints processes, not abuse.

**277. You receive a summons to attend court as a juror. You are busy and think of simply not showing up. What should you know?**
- A. Jury service is a civic responsibility — you should attend, or follow the official process if you truly cannot ✓
- B. Summonses are optional invitations
- C. Only people with legal training serve on juries
- D. Ignoring it has no consequences and affects no one
- *Explanation:* Juries are ordinary citizens doing an essential democratic duty. Fair trials depend on people taking this responsibility seriously.

**278. It is census time and your household must complete the national census form. A friend says, 'Just make up the answers — nobody checks.' What is the right approach?**
- A. Make up answers to finish faster
- B. Answer honestly — the census guides services and funding for the whole community ✓
- C. Leave the form blank in protest
- D. Copy your neighbour's answers
- *Explanation:* Honesty with government processes matters. Accurate census information helps plan hospitals, schools and services for everyone.

**279. While carrying a couch through a public park, you knock over and break a park bench. Nobody from the council saw it. What should you do?**
- A. Leave quickly before anyone notices
- B. Blame the council for putting a bench there
- C. Report the damage to the council — public property belongs to everyone and should be respected ✓
- D. Hide the broken pieces in the bushes
- *Explanation:* Public facilities are shared by the whole community. Owning up to damage you caused is honesty and respect for the public good.

**280. Your family goes camping in a national park. When you leave your campsite, what does caring for the environment require?**
- A. Leave food scraps for the animals
- B. Bury all your rubbish deep in the bush
- C. Leave the fire smouldering — rain will put it out
- D. Take all your rubbish with you and leave the site as you found it ✓
- *Explanation:* Our national parks belong to everyone, including future generations. Leaving no trace protects the land and its wildlife.

**281. Driving at night, you find an injured baby kangaroo (a joey) alive beside its dead mother on the road. What is the right thing to do?**
- A. Call a wildlife rescue service for help — native animals are protected and need specialist care ✓
- B. Take it home as a pet for your children
- C. Leave it — wildlife is not our concern
- D. Feed it cow's milk and release it the next day
- *Explanation:* Injured native animals are protected by law and need trained carers. Calling wildlife rescue gives the joey its best chance.

**282. In the local library, a man watches videos on his phone at full volume despite signs asking for quiet. Other readers are disturbed. What is the issue?**
- A. Libraries should ban phones completely
- B. Shared public spaces have rules so everyone can use them — he should use headphones or go outside ✓
- C. Other readers should shout at him
- D. Quiet rules apply only to children
- *Explanation:* Respect in shared spaces means following reasonable rules so that everyone can enjoy them.

**283. On a crowded train, a passenger puts muddy feet on the seat opposite and leaves food wrappers behind when he gets off. What does this behaviour lack?**
- A. Nothing — cleaners are paid to deal with it
- B. Only money for a taxi
- C. Respect for other passengers and for shared public property ✓
- D. It is only a problem on new trains
- *Explanation:* Public transport belongs to everyone. Leaving it clean and treating it with care is basic courtesy and respect.

**284. In a severe drought, a farmer with a reliable water supply offers his neighbours water for their dying livestock, asking nothing in return. Which value is he living out?**
- A. Expecting a favour later
- B. Following water restrictions only
- C. Showing off his good fortune
- D. Compassion and helping others — sharing what he has with people in need ✓
- *Explanation:* In hard times, Australians share what they have. Generosity to struggling neighbours is the public good in action.

**285. Your council holds a public meeting about building a new community centre. Residents with very different views attend. What makes such a meeting work in a democracy?**
- A. Everyone gets a chance to speak and be heard, and the final decision follows a fair process ✓
- B. The loudest group should win
- C. Only property owners may speak
- D. Opponents should be banned from attending
- *Explanation:* Parliamentary democracy works at every level through fair hearing and lawful decisions. Listening to opposing views is part of the process.

**286. After a council decision to raise local rates, angry residents post a council worker's personal phone number online and urge people to call and abuse her. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Nothing — public servants give up their privacy
- B. It is harassment — decisions can be challenged through proper channels, but individuals must never be targeted ✓
- C. It is fine if the decision was unpopular
- D. She should change her number and accept it
- *Explanation:* Democracy allows strong disagreement with decisions. It never allows the harassment of individual workers or their families.

**287. You visit an Aboriginal sacred site. Signs from the Traditional Owners ask visitors not to climb the rock formation or take photographs. What should you do?**
- A. Climb quickly when no one is watching
- B. Photograph it anyway — you paid to enter the park
- C. Respect the request — honouring the wishes of Traditional Owners shows respect for the world's oldest living cultures ✓
- D. Ignore the signs — they are only suggestions
- *Explanation:* Respecting Indigenous culture and the wishes of Traditional Owners is part of Australian values. Some places are sacred and must be treated accordingly.

**288. At the beach, the public bins are full and overflowing after a hot day. Your family has a bag of rubbish from lunch. What is the responsible choice?**
- A. Leave it neatly beside the bin for someone else
- B. Bury it in the sand
- C. Throw it into the dunes
- D. Take your rubbish home with you if the bins are full ✓
- *Explanation:* Caring for our beaches and environment is everyone's job. If you brought it, you can take it home.

**289. Walking your dog near a creek, you see a man unloading old tyres and oil drums from a truck into the waterway. What should you do?**
- A. Note the details and report the illegal dumping to the council or authorities ✓
- B. Help him unload so he finishes faster
- C. Ignore it — the creek is not your property
- D. Dump your own rubbish there next time, since others do
- *Explanation:* Our waterways belong to the whole community. Reporting illegal dumping protects the environment and public health.

**290. At a supermarket, some teenagers mock the cleaner mopping the floor, calling his job 'worthless'. What is wrong with this?**
- A. Cleaners should work only at night
- B. Every honest job deserves respect — a person's worth is not measured by their occupation ✓
- C. He should be grateful for any job
- D. Teenagers cannot be expected to respect adults
- *Explanation:* In Australia there is no class system. All honest work contributes to the community and deserves dignity and respect.

**291. After loading your shopping, the trolley bay is only a few metres away, but it is raining lightly. What is a small act of respect for others?**
- A. Leave the trolley in the empty parking space
- B. Push it toward the road and let it roll
- C. Return the trolley to the bay so it does not block cars or roll into them ✓
- D. Leave it — attendants are paid to collect trolleys in the rain
