- *Explanation:* Mutual respect often shows in tiny everyday choices: leaving shared spaces as others would wish to find them.

**292. Roadworks are slowing traffic. A driver winds down his window and screams abuse at the young traffic controller holding the stop sign. Why is this unacceptable?**
- A. Traffic controllers enjoy the power
- B. Drivers are allowed to vent at roadworks
- C. Only police deserve courtesy on the road
- D. Road workers are people doing a dangerous job to keep everyone safe — they deserve respect and protection ✓
- *Explanation:* Abuse of workers is never acceptable. Traffic controllers stand in dangerous places so that road users and road workers stay safe.

**293. At an Anzac Day dawn service, the crowd falls silent for one minute's silence. A visitor keeps talking loudly on his phone throughout. What does this moment deserve?**
- A. Quiet respect — the silence honours Australians who served and died for the country ✓
- B. Phone calls are more important than ceremonies
- C. Only veterans need to be silent
- D. He should just speak more quietly next year
- *Explanation:* Respect for those who served is part of Australian life. Observing silence at commemorations honours their sacrifice and costs nothing.

**294. At a school crossing, a supervisor in a high-vis vest holds up a stop sign as children cross. A driver, late for work, thinks of driving around her. What must the driver do?**
- A. Drive around slowly — he is late
- B. Stop and wait — children's safety at crossings is the law and a shared responsibility ✓
- C. Sound the horn so children hurry
- D. Crossings are guidelines, not rules
- *Explanation:* Protecting children is a value the whole community shares. Stopping at children's crossings is both the law and basic care.

**295. You are selling your car privately. You know the brakes sometimes feel soft, but mentioning it may lower the price. What does honesty require?**
- A. Say nothing — buyers should check themselves
- B. Mention it only if the buyer asks directly
- C. Tell the buyer about the problem — hiding a safety fault is dishonest and could endanger lives ✓
- D. Sell it quickly to someone far away
- *Explanation:* Honesty in buying and selling protects people. Concealing a known safety problem puts strangers' lives at risk.

**296. A landlord refuses to return a tenant's bond at the end of a lease, inventing damage that was already there when the tenant moved in. What should happen?**
- A. Tenants should expect to lose their bond
- B. The tenant should damage the property in revenge
- C. The landlord may keep it — it is his house
- D. The bond must be handled as the law requires, with disputes decided fairly through the tenancy process ✓
- *Explanation:* Commitment to the rule of law protects both tenants and landlords. Money held in trust must be dealt with honestly and lawfully.

**297. The council approves a development on your street that you strongly opposed. You believed the process was fair and you had your say. What is the democratic response?**
- A. Accept the outcome, or use the legal appeal processes — decisions are not overturned by anger or threats ✓
- B. Block the builders with your car
- C. Damage the site at night
- D. Refuse to pay council rates forever
- *Explanation:* Democracy means having your say and accepting lawful outcomes, even disappointing ones. Legal avenues exist for genuine grievances.

**298. On a bus, a passenger begins racially abusing the driver, who migrated to Australia years ago. The driver stays calm and keeps driving. What could fellow passengers rightly do?**
- A. Join in so the trip is entertaining
- B. Support the driver — for example by speaking up calmly or reporting it — because racist abuse is never acceptable ✓
- C. Move to the back and film it
- D. Tell the driver to drive faster so it ends
- *Explanation:* Everyone deserves to do their job free of racial abuse. Standing with the victim is a practical expression of respect and inclusion.

**299. Members of a community garden share the vegetables grown in common beds. One member quietly takes most of the harvest for his own family each week. Why is this against community values?**
- A. Gardens should have no sharing rules
- B. First come, first served applies to everything
- C. Shared resources must be shared fairly — taking more than your share breaks trust with the whole group ✓
- D. He should take it at night instead
- *Explanation:* Community projects run on trust and fairness. A fair go means shared benefits are divided fairly among all who contribute.

**300. A country town welcomes several refugee families. Local people invite them to English classes, junior sport, school events and a community welcome barbecue. What does this best show?**
- A. The town expects the families to forget their cultures at once
- B. Charity that will end in a week
- C. Only the council's responsibility
- D. Australian values in action — everyone is welcomed, respected and given a fair go to belong ✓
- *Explanation:* Belonging grows when communities open their doors. Freedom, respect, fairness and compassion sum up the Australian welcome.
